Gently he placed the unconscious girl upon the bed. Walking toward a hanging lamp, directly in his line of vision, he

lit it. The bright glow revealed several articles of furniture that one would never dream of seeing in so remote a place.

The cabin contained a fine, wide-open fireplace, and although it had only one room, as it was high and spacious, well furnished and cozy, John thanked the stars for his good luck in stumbling upon it. The hard pine floor held two large, serviceable rugs, while several small ones of various shapes were scattered about. 

A book-case well stocked with light and serious literature, was conspicuous in one corner of the room. In a cupboard he found a large collection of canned goods, and other provisions. The assortment was just the kind that a man of means acquainted with the region, would choose. 

Kettles, dishes, and glasses were also in evidence. Several cords of hardwood in suitable lengths for the large fireplace were piled outside of the back door. As quickly as possible, he started a fire and put on a kettle of water. 

He next approached the sleeping girl, and shook her gently.

Failing to awaken her, he chafed her hands, forced some water down her throat, and applied cold water compresses to her forehead. His efforts were unavailing. Her eyes were closed. Her breathing was slow; her heartbeats faint. Her aspect was like that of one in a coma. John decided that if he were to fix the injured ankle, it would be better to do so before she regained consciousness. The fact that he was a mining engineer, and trained to some day manage the Desmond properties, did not mean that he had learned nothing at all about surgery during the year or so that, contrary to his best interest, he thought he would like to become a great surgeon.

Loosening, and then removing her outer garments, he again examined the injured limb. Under the cabin lamp, it showed up even more bruised, discolored and swollen. John also saw on the knee, right over the knee-cap, on that skin of velvety softness, a tattooed emblem representing a dove alighting on several branches of olive leaves. It mystified, baffled and intrigued him; but the love of the medico for his profession still slumbering somewhere down deep in his heart, set him to work; and for the next half hour he busied

himself in putting the limb in improvised, though efficient splints.

The stillness in the cabin was broken only by the patient's regular breathing, and the very slight noise of his own movements. At last, with a sigh of relief, he straightened up and looked at his handiwork. It was with a glow of pardonable pride that he contemplated it. Everything considered, it was a very creditable performance for one not of the profession. "Poor kid," said John, "wonder who you really are. It will be some time before you will walk again." After a pause, "I wonder if you are married?" Then, to himself, "Steady, John, steady! Hang it all-what is the matter with you? There you go again. Better stop it-John, stop it, and get yourself something to eat." "That's it," he chuckled. "It's my appetite-no fooling, I am really hungry. Besides," continued he, musingly, "it takes two to make a bargain—and this little lady may not be free."

Then again laughing at himself for his persisting romantic thoughts, he turned away. Walking to the larder, he selected and cooked a meal from some of the provisions reposing there. After topping it off with two cups of steaming hot coffee, he sat down by the open fireplace, lit his pipe, and puffed away with a contentment he would. never have again believed possible, since the agony of a certain eventful night in New York, three months previous.

Outside, the wind blew and moaned as it swept the sturdy cabin walls and peppered them with the swirling desert sands.

CHAPTER II. 

WITH a start, John Desmond awoke to find it was daylight.

The fire had gone out and the air was a trifle chilly. Instinctively, he turned toward the bed and was relieved to see that the injured girl still slept. He felt famished and figured that without much trouble he could do justice to a nice, hot breakfast. Undoubtedly, his patient would also be inclined to partake of some nourishment, on awakening. 

John was no chef, but he could do a little plain cooking; so without any misgivings that he would fail to please, he started things going. When right in the midst of arranging a tempting tray, a wild scream vibrated the window panes.

It was a replica of the one on the night before. It emanated from his patient. Hastening to her side, he saw at a glance that she was delirious. Small as she was, he had to exert his strength in order to keep her down. Finally his attempts to soothe her, at first unavailing, seemed to calm her somewhat, for her violent outbursts became weaker and weaker. Ceasing suddenly, she broke out into conversation-first low and soft; then as it progressed, it grew louder and faster, until the intensity of her mounting agitation put him on his mettle to restrain her from undoing all the work he had done on the injured limb. 

In her delirium she kept re-enacting previous events. 

Part of her speech was in English with traces of a foreign accent, but most of her outbursts were in a tongue he did not understand. "Unhand me, sir! How dare you touch me?" she cried. "I do not know you do not care for you! Who are you? 

Bring me back-no, leave me; do it now. Who is your sister? Why should she take an interest in me?" 

Then, after a slight pause, "Mother, oh, mother dear! 

Come to me! I need you; I don't know what to do, where to go! I'm afraid-yes, afraid!" 16

After another interval had passed, one that was leading John to believe that she was again slumbering, he heard her tremblingly ask in a voice laden with dread, "Why did we come here? I see no habitation. What is wrong? Can't you fix it? Where are you taking me? No! No! I do not wish to go any further with you. I prefer to stay here alone -go-leave me, and-go-go-now."

Toward the last the articulation of her words became almost unintelligible, finally ceasing altogether. It then, and not until then, that John Desmond, with a sigh of relief and concern for the suffering little soul now finally quieted in slumber, stopped to wipe the perspiration from his brow. "You poor kid," said he to himself, "you surely must have been through some very trying experiences. I'm afraid we are going to be here for quite a while, unless someone shows up. I wonder where we are. The neighborhood is new to me," continued he musingly. "Yes, I've been out here all of three months, and I have never gone much beyond the confines of my own property. I hope somebody lives close by. My patient needs a woman to stay with her. I wonder who owns this cabin? Funny a man should go in so strong for this sort of thing and then fail to make use of it. From the dust and cobwebs, I imagine the owner has not been here for years. I was certainly lucky to stumble upon it! And that reminds me while my patient is still asleep, I'll amble back to the man I trussed up last night. If he speaks English I'll make him talk whether he wants to or not-I'll make him," he mused grimly, "if for no other reason than to square things for the shot he took at me last night. Yes, I certainly am curious to know who the young lady is." 

Turning to the sleeping girl, he said, "Well, I think it will be safe to leave you for about an hour or so. But it might be just as well to let you know why I am absent, in case you should revive while I'm gone." Picking up a pencil and paper from the old writing cabinet next to the bed, he wrote as follows:

"Dear Lady: 

Please do not be alarmed. You are in good hands. 

Will return soon. 

Sincerely, your friend,

JOHN DESMOND." 

"There!" said John, "I think that will fill the bill."

Placing it on the writing cabinet in a position where the reclining girl could not fail to see it on awakening, John shouldered his rifle and tiptoed through the door. Outside, the sky looked dreary. The chill in the air, and the quickly darkening horizon heralded another approaching storm, so John hastened his steps in the direction from which he had come on the night before.

After twenty minutes of travel, he was pleased to find, that either through instinct or luck he had arrived at the very spot he sought. He particularly examined the place behind the sagebrush, where, on the previous night, he had dropped flat at the shot of the unknown marksman. He also located the places where he had fought the stranger, and had found the helpless girl. He looked for his assailant of the night before. He was not in sight. The man had disappeared. Could it be that someone had passed that way and found him? John walked back and forth through the dense growths of sage and grease-wood for some trace of him. On the point of retracing his steps in disgust, he saw something shining from a little scrub growth a few paces away. He strode over and picked it up. It was the metal buckle on the end of the belt which he had used to bind the man with. Looking more closely, he saw footsteps making off in an easterly direction. The footprints showed the man was alone.

There is much to be learned by making this man talk, thought John. It will not do to let him get away under such circumstances. Besides, the man might perish if left alone.

It might be that he had a horse. If he did, a short walk should show where he had mounted and ridden away. Then, and not until then, did it dawn upon John Desmond that while the injured girl was dressed in the fashion that a lady of quality would be within her home, the man was

togged out as an aviator. They must have come in an aeroplane, and if so, where was it? And why were they so differently attired? 

The condition of the girl's shoes, as John remembered them, showed no signs of desert travel. In that case, they must have come in a plane, and if so, it must be close by. 

Grasping his gun, he started to walk briskly in the direction of the unknown man's receding footprints. In his advance he kept his eyes wide open for the first possible view of the man and plane he expected to find. Suddenly the terrific, deep-throated drone of a powerful aeroplane motor split the stillness of the desert air. Almost instantly, a magnificent plane glided from behind a sand ridge a short distance ahead and took off. When it reached an altitude of several hundred feet, it turned and came straight toward him at lightning speed.

Believing the occupant intended to run him down, John threw himself prone upon the ground. The plane swooped down and cleared him by several yards, zoomed, turned again and circled him. It contained a solitary man. He was helmeted and goggled. The stranger peered at him intently. John shouldered his rifle and fired twice as the plane swept by. He felt if the man was out for some more trouble he could have it. If the aviator understood, he certainly had no intention of accepting the challenge, for he shook his clenched fist in John's direction, pointed the nose of the plane toward the fast approaching storm, and at an amazing angle winged away. John watched it with mixed emotions, as it rapidly grew smaller and smaller in the darkening sky. "Hang it all," exclaimed he, vehemently. "Of all the rotten luck; if I had only come a little earlier, I might have stopped him." Then, admiringly he mused, "He certainly had some crate. She was a pip of a climber; well—wellwe may meet again, and if we do, I will make it a point to get better acquainted."

John retraced his steps to the cabin. Upon entering, he saw the girl had awakened; and though she was lying pensive and quiet, the blank stare in her eyes denoted that she was still out of her mind. Hastily warming the breakfast

which he had failed to give her a few hours before, he again brought it to her, and was pleased to see she made no objection to being fed. 

John Desmond was in a dilemma. Here was an unknown crippled maiden upon his hands, hardly out of her teens and also out of her mind. "Poor kid," said John, "you recently must have had a very tough break, because, as bad as your injury is, it is not the real cause of your delirium. Of that I feel sure."

John knew he must get some help and get it soon. He also knew his men would worry about him, and that they would comb the surrounding country when he failed to appear. 

And to cap the climax, he was not sure of his location.

He was in unfamiliar territory. On the day before, while in a petulant mood, he had given his mount the rein, unmindful of her speed and direction. Spitfire was a highspirited mare that had the reputation of making a rider know that he was in fast company whenever she got a free rein. "Darn her," thought John, "if she had not stumbled and thrown me, then fool-like bolted for home, I would not be in such a pickle now." And yet, had Spitfire behaved, he would now be back at the ranch instead of with this injured girl.

They would never have met; and he had to finally admit to himself that between the two, he preferred the present situation caused by the mare's misbehavior. John 

Desmond knew that most prairie-bred mustangs had the faculty of finding their own way home, even at times when their masters were at fault. "Spitfire probably got home after midnight," mused he, "and the boys will follow her back-trail if the storm has not obliterated her tracks. However, I think we will live for awhile, judging from the food stored here." 

For seven days, John administered to the delirious girl, and daily hoped that someone would find them. On the morning of the eighth day, her fever suddenly abated; and

John knew that the crisis had passed. For about the hundredth time he walked over to gaze upon her, and was

finally overjoyed to see her now looking at him with wideopen eyes-eyes that no longer stared into space. "Good morning, miss. How are you feeling?" he greeted.

For a full minute she looked up at him. A slight wrinkling of her daintily arched brows over a pair of wonderful deep blue eyes denoted she was striving to collect her thoughts. 

Suddenly a glorious smile lit her cameo-like features-a smile that made his heart stand still. "My good friend," said she, "I want to thank you for your kindness, but I cannot find the proper words to convey my gratitude." "Please do not try, because I understand," answered 

John. "Now if you will tell me your name, and where you live, I will see about getting you safely home." For a moment a troubled look clouded her expressive eyes, then, with a tremulous little smile at the corners of her mouth, she reached out and clasped John's browned muscular fingers with her own five tapering, slender ones. "Please don't think me unappreciative," pleaded she, "if I ask you not to insist in wanting to know more about me. I must have a little time to think, to decide something before I can tell you." Then, noting the disappointment on his face, added simply, "But you may call me Clarita; it is my first name." "Clarita," said John, now giving her still lingering fingers a comforting little squeeze, "I shall respect your wishes, and until you see fit to enlighten me, I shall be content. Now do try and go to sleep. You must not worry. 

You must lie quiet." 

Upon her submissive smile, he in turn smiled down upon her and said, "Now that you have regained consciousness, I believe it will be quite safe to leave you for a few hours." "Where are you going? You are not leaving me?" There was a decided tremor in her voice. "I don't like to go, to leave you even for a few hours, but I must try to make arrangements to move you to my ranch. You will be much more comfortable there." "Please don't leave me," she begged, "I am afraid!" "Afraid—afraid of what, my dear?"

"Of that terrible man who abducted me." It was quite apparent that the girl was unquestionably frightened. "So, you were kidnapped, were you? What was his object, ransom?" "No, I don't think so. I really don't know what to think." "Well, well, never mind. Don't worry over it, and don't be afraid, because the man to whom you refer departed in an aeroplane six days ago, and I have not seen him since." 

For a moment she looked startled, and then asked in a voice that was almost inaudible, "Have-have I been here all that time?" "Yes," was John's answer. "Alone with you?" 

John nodded, in the affirmative. 

Slowly, the eyelids with those long, curling lashes, dropped over her wondrous eyes. The twitching fingers of her little, snow-white hands, nervously clasping and unclasping above the bed coverlet, were the only signs that betrayed the agitation of her thoughts. Just as John was beginning to think it would be proper to leave her for the time being, he heard her ask softly, "Did I talk-say anything?" "Nothing of much consequence," was John's rejoinder, "most of your conversation was in a language I do not understand." "There now," said he, patting her arm, comfortingly, "you must keep quiet, otherwise you will have a relapse, and that would mean your removal from here would be delayed. Clarita, I want you to be tranquil in mind. I want you to be comfortable and happy, and let me also assure you that if I had a sister, I could not have tended, cherished and respected her more than I did you, during your illness. Now, do go to sleep; and tomorrow, if you wish, I will answer your further questions." 

Once again, she smilingly offered her hand. "You are kind, you are wonderfully kind, and I-I am glad it-it was you," she answered softly. 

Her words, so sincere, without guile and uttered in just the manner one would expect from a trusting child, made

John's heart beat faster at the confidence that her words implied. Before he could frame a suitable reply, a sidewise glance revealed that those wonderful orbs of hers were again closed-closed now in restful slumber. The smile upon her lips, the gentle movement of the blanket over her breast caused by her deep, measured breathing, enthralled John Desmond to the extent that only the knowledge of her helplessness kept back the almost irresistible temptation to kiss her. Instead, with a song in his heart, he turned away, and within the minute, a little cotton-tail romping about outside, saw John Desmond with head up and shoulders back, tip-toe across the threshold of the back door, and mechanically sit upon the woodpile there. 

For almost an hour the little desert habitant saw him sitting there, complacently smoking his pipe, with a smile on his lips and a far-away look in his eyes.

CHAPTER III. 

At the Desmond ranch, all was in a turmoil. John Desmond was missing. For five days his cowboys, in separate squads, had searched the countryside for miles around. The storm, to a certain extent, had hampered them and even destroyed the return tracks of Spitfire, who, riderless, was found neighing at her stable door, before daybreak, on the morning after her master's departure. 

Norbert Miller, the ranch foreman, was at his wit's end on account of Mr. Desmond's disappearance. To make matters worse a bevy of light-hearted, inquisitive young women, accompanied by what Norbert considered two silly men, descended like a thunderbolt on the Desmond ranch, on the second day of John's disappearance. 

As guests of John Desmond, the new arrivals not only expected to be entertained, but they drove Norbert almost insane with questions about Mr. Desmond's disappearance, the wonderful weather, the ranch routine, the name of this, the reason for doing that, what did he consider the best horse for one to ride, and so forth and so on. "Tom," exclaimed Norbert to a smiling youngster on the fourth morning after the visitors' arrival, "I think I'll delegate you to entertain the new arrivals and chaperon the girls. That goes also for their male escorts, for I believe them to be as useless as their half-naked women. I sure am not a-going to be pestered any longer with their questions. 

Beginning tomorrow, do take them out for a ride and show them the sights." "Where shall I find them?" asked Tom. "Find what?" "The sights to which you refer." "Oh, hell, you know what I mean, Tom! Take them north, south, east and west. Show them the desert with its sand, sage and sunshine. If they are what I think they are, they will enthuse over it. If they do not," said he with 24

a grin, "apologize by telling them the desert was just like that when you came here. Tom, my boy," said Norbert, for he liked the clean, clear-eyed youngster, "go anywheredo anything you want, just so long as I don't see them.

I've stood that bunch for three days running, and I'm all in!"

Tom was young, impressionable, and of a jovial disposition--the son of a well-to-do family back East. He and 

John Desmond had been room-mates at college. The first thing John did on arriving at the ranch, was to wire his chum as follows: 

"I HAVE THE BLUES. WON'T YOU COME OUT 

AND STAY AWHILE?" 

And Tom's return wire was: 

"WILL START TOMORROW. DON'T GIVE UP THE SHIP." 

Tom Norton's arrival several days later not only cheered up his friend, but Tom also soon endeared himself to all about him, by his sunny disposition, his spontaneous good humor, and his obsession to wear spick-and-span cowboy paraphernalia.

The morning after the New York crowd's arrival, on two different occasions, Tom Norton had caught the admiring, mischievous glances of one of the invaders, a pert little miss, no higher than a prairie mustang. And so, the commission as guide, now offered him by the ranch foreman-manager, was what Tom considered nothing more or less than "a gift from the gods."

Next morning at breakfast, the visitors were told of the arrangement for their entertainment. It met with the hearty approval of all those concerned, all except Miss June Ostend. She preferred going with the ranch searching parties. To this, Norbert offered an objection. "Sorry, Miss," said he to her, "I shall have to disappoint you. You see, Miss, the searching parties are made up from my best and toughest riders; they travel fast and go far. A woman would slow up and hamper them."

By the smiles that flitted across the faces of her friends, who knew she was no centaur, June concluded that her horsemanship was being sarcastically alluded to by this

hireling of John's; so, she then and there decided to not only assert herself, but to also put this pompous person in his proper place. "My man," she said haughtily, "do you realize whom you are addressing in such an indifferent, and I might add, so insolent a manner?"

Norbert, who had turned away to issue orders to the assembled riders as to the territory they were to comb that day in search of their employer, at her words, wheeled about, and looking at her intently, with the slightest trace of amazement in his voice, answered: "No, Miss." "Well, then, in order that you may know your place, allow me to inform you that I may some day be Mrs. 

John Desmond." 

At her words, Baron Reginald Van Guard was seen to give an involuntary start. His face flushed and then paled. 

Only the gleaming, unfaltering, malevolent look in his eyes showed that her speech was not in accord with the hope he himself entertained in her direction.

Upon hearing that she might marry his beloved employer, Norbert received the information with mixed emotions. Respectfully removing his hat, he said in a low tone of voice: "Miss, you have my best wishes."

June Ostend was a spoiled child. She had never wanted for anything. In fact, being an only child, through the parental indulgence accorded her, she had acquired a distorted outlook upon life that was all out-of-tune with prevailing conditions. Somewhere back in her well-shaped head, June had more or less of a hazy idea that the funny little sphere we call the "World" had been especially created for her as a place in which to live and frolic. 

Believing that Norbert's respectful behavior and subdued tone implied that he would stand for a further dressing-down, June could not resist the temptation to show off before her friends and the assembled throng. So, turning and again addressing the ranch manager, she said: "And you will be a very wise man, Mr. Miller, if, in the

future, you will cultivate your disposition to the extent of according me the proper attention."

Norbert, like most men of the Golden West, had the knack of instinctively appraising people at first sight. His first impression of June Ostend had been such, that he now wondered if his employer would not be making a serious mistake in marrying June Ostend. However, he quickly decided that that was something which did not concern him. 

He had been hired to supervise and run the Desmond ranch by the late John Desmond, Senior; and had likewise been so retained by his son, the present owner. It was with a just pride that he looked back over a number of years, and saw it was mostly due to his untiring efforts and good judgment, that the Desmond ranch had grown and prospered from an ordinary holding to the largest and most prosperous in the county.

Norbert also prided himself somewhat on the knowledge that no woman, other than his beloved mother, had ever dictated to him since he could remember, and while being a bachelor did not necessarily mean he despised the gentler sex, still, no daughter of Eve was going to countermand his orders, or make him appear ridiculous.

On the other hand, he reasoned from June Ostend's statement that she was John's sweetheart-the girl his employer might some day marry; and for that reason, if for no other, Norbert swallowed his indignation, nodded, and asked: "Is that all, Miss?" "Yes," answered June, "I believe that will suffice for the present." 

Again Norbert bowed his head, and said: "Thank you, Miss."

A titter ran amongst the bystanders, for while the ranch manager had been courteous, the consensus of opinion was that, in the veiled clash, June had pulled a boner. 

Turning to one of the cowboys, Midget Jim Kennedy, Norbert said: "Midget, no women are to accompany the search parties. 

See that it is passed along." "You bet!" replied Midget, striding away.

June Ostend turned a crimson red at the seemingly new affront, but the seething volcano within her that was on the point of eruption was held in check by the intercession of one of her own party, the tactful Mrs. Grant, who, encircling June's waist with her arm, led her away, with the words: "Come along, dear. He has his master to find, as well as his other duties to perform."

Mrs. Grant's voice, though soft and low, nevertheless reached Norbert's ears, whose hearing had become acute through his life in the open. 

Catching her eye, he inclined his head in acknowledgement of her kindly office; then strode to the door and disappeared from view, just as Tom Norton made his appearance to announce that the horses were saddled and waiting for the sight-seeing party.

Tom was dressed in his best cow-togs, consisting of brand new grey leather chaps, a silvery-grey shirt, open at the throat, and a sombrero to match, with a coiled rawhide quirt around it. A light, tan-colored cartridge belt with holster and a six gun encircled his waist. His general appearance was enhanced by the neatly knotted brown silk handkerchief around his neck. All in all, Tom Norton appeared more and more the lovable youngster that he was; and in the eyes of little Miss Douglas he was the reincarnation of the 'Prince Charming' of her dreams.

With a gasp of unconcealed delight, the little minx poked 

Mrs. Grant with her elbow and whispered: "Agnes, isn't he simply gorgeous?"

Before that estimable lady could reply, the little tease walked right up to Tom, made a faultless Victorian curtsy and said, while smiling up into his eyes: "Good Sir Knight, if thou art truly real, fain would I crave thy protection in my wandering through the castle grounds."

At this, everybody laughed. At first, Tom flushed and coughed in his embarrassment; but as a knight of the younger sociai set back home he was somewhat accustomed to this sort of thing and rapidly recovered his poise. Removing and holding his sombrero in his extended right

hand, he made a sweeping bow from the hips. "Fair lady," said he, "I pray thee to accept the castle and grounds as a slight token of my humble homage. Were 

I not overbold, fain would I ask of thee a boon." "Speak up, Sir Knight, have thou no fear. I pledge thee my royal word. What wouldst thou have?" retorted Lillian majestically. "Wouldst thou suffer me to implant-a token of my devotion, upon thy artistic rosy-red lips?"

The delivery of this audacious speech was smilingly and good naturedly received by Miss Douglass, 'mid the roars of the surrounding company. "Dear me!" said Lillian, "I did not know that you were so witty and--and-so horrid!"

Graciously extending her hand, she mischievously interjected: "Sir Knight, your persiflage is dangerous. Let's be friends."

Tom, with a grin of boyish pleasure, which was not lost upon her, clasped and shook it in a real he-man fashion. "Shucks!" exclaimed Miss Ostend to Baron Reggie Van 

Guard, "how can she tolerate him? He is only a cow-hand." "Perhaps she is only amusing herself," replied the baron, also unaware of Tom's true status at the ranch. "I hope so," retorted June. "I should feel disgraced were it otherwise. Let's go outside," said she taking his arm "Amusing one's self in the game of hearts, I believe, seems to be your strong suit also, June," answered the 

Baron falling in step beside her "What do you mean, Reggie?" "You heard what I said-your declaration to Mr. Miller." "Well, what about it?" "Is it fair to me?" "Pray, tell me, when were you ever considered in that respect? You have known right along, I would never marry you. Have I not told you so repeatedly?" "Oh, ho! I see," said Van Guard. "Then your coming out here was for the purpose of discarding the old and on

with the new, without even lowering the curtain for the intermission." "Reggie, I declare at times you are simply impossibleyou certainly bore me." "At least, I don't love promiscuously," replied he somewhat irritably. "Were you bored one night at the " "Don't!" said she, gripping his arm and turning pale under her rouge. "Have you no consideration?" 

Changing his tactics, the Baron continued: "June, what do you think of this place?" "Really, Reggie, you must be more explicit." "Well, then, what in the world could have induced John, with an income of millions, to bury himself for three whole months, in a God-forsaken country like this?" "I really don't know. There are some things I don't yet understand about John." "June, were you really engaged to him back there, or merely anticipating?" "Yes, we were to announce it as soon as his mother returned from abroad." "Will he approve of your telling it now?" "I should worry," answered she flippantly. "Do you really love him, June?" "Why shouldn't I?" "I was wondering how you could, after what you've been to me!" "Oh, pshaw! You take women too seriously, Reggie!" "Am I to understand, then, that your protestations of affection, your pleasure in my society almost daily back there, your caresses and kisses meant nothing at all-were no more to you than your stock-in-trade?" "Oh, well, if you really want to know, I got a kick out of it; didn't you?" "Are you that kind of a woman, June?" "Yes, if it pleases you to think so!" flared she, removing her arm and stepping away from him. "Then I see how you can so unconcernedly reconcile your--your actions." "For goodness sake, Reggie, don't be a cad-don't pretend to be so unsophisticated. Do you, for a moment, be-

1 lieve that a woman must be in love, in order to dispense a few favors now and then?" "Apparently not, from your attitude, June. Perhaps it is your American custom and quite O. K. from that point of view, but it would never be from mine. I believe that a woman may flirt, kiss, may even slip and still be an excellent woman, still make a wonderful mother; but only if in erring, she errs through a weakness brought about by a great love; otherwise, she is not only vile and deceitful, but unclean---morally, mentally and spiritually." 

Receiving no response he continued: "In which of these two should I catalogue you, June? 

I dare you to tell me!" he challenged irritably, as she remained silent.

With the fevered flush of a rising passion, June Ostend had listened angrily to the Baron's reproachful tirade. Suddenly giving vent to her pent-up anger, she rushed forward as if she would annihilate him. In a low, suppressed voice that boiled with rage, she flung defiantly at him: "Take your choice, Reggie-damn you, take your choice!"

The explosion would have fazed most men, but it failed to even ruffle the Baron. Making a courteous bow, he answered sneeringly: "Madame, I thank you. You have chosen for me!"

He turned his back upon her, and walked leisurely away, but with a gleam in his eye that did not escape her. "Now, I wonder what he meant by that?" pondered 

June, as she uneasily watched his disappearing form. “I, perhaps, handled him too crudely. Oh, well, it's his word against mine, and I dare say I shall see him again, and when I do, we we shall see what we shall see!"

CHAPTER IV. 

NORBERT MILLER, the ranch manager, about to enter the bunk-house on his weekly tour of inspection, was accosted with the words: "Good morning, Mr. Miller. Any news of Mr. Desmond?"

Turning around to view his questioner, on recognizing 

Agnes Grant, Norbert smilingly acknowledged her presence. "Good morning, Miss. No, none as yet." "Perhaps he has gone back to New York?" suggested 

Agnes. "If you know John, you must realize he is impulsive. He left New York quite suddenly, without telling any one." "That is news to me, Miss, but it does not alter matters. 

I don't see how he would do such a thing in this case. You see, his horse came back, riderless. No, Miss, as much as I would like to believe he went to New York, I am worried-very much worried." "Do you believe him a victim of foul play?" "No," replied Norbert, thoughtfully and slowly, "I don't see how that could be." Then looking down at her smilingly, he added, "Mr. Desmond is such a fine fellow I know he has no enemy-at least, not out here!" "Will you let me know as soon as you get any news?" inquired Agnes Grant, looking archly at him. "To be sure I will," replied Norbert, with a pleased look and heightened color, as he returned the gentle pressure of her outstretched hand. "I will do so gladly, if you are handy." 

With an enchanting smile and nod, Agnes Grant left him.

Less than an hour afterwards, she burst into June Ostend's presence, exclaiming: "Oh, there you are, June, dear! Did you hear the good news?" 32

"No, what is it, dear?" "John has been found! Found by an old man that just rode in. Mr. Miller said he was a desert rat. He brought a letter." "Really, that is good news," said June. "Where is John?" "About twenty miles from here." "What happened to him?” "I really don't know; that is all Mr. Miller told me. I did not question him. He was in an awful hurry." "Then I think I shall see the foreman." "May I go along with you, dear?" "If you wish to."

Norbert was in the midst of a dozen riders. Some were saddling their horses, while others were carrying bedding and blankets, which they piled upon a four-wheeled haywagon (an improvised ambulance) drawn by two mettlesome jet-black colts that were being held in hand by Tom Norton.

Standing beside him, looking as if her eyes were about to pop out of her head, stood Lillian Douglas. "Oh, June," she fluttered, on noting the approaching girls, "Mr. Desmond has been found. He is over twenty miles away in a little house, all alone with a beautiful lady who has been injured. They have been there all the time, and he took care of her. Oh, I think it's so wonderfully romantic--don't you, June? See," said Lillian, pointing to the hay-wagon, "the royal coach has been requisitioned to rescue the princess in distress, and Tommy-I-I mean 

Mr. Norton here, is going to drive and--and he said I could go along if I wished. Isn't it simply thrilling?" "It certainly is," was the rather icy response, "but I'm afraid your going is-is-rather de trop. Under the circumstances, I think I shall take your place. In fact, I have decided to do so." "But June, dear, Tom invited me first." Then, as a bright idea occurred to her, "Why can't we both go?” "I'm really sorry," spoke up Tom Norton, looking directly at Miss Ostend, "there is not room for both of you." "There," said June, turning to Lillian. "You will understand dear, that leaves me no alternative."

Still believing Tom to be merely an employee, June continued, in a voice intended to be impressive: "My man, when do you start?” "Right away, Miss Ostend." "But I am not properly dressed. Wait for me. I shall not be gone long." "Sorry, but my orders are to go as soon as I am ready."

Then, noting Miss Ostend's annoyance, Tom added: "Better see Mr. Miller-what he says goes with me." "Mr. Miller," said June, walking up to where Norbert was talking to Midget, "I understand your employer has been found, and that a relief party is going for him." "Yes, Miss," was the reply. "It is my desire to accompany the party. I requested the driver to tarry until I changed. He seems to think he should go right along. Kindly instruct him to wait." "Sorry, Miss, but I don't dare lose any time, even to oblige you. There is a lady with Mr. Desmond who needs medical attention. He wrote ordering me to hasten. The place is over twenty miles from here and the colts will have all they can do." "Then why not send a motor car? There are plenty around here.' 

"The roads are bad. A car would never get through, Miss." 

"Have you the letter with you?" "No, Miss, I left it in my room.' "Will you let me read it?" "To be sure I will, just as soon as the boys get started." 

Believing he was deceiving her, that he had the letter with him and his denial was only a subterfuge to stop her from going, June looked daggers at him. With the words, "Oh, you-you," she turned about and half walked, half ran toward the house to lock herself in her room and give vent to a flow of words that are taboo in polite society. 

It was with a delightful little yip of pleasure that Lillian extended her hand to Tom Norton when he smilingly said to her: "Miss Ostend is not going. Give me your hand, and 

I'll pull you up beside me."

The emergency ambulance was accompanied by five outriders. One of them led Spitfire, saddled and bridled, for his master to ride on the return trip. The country through which they sped was green at this time of the year; the air was balmy with just the right snap and tang; and the little tease, perched high up beside Tom Norton, was congenial and merry.

The time sped in their exchange of aims, hopes and fears, until with a start, they both realized that just one short mile more lay between them and their journey's end. 

Tom thereupon pulled himself together, grasped the reins more firmly, and with a great flourish, drove the sleek, sweating colts up beside the cabin door. 

Before Tom could interfere, Lillian leaped from her perch, to alight, like a beam of sunshine, gracefully upon the ground. She romped into the cabin, just as John Desmond opened the door. “Hello, John, old dear," she sang out merrily, on espying him. "We had you listed with the slain. Goodness, how brown and happy you look! I sincerely hope your patient approves of your personal appearance and beaming countenance. It was never like this back in ole Noo Yawk!" "Do be still," was John's laughing rejoinder, as he grasped her by the shoulders and shook her. "You will ruin the house that Jack built with your prattle! Gee, but I am glad to see you, Lill! How are your folks? Are they with you? But never mind, dear-that will keep; first, let me introduce you to another very nice girl, one whom I know you will love just as- 

"Well, finish it, John," said Lillian, with a twinkle in her eyes, as he stopped in confusion. "Never mind, pest," answered he. "Come along in." So saying, he led her inside. Approaching the couch upon which Clarita was reclining, he said: "Clarita, let me introduce to you a dear little friend of mine, whom I have known since childhood-Miss Lillian 

Douglas. I know you will like Lillian. She talks without thinking. You will find her very entertaining." 

At the humorous introduction, the eyes of the two smiling girls met. For a moment, they seemed to keenly appraise

and weigh each other; then impulsively, with one accord, they embraced. "I know I shall like you," said Clarita, with a smile that even Lillian could see was unquestionably alluring. "If you don't," retorted Lillian, "it won't be my fault, because I think I like you already as much as- -as much as,-oh, well, if you can't guess, dear, some day I will tell you, if the person," looking sideways at John, "does not beat me to it." "Clarita," said John, blushing, "you must not take Lillian too seriously; she was born that way. Poor childwe all feel and are so sorry for her!" "Run along, big boy," ordered Lillian. "Come back for us in a little while, and perhaps a little fairy godmother will have your patient ready to meet your crowd at the ranch." "By Jove! How silly of me," said John. "So Miss Ostend is at the ranch. Who else?" "Well, there is Agnes Grant. You have met Agnes, haven't you? She is chaperoning us." "Yes, I know her. A very charming and sensible young woman. Who else?" "Well, you'll find Eddie Pickett there and the Baron, too." "The Baron-Baron who?" "Why, Baron Van Guard! You seemed to like him quite well before you left New York. June and he have been inseparable since you vamosed so suddenly. Our crowd has often wondered what it was all about." Then nudging up close to him, and giving his coat sleeve a little tweak, she asked: "Honest to goodness, John, I'll never repeat it. Tell me, old dear, was it a whim, brainstorm, or inspiration? "Never mind, little one," replied John, "perhaps it was all three rolled into one." "Clarita," pouted Lillian, as she turned to the amused girl, who, smilingly, was watching the by-play, "John was never like this. At home, we used to call him the sphinx.

But look at him now. Even his feet do not seem to touch the ground. I'll bet he could now talk me to death. I

don't know how you were able to be with him for eight days and live. You surely deserve a medal. Ho! Page," continued the imp, with a grandiose tone of voice and gesture, "inform the Chancellor, we bestow upon both our worthy subjects, our highest decoration." 

A soft, silvery laugh rang through the cabin as Clarita reached out, drew down the face of the little madcap, and kissing her full upon the lips, said: "We're going to be good friends, you and I-I am sure we are." 

Lillian, looking up at John Desmond, did not fail to note the admiring glances he was unconsciously showering upon 

Clarita. Thereupon, this sparkling bit of humanity could not resist the temptation to take one more thrust at him. 

Turning so that the reclining girl could not see her pantomime, she crossed her hands over her breast, and tantalizingly asked, with the accentuated closing of one of her baby-blue eyes, "John, dear, I find her simply irresistible. 

What sayest thou?"

Before any retort could be made to this impish one-hundred-and-ten pounds of tantalizing humanity, Tom Norton entered to ask if he should bring anything inside. "No," said Lillian, "I believe not."

John, with scarlet cheeks, then hastily made his excuses and left the room accompanied by Tom. For almost an hour, the two girls left them chafing for their appearance.

Finally the door opened and little Miss Mischief beckoned them. 

Entering the cabin, they soon reappeared. Clarita was seated upon a chair which the young men carried between them. Setting it down beside the wagon, they gently placed her upon the billowy arrangement of mattresses, pillows and blankets. Then, with just one long, last, lingering look at the cabin that for eight days had so comfortably sheltered them, John Desmond, with a sigh, turned away and gave the order for the return trip.

CHAPTER V. 

Ar the Desmond ranch, things were on the qui vive. A large bonfire built on a slight elevation near the corrals was kept burning from the first fall of dusk. In the crossbeamed, high-ceilinged living room, a long table had been placed and serviced for forty people.

On a knoll a mile away, a rider placed by Norbert was to speed back with the news upon sighting the return of the rescuing party.

All were intensely keyed up with pleasant anticipations, when a very distinguished, middle-aged gentleman entered, and looking over the gathering, asked: "Any news of my nephew, John Desmond?" 

Richard Wallace was a capable man, kindly and still vigorous for all of his three score years. His one hobby and obsession was his love and regard for his nephew, John 

Desmond, whom he actually worshipped. Uncle Richard had never married, and the consensus of opinion was that he never would, though many had sought him. 

Spying Norbert just entering, he pounced upon him and exclaimed, while shaking hands: "Well, here I am again! How are you? Any news of 

John?" Then looking at the festive table, "He has been found? Is he all right? Where is he?"

Upon being informed that he had been located, was on his way to the ranch and momentarily expected, Uncle 

Richard heaved a sigh of relief. "Dash it all, Norbert," said he, in an undertone, brushing his hand across his brow, "that boy will yet be the death of me. Almost overnight, he leaves New York at a time I thought he was going to take hold of the business. 

Of course," he added thoughtfully, "I realize he is only twenty-six-but even at twenty-six, well-well, never mind," said he, terminating the remark, with the statement "your telegram to New York was cabled to me, 38

at Cairo, Egypt; so I took passage and left at the earliest possible moment on the Trans-Continental Air Line. I was worried-greatly worried. See you have some young people here. Friends of John's, I suppose."

Upon being informed that his surmise was correct, Norbert introduced John's uncle to those of the guests who had never met him, of whom June Ostend was one. She immediately started to train her guns upon him, with the object of making herself exceptionally agreeable. Drawing him to one side, she coyly remarked: "So you are John's Uncle Richard! I am so glad to know you! John has spoken so often, and so nicely about you. It has made me very anxious to meet my my prospective uncle," said she, squeezing his arm significantly. 

At Uncle Richard's questioning look, she continued: "You see, John and I are engaged; but it is not to be made public until his mother returns from abroad." "You don't say!" was Uncle Richard's reply. "Wellwell-so you are the little lady for whom my nephew has fallen! You have my best wishes," said he, taking hold of both of her rather well-shaped, soft, white hands. "You will find John a nice boy--a very nice boy-there is none better."

June's answer was drowned by the sudden uproar that now rang through the house, as several score men and women broke into cheers. John Desmond, looking fit, browned and extremely happy, was seen to emerge from the noisy throng surrounding him. Right behind him, Tom and Midget carried a chair, upon which sat a wondrous, smiling young girl of aristocratic mien and deportment. 

Her shapely little hands, the pose of her head, laden with a wonderful array of auburn tresses, the intriguing lips, pearl-like teeth, and deep blue eyes with long curling lashes, coupled with her winsome smile, radiated a magnetism that held the observers spellbound.

John's eyes beamed with pleasure at the glances of admiration which his radiant companion received; and a shout of delight issued from him, upon seeing his uncle among those now coming directly toward him. John Des-

mond acknowledged June's greeting by a bare nod of his head as she also approached, clinging to his uncle's arm. "John," said he, grasping him by the shoulders, "thank 

God that you are back safe and sound. What happened to you?" "Oh, it was quite an adventure, but nothing serious.

Will tell you all about it later. First of all, I want you to meet her-my patient-the girl I found in the desert."

Putting his arm through his uncle's, John led him to where the boys had set down the chair containing Clarita. "Uncle Richard," said he, "let me introduce you to 

Clarita a wonderfully brave little lady. She met with an accident in the desert and it was my good luck to find her." "Clarita," said John, looking at her with his heart in his eyes, "I want you to meet my Uncle, Richard Wallace. Uncle Richard has been like a father to me since I lost my own dear one; and I hope you will learn to like and love him as I do. Uncle is a confirmed bachelor; but please do not hold that against him," stated he jokingly. 

At this sally, Uncle Richard laughingly took the girl's extended hand, and giving it a gentle little pat said: "My dear, I hope you will take my nephew's advice," and then bending down closer, added, "It would please me very much to have a charming niece like you." 

A bewitching smile was his reward. "What are you two conniving?" asked John, smilingly. "Some day we may tell you, John, so be patient," was 

Uncle Richard's rejoinder, looking straight at Clarita. 

A radiant smile was her reply. "Gangway, please!" was the peremptory order of a girlish voice, and Lillian Douglas was seen forcing her way through the circle surrounding Clarita and John. "If the guest of honor and I are expected to answer the dinner bell at this glad reunion, we must have time to remove some of the sand and alkali we picked up in our ride through God's country. And, oh, Mr. Norton!" she called, accentuating 'Mister' with a grimace, on observing Tom, "won't you and Mr. Midget kindly carry the patient-lady to my room?"

"It will be a pleasure, I assure you," answered Tom. "Very well, then," said the little tease, screwing up her face and cocking her head to one side, as if in deep thought, "as you are so anxious to please, you may wait outside in the corridor, to carry her back. That also applies to you, little one," said she, looking up with a dimpled smile, at Midget's expressionless face, six-foot-three above the ground. "I'd hate to see Mr. Norton attempt it alone."

Midget's only answer was to change the quid to his other cheek-spread his heels, put both thumbs in his cartridge belt, and articulate a throaty "you bet!"

Once in her room, Lillian busied herself in waiting upon Clarita, whose injury made her incapable of moving around. A comradeship had sprung up between the two girls, almost unbelievable on so short an acquaintance. 

They had about completed their toilettes, when a slight knock on the door was immediately followed by the appearance of June Ostend. With a bewitching smile, June approached, and addressing Clarita, inquired: "So, you are the little lady found by John, in the desert?" "Yes, and I am very grateful to him." "Just think of it!" exclaimed June, "you and Mr. Desmond were alone in a little one-room cabin, for eight days and nights, while he nursed you back to health and reason!" 

For answer, Clarita nodded. "How very interesting!" continued June, deliberately.

With flushed cheeks at the cruel insinuation, the outraged girl straightened up in her chair, and in a voice laden with partially suppressed disgust and scorn, inquired: "Am I to infer from your remarks, Miss Ostend, that it is your intention to be offensive?” "Oh, dear, no!" was June's hasty response, holding up her hands in mock horror. "How could you? Really, my dear, you are over-sensitive. I just dropped in to see you, and you get touchy! And still," she added-softly-insinuatingly, "it might be just as well to warn you, before you get in too deep!" 

Clarita, although small and winsome, beautiful, refined

and educated to an exceptionally high degree of maidenly decorum, was, for all her demureness, a woman-a woman with the same passions and instincts to battle, to love, or to hate, as any of her sisters, since time immemorial. Therefore, her reply took June Ostend by surprise, shared even by the perturbed Lillian. It enraged one and delighted the other. "Thanks very much for your warning, Miss Ostend; but you might have spared yourself the anxiety on my account. Mr. Desmond-I mean John-likes me. Of that I am sure. While I may not have dwelt upon it overmuch before, I may yet decide to be Mrs. John Desmond. I believe it could be arranged." "Huh!" sneered June, "you feel pretty sure of yourself." "No--the list is still open, Miss Ostend." "Would you marry a man who broke his solemn promise to marry another? John Desmond and I are engaged!" retorted June, angrily, uneasily. "I don't believe it," replied Clarita crisply, for while her face blanched, she nevertheless could not conceive how any man, with the lovelight she had seen in John Desmond's eyes, could be other than honorable. "No," she insisted, shaking her head with the conviction that her benefactor was all she wanted him to be "No, I do not believe it!" "Oh, well!" replied June, with compressed lips that now were drawn into a straight, bloodless line, "I will have him tell you so!" "Yes, do-but I know you will fail, my dear," said Clarita.

Without replying, June Ostend gave her one look, a look whose venom could have killed. Turning sharply about, with her head in the air, she flounced from the room. "Never mind her, dear," said Lillian, putting her arms about Clarita as the door closed behind their unwelcome visitor. "I don't believe a word she said. June is jealous. 

She gets downright crazy if anyone looks at John, and you really can't blame her. John is one of the finest men in the whole world, and one of the richest, too."

"Would-would you marry him, Lillian?" asked Clarita with a puzzled little look. "Well-now-, you are asking me something to which I had never given much thought. No, I don't think I would -not because we would not be happy, he is such a dear, but because John has known me all my life. As children, we played and romped hand in hand, on our adjoining estates. 

"Do you love him?" "Why, of course I do. We are very fond of each other, but only in a brotherly and sisterly way. Beyond that, I don't believe either of us has ever gone. Having always regarded him as a brother, I don't believe I ever could as a husband. And then," she added blushingly, "there is

Tom-and-and-you," the latter statement being made in order to recover herself from her unintentional admission concerning Tom Norton. "Why me?' asked Clarita. "Because you love John and he loves you," said Lillian, with a positiveness that would not be denied. "What makes you think so?" blushingly inquired her rather willing listener. "Well, I have eyes. So Clarita, dear, so far as I am concerned, the field is yours. Go to it!" 

For answer, two warm, slender arms entwined themselves about Lillian's neck, and-four lips met.

All further conversation was ended between them, by another knock on their door, followed by a voice that 

Lillian at once recognized as that of Agnes Grant, calling: "Yoo-hoo, in there! May I come in?" "Delighted to have you, Agnes dear," was Lillian's rejoinder. "Turn the door knob to the left, and wipe your feet as you enter!" 

Amid squeals of uncontrolled delight, the door opened to bring together three charming, laughing girls-Clarita, Lillian and Agnes Grant. "Why, Lillian!" exclaimed Agnes, "what in the world have you been doing to Clarita? She is actually a dream!" "Really, Mrs. Grant," said Clarita, "you are very kind and lavish in your appraisal. Lillian insisted on my wear-

ing this Worth creation. It arrived here only yesterday from her parents in Paris. Isn't it a perfect dream? It is charming of Lillian, and an imposition for me to accept. 

But, I am very thankful, just the same. You see, my departure was so-so sudden, that I have only what I was wearing." "Would you mind telling us about it, dear?" asked Agnes. 

A fleeting shadow, for a moment, was seen to cloud 

Clarita's eyes; then clasping the hand of each of her friends, she said looking up at them: "Some day I will tell you all, I promise. Please do not feel hurt-I would not wish it for anything in the world." 

For answer, both girls squeezed her hands in silence. "Oh, how silly of me!" exclaimed Agnes. "I was sent here to bring you two, as dinner is ready to be served.” "All right, we are almost ready," replied Clarita. "Say, Agnes," asked Lillian, "did you see Tom and Midget as you came in?" "Yes, they are out in the end of the hall, standing first on one foot and then on the other." “Do you think they have corns, Agnes?" "Lillian, you are a downright torment," laughed Agnes. "When are you going to grow up?" "Oh, well, then, if you don't mind, Agnes dear, will you please tell them that Princess Clarita is ready?" responded she, and then turning she caught Clarita gazing sharply at her. 

It flustered Lillian. Made her feel that Clarita was hurt at the jocular and seeming frivolity of her last remark. "Please forgive me," said Lillian, looking contritely at 

Clarita. "I did not mean to be rude. Tell me you are not angry, dear." "No, of course I am not, Lillian. But what makes you say such things?" "Oh, I don't know, it is just my way-I suppose that both John and Agnes are right. I talk without thinking and will never grow up. Anyway, I somehow cannot picture you anything other than a- a real princess. You know you look just like one-I mean my idea of one-honest

and truly you do, but just the same I'm very, very sorry. 

I did not mean to hurt you." "Why, Lillian dear," said Clarita smilingly, "I love you too much to believe you would want to hurt my feelings. 

It was very dear of you. There is nothing to forgive."

Noting that Lillian still looked abashed, Clarita reached out her hand and drew the tousled little head toward her with the words, "Come, dear, let me see you smile-smile at your Princess."

At this they all again laughed merrily, and the three girls were ripe for another merry verbal session, if Tom 

Norton, standing before the half-open door with Midget right behind him, had not inquired: "How about it, girls? Do we eat tonight?" "We do, Sir Raleigh," retorted Lillian, turning about to make a mock curtsy. "Please walk in. Pick up the chair of state and carry it to the banquet hall."

CHAPTER VI. 

APPLAUSE and cries of welcome greeted their appearance not so much owing to the wonderful dinner about to be served, but because of Clarita's exquisite beauty.

Her wondrous auburn tresses curling almost to the border line of ringlets, were challenged by a pair of lustrous, deep blue eyes, which shone upon those assembled, with a warmth that elated them. Her lips, so rosy red, showed no traces of any artifice or coloring, and her gown. 

It was with a gasp that all the women assembled first expressed their impressions.

On a simple white frock of silk chiffon and black Chantilly lace, having a low-cut bodice and sleeves, were clusters of snow-white pearls artistically arranged. At her girdle reposed just one large, deep red rose. About her neck was a delicately wrought platinum chain, the center of which held a blood-red ruby. Besides the jeweled buckles of her black satin pumps, she wore no other ornaments.

At a sign from John, who, as host, was to preside at the head of the banquet table, they all approached and sat down. Clarita was seated at John's right, with Uncle 

Richard at his left. 

June Ostend, with gloom in her eyes and hate in her heart for the girl whose beauty and grace she grudgingly conceded, found herself placed at Uncle Richard's left, and almost opposite Clarita. June's chagrin was inwardly intensified by the arrangement, when she caught Baron Van 

Guard looking at her with an amused grin.

Several men and women employees of the Desmond ranch now entered with steaming platters. The savory odors of the hot roasts and vegetables impregnated the ranch house as the servants began to serve the hungry guests.

As the meal progressed, the continuous and rising hum of animated conversations and jovial laughter rolled back 46

and forth amongst them. The spontaneous good fellowship of all those assembled had reached the peak upon the arrival of the dessert. 

Try as she would, Mrs. Grant who sat next to June, was unable to draw her into any lengthy or retentive conversation. June answered such questions as she took cognizance of, only in monosyllables. She was sulking, and several were beginning to notice it.

Eddie Pickett, a tow-headed boy who had just received his M.D., tried to give an impromptu lecture on "The 

Origin of Species," but as Eddie was young, bashful inclined to stutter and now also "stewed," wrestling with a subject entirely out of tune with the hilarity of those assembled made his incoherent flight into the realms of oratory just so much more noise. Nobody listened or cared. 

Eddie was sensitive, and in order to appease his feelings, the flask in his hip pocket made several more stealthy excursions to his lips. Beginning to feel much better after these repeated imbibings, Eddie sensed that if he would make himself noticed he should say something interesting. 

Therefore, with a superhuman effort, he rose dizzily into the wobbling atmosphere, propelled by a pair of protesting legs. "Brottish an' shisters," he announced unsteadily. "Let ush all drink to the hen-gayshment of Shorn Deshmen' an' -an' Shoon Os-end!"

As the import of the words flowing from the mouth of the tipsy youth dawned upon him, John Desmond's face flushed. Glancing inquiringly at June, he noted the gleam of triumph that every woman has cuddled since time immemorial when she puts it over a prospective rival. With an unspoken appeal in his eyes, he turned to meet the interrogating ones of Clarita. 

And as he looked, a smile parted the perfect bow of her bloodless lips, as she strove to hide the hurt that gripped her heart. Observing that all eyes were now focused upon her, Clarita raised her half-filled glass of wine to her tremulous lips, and locking at June Ostend, said softly but clearly: "My best wishes!"

"Stop!" Loud and clear rang the command. All eyes, instantly, were now turned in the direction of John Desmond, who, as the speaker, had sprung to his feet. John's face was pale, but the square chin and set jaws showed that the Desmond strain-the strain that had made his ancestors warriors, statesmen, scientists, and captains of industry could not be coerced or submerged by another's capricious will and unforeseen circumstances. "I believe there must be some mistake," said he, looking somewhat angrily at Eddie Pickett, who was now too far gone to mind what anybody said or did. "With your permission and indulgence," said he, looking across and down at June, "I desire to say our engagement was broken by you—three months ago in New York."

With a gasp, all eyes were now turned to June, to see what she would have to say; for, in view of the declaration she had made that morning, to the ranch manager, Norbert Miller, her present position was an extremely delicate one. 

June bit her lips, and with a look of vexation and amazement, which was half sincere, half simulated, in a soft but well-modulated voice, inquired: "I-I-do not quite understand you, John." "I am sorry, June, that this has happened," was John 

Desmond's rejoinder, "but you surely have not forgotten that we parted in the arbor near the fountain, on the night of your masque ball!"

As she still looked puzzled, he continued: "If you will think again, I'm sure you will recollect the occurrence there."

At John's words, delivered in a tone intentionally commonplace, yet holding a veiled implication, June's thoughts flew back to the night and the spot mentioned. And once again she saw herself clasped in Baron Van Guard's embrace, as her lips and cheeks received his passionate kisses.

As the full meaning of John's remark now flashed across her bewildered brain, she dropped her eyes. But it was only for a moment. With a little resentful toss of her head and a shrug of her shoulders, with flushed face and half-veiled eyes, she looked smilingly up at him, with the words:

"Dear me, it was rather silly of me to forget, wasn't it, John?" 

Turning to the assembled guests June continued: "An error has been made. I beg your indulgence."

At this, a relieved sigh escaped her listeners, but it failed to clear the charged atmosphere. Several of them even tried to pick up and resume conversations which, at Eddie's tipsy words, had been so abruptly cut. Try as they would, however, no one was able to renew and continue the festive feeling that had heretofore prevailed, so the party soon after broke up. 

June Ostend, pleading a headache, retired to her chamber. Once in her room she locked the door, sat upon the bed, clasped her hands and tried to control her racing thoughts and outraged feelings. For the moment she hated 

John Desmond as well as Clarita.

Eddie Pickett was put to bed by the servants, unconscious of the conflagration he had so unwittingly started. 

Within an hour, most of the invited guests had departed. 

Others, in groups, were strolling about the grounds or sitting upon the ranch veranda, smoking and conversing in low tones. 

Mrs. Grant and Lillian Douglas, at the first breaking up of the dinner party, had gone over to Clarita. With a smile, and a tap upon the injured girl's shoulder, they had her chair carried to a secluded corner of the adjoining spacious drawing room. It was there that John Desmond and Uncle Richard found them a few minutes later. As they entered, Uncle Richard was heard to say: "I don't blame you, John-you could not do anything else, although it was unfortunate."

In answer, John was seen to squeeze his Uncle's arm, as they both strode over to where the girls were seated. Stopping before Clarita, John bent down over her with the words: "I am sincerely sorry, and hope that you will understand."

Clarita, who with downcast eyes, had nevertheless been noting his approach, at his words now looked up. After a searching look, one as if she would read the soul that shone

through his unwavering gaze, she nodded and with her eyes motioned that he might be seated on the chair beside her. 

This little side play did not escape the ferret-like keenness of Lillian, who at once poked her elbow slyly into Mrs.

Grant's ribs, with a force that made the young and estimable widow wince. With a mischievous little grimace at 

John and Clarita, Lillian Douglas coyly linked arms with 

Uncle Richard and Mrs. Grant, and headed straight for the door. 

Once outside, on the veranda, Uncle Richard looked down at Lillian, and pinching one of her rosy little cheeks, asked: "Baby, are you on my nephew's payroll?" "No," replied Lillian, looking up at him innocentlyand then with a wink that completely closed one of her baby-blue eyes, vouched, "I work for love only." "Meaning ?" Uncle Richard inquired. "That if I'm not their maid of honor before spring comes, I am going to know the reason why!" 

At this sally, both Mrs. Grant and Uncle Richard roared. "You should be in the secret service," said Uncle 

Richard to Lillian, with a wink at Mrs. Grant. "Why?" was Lillian's inquiry. "You are so deucedly observant and clever." "Pshaw! You just wait till I get a-going." "What are you all so merry about?" asked the Baron, who, with Tom Norton, now joined the group. 

Uncle Richard turned to Lillian, with the request: "You tell them, little one." "What can I do for you?" inquired Lillian, addressing the Baron, but with a side glance at Tom Norton. "Tell us what it's all about, so we can laugh, too," pleaded Tom. "Well, then, did you ever hear the story of how the traveling salesman lost his sense of humor?" "No," was the Baron's quick response. "How did he lose it?" "He died," was the laconic rejoinder.

Her reply threw them all into another fit of merriment, heightened on the observance of the Baron's discomfiture. 

While right in the midst of their merriment, a sharp, piercing scream issued from the drawing room behind them, followed by the report of a pistol shot. Instantly, everybody stood still, paralyzed in their tracks, all except Tom Norton. With gun in hand, he was seen sprinting for the scene of the disturbance.

CHAPTER VII. 

SEVERAL days previous to the opening of our story, in the chamber of a royal castle, the abode of King Augustus, a maiden knelt at the feet of a woman of mature years. 

Clasped in each other's arms, the girl was sobbing softly.

They were mother and daughter-Queen and Princess, bidding each other farewell, for ere another sun, the Princess would leave the love and shelter of the parental roof, to live amongst people who spoke not her language. 

The marriage was approved and abetted by Ismar, the inscrutable High Priest of Dovette, whose underhand machinations for bringing it about were not only condemnable, but treasonable. Ismar, through the superstition of centuries, by dire predictions, many of which had in a measure materialized, was respected and feared alike by those who met him socially, and by those that shunned him. "Mother, dear," said the Princess brokenly, "I cannot- 

I simply cannot marry him! I don't love Prince Sigismund; I don't even respect him; he fills me with loathing."

The Queen mother ere replying, with trembling hand and compressed lips, gently stroked the girl's auburn tresses: "Yes, darling, mother understands and wishes it could be otherwise; but, dear, it means so much to your country, to the happiness and prosperity of the people--your father and me. Your refusal to marry Prince Sigismund would throw us into war with King Modoc-a war that both the King and Council concede could only terminate in our overwhelming defeat. Child, I dread to think what the consequences of such a state of affairs would mean to us all! Sigismund's father, King Modoc, is notoriously ruthless to those who cross him. My dear, your sacrifice would mean the happiness of a nation. Besides, as the wife and consort of Sigismund, you will some day be Queen, and— 52

and possibly a mother. Does not the outlook somewhat reconcile you to the situation?"

The girl thereupon raised her head, which had been lying upon her mother's knee, and gazing up into her eyes, with a wan smile, answered: "Yes, mother dear, it should. At any rate, I promise to be brave-as brave as my father's daughter should be. Of course I will marry Sigismund, but I shall never love him -never' Oh, how I wish that I were dead!" "Hush, child, hush! You must not carry on so. It will only unnerve you. Tonight I want you to look your best. 

I want to be proud of you."

Impulsively, they again embraced. The Queen mother kissed her daughter once, twice, thrice-fondly, fervently ---and in releasing her said: "Now go to your apartment, child, and compose yourself." "Yes, mother dear," acquiesced the princess, "I suppose it is all for the best. So have no fear but what I will be worthy of you both, for I am resigned." Then turning her head so that her mother could not see the tears again gathering in her eyes, she said, "If you don't mind, mother dear, I will first take a last stroll through the gardens. I would for awhile be alone with my thoughts."

As the princess walked toward the French window that overlooked the Italian sunken gardens in the castle grounds, Kathryn, her maid-in-waiting and companion, started to follow her. Noting this, the princess, with a slight shake of her head and affectionate wave of her hand, continued her walk, with the words: "No, no, dear, I wish to be alone. Please don't mind."

With a slow step and bowed head, the princess walked along beside wondrous beds of fragrant flowers. Unmindful was she of her surroundings for her heart was sad.

A fawn-colored Russian wolf-hound, that bounded forward to greet her with sharp yelps of delight, she waved aside with a low, yet kindly, word of command. Without stopping she continued her walk and was again lost in meditation.

The faithful obedient dog stood still in his tracks. With a puzzled look in his eyes, he watched his beloved mistress until she disappeared, at a turn in the path that led through a dense growth of flowering shrubs. 

And just one hour afterwards, the princess was reported missing. Kathryn, her maid-in-waiting, who was sent in search of her, was unable to locate her. Like wildfire, a hue and cry went through the castle and up and down the streets, that the princess was missing. 

Almost instantly, various rumors were circulated to the effect that she had eloped-had been spirited away for ransom--and that she had been hidden by friends-to save her from the loveless marriage. Unfortunately the latter report found credence in the mind of King Modoc, the powerful, despotic King of Atlanta. The rumor increased his rage, because of the ridicule secretly heaped upon him and his jilted son, Crown Prince Sigismund. 

Regardless of the sincere anguish manifested by the King and Queen of Dovette, over the mysterious disappearance of their only child and daughter, King Modoc, in his unreasoning state of mind, refused to believe that they were unaware of the princess's present whereabouts. He and his retinue immediately left Arcadia. Arriving at Helga, the capital of his Kingdom of Atlanta, he issued an ultimatum to King Augustus, in a hastily drawn-up note, to the effect that unless the princess were produced, and her marriage to his son consummated on or before noon ten days hence, an Atlantian army would take the field and invade the Kingdom of Dovette.

King Augustus, torn between grief at his daughter's mysterious disappearance and the catastrophe of being invaded by so powerful a foe, called his Council to confer upon the urgent and serious situation.

The ten men comprising the Council of King Augustus were mostly men of mature years, native born, and of more than ordinary ability, as shown in the success attained by each in his respective vocation. Sincere in their beliefs, intelligent in their decisions, and patriotic in their advice to their sovereign in all matters appertaining to the

welfare of the state, they enjoyed the respect of the populace, and the entire confidence of their monarch. 

This, however, could not be said of Ismar, the High Priest of Dovette. A man of austere bearing, between forty and fifty years of age, distinguished in appearance to an extraordinary degree, and brilliant in intellect, he nevertheless was so enigmatical to even his close friends, that familiarities with him were seldom contemplated, or if indulged in, rarely carried through. 

As the head of the Order of Ismarites, a semi-secret political organization, the office enabled Ismar, the High Priest of Dovette, to enjoy a unique if not a dominant national situation. King Augustus, while never particularly caring for the man, nevertheless conceded him his brilliant intellect and relied more than he should have upon Ismar's astrological readings and predictions. 

Such was the state of affairs, and the status of Ismar's presence at the Council table when the king notified the councilors to immediately assemble at the castle. 

After a whole day's session, hotter heads counseled armed resistance in the field; others, while they preached and advocated peace and moderation, advanced no concrete plan for the pacification of King Modoc. It was then, and not until then, that Ismar, the High Priest, rising slowly and silently, made his astounding comment. While all eyes watched him intently, he extended his open hands before him, palms uppermost, and said: "If we attempt armed resistance, the armies of King Modoc will crush us like this," and he suited the action to his words by slowly clenching his fists.

Waiting a few moments for his dramatic words and action to properly impress his hearers, he continued: "And yet, if we, the people of the Kingdom of Dovette, do not want to be crushed, we must either defend ourselves, or we must run away! To do the first is follythe second, both foolish and cowardly; but I believe there is a way out a way to at least stay the hand of King Modoc until we can produce our beloved princess, or prove her to be no longer among the living."

After another pause in which his piercing eyes looked

over the expectant countenances of his listeners, he continued: "I propose that habitations and edifices of all kinds and descriptions throughout the city, be thoroughly searched. 

I recommend that the police, and the intelligence service, co-operate in checking up all departures from the city, since the moment the princess was last seen. I most urgently recommend that a delegation be chosen here, at this conference, to wait upon King Modoc, with the authority to pledge the throne and crown of Dovette to the honest fulfillment of the marriage nuptials in ninety days; or, in the event of failure to comply, we guarantee to furnish proof that the princess is no longer in the land of the living. The fulfillment of what we promise relevant to our princess should satisfy King Modoc and stay his hand."

Had a bombshell plowed its way through the ceiling and exploded in their midst, it could not have caused a greater commotion than this most amazing of speeches. But, as everything has an ending, when the turmoil had somewhat subsided, a younger member of the assembly arose and asked, "What assurance have we that King Modoc would agree to such a proposition?" "I would see to that myself," said the High Priest, "and furthermore, I believe I can reasonably assure you all, that 

I would succeed in my mission." 

Turning to King Augustus, he continued: "Sire, what is your Majesty's pleasure regarding what you have heard?” 

Augustus, whose first start of amazement had left him in a brown study, raised his head and asked: "What would the Council suggest?"

Upon this inquiry from the king, heads began to moveto group and bend closer to each other. Finally, as if agreeing upon some unanimous verdict, they separated. 

Their ranking member, the Duke of Althunia, then arose and, bowing to the king, he thus addressed him: "Sire, believing it is your pleasure to have us state our views upon this momentous epoch of our country's destiny, it is an undeniable fact that the defensive forces at our

disposal are far inferior to the number of men and guns that King Modoc could throw against us. A war with Atlanta would soon end in our overwhelming defeat. The invasion of such a conquering army would mean the disintegration of Dovette, and would make us all vassals of 

Atlanta. Therefore, the Council's opinion is," and he paused to look around him as if to gain courage, “isthat your Majesty should accede to the plan voiced by the 

High Priest, as the most logical course to pursue." "What does the High Priest proclaim shall be the possible outcome of the pledge desired from us? Has he consulted the planets on this deplorable situation?" asked the king. "Sire," answered the High Priest, "the stars and planets are very auspicious to the pacification and enduring friendship of King Modoc. Your consent to the proposed plan would also promote the prosperity and happiness of your subjects." "Very well," replied Augustus, "so be it." 

Turning to the Council, the king dismissed them, with the words: "Gentlemen, I thank you. May God watch over and guard Dovette and my beloved daughter, the Princess."

CHAPTER VIII.
