Just four days after the council meeting at the castle, the crafty High Priest of Dovette entered his study, sat down behind the long, mahogany desk facing the door, and pressed an electric button. Almost immediately, a woman with a flushed face and perplexed frown, was ushered in by Giggi Du Mont, his secretary. "Sir," was her greeting, "are you the man responsible for this outrage?" "Fray be seated, Madame," he replied imperturbably, at the same time rising and gently forcing her to a seat in the armchair at his left. "Madame," said he, upon Giggi's exit, "I exceedingly regret that affairs of state necessitated your presence here. 

If my agents used unnecessary means to accomplish this, I tender you my apologies, with the assurance they shall be suitably punished." "Do you know that you are playing with fire?" asked she, with a hauteur that utterly failed to impress the High 

Priest. "Do you know that a word from me to Prince Sigismund would end your existence?" "Yes, but Madame Le Roy, the favorite mistress of the 

Crown Prince is much too clever to place her own neck in jeopardy. Reports also state you are more than ordinarily intelligent, Madame. Therefore, I am still hoping this interview will yet promote our better understanding." 

Madame Le Roy was a beautiful woman in her early thirties, plump, rosy-cheeked and vivacious. Born of a

Danish father and French mother, she inherited the fair skin and blonde hair of one, and the gracefulness of the other. Her presence here under a veiled threat, caused by the High Priest's knowledge of one of her late indiscretions, denoted the standing and ruthlessness of the High Priest of Dovette, who, in order to attain his ends, would stop at nothing. 58

"My dear Madame," asked Ismar, "how much do you love Crown Prince Sigismund?" "With all my heart and soul!" was the instant response -accompanied with a smile that even the High Priest seemed at a loss to fathom. "But how does it concern you?" she added. 

Ignoring her question, Ismar continued: "Do you love him more than you do your brother, Jules?" "I'm afraid I don't understand you, sir." "Perhaps I do not make myself clear-however, let it pass. Would you mind telling me when you saw your brother last, Madame?" "About four days ago." "Do you know why he went away?" "I do not!" replied Madame Le Roy rather vehemently. "I have no idea." "Or where he is now?" importuned the High Priest. "I tell you sir, I do not know, I really do not." Then, flaring up, "And if I did, I would not tell you!" "There are ways and means, Madame places that never see the light of day." "You-you would not dare to torture me?" she questioned, with a bravado that was lost upon Ismar. "Who would know?" answered he softly.

A shudder passed over the woman's frame. After a moment, which, to her jangling nerves seemed eternal, noting that the man opposite no longer spoke, but was looking at her intently, she asked: "Well-what do you want of me?" "First of all, I want you to listen to reason. Have I your promise, Madame?" 

In answering, Madame Le Roy bowed in assent.

Ismar with a satisfied air then sat back in his chair, crossed his legs leisurely, folded his arms, and resumed: "Four days ago, right here in Arcadia, a princess disappeared from her home on the very evening she was to marry 

Sigismund, Crown Prince of Atlanta. At about the same time, a super-acroplane of a special foreign construction, belonging to your brother Jules, was seen to take off from

the edge of the castle grounds." Then, intently holding her gaze, he said menacingly, "Prince Sigismund would undoubtedly be pleased to know that your love for him is so deep and great that you did not stop at the assassination of his fiancee upon his wedding day." "No! No!" almost shrieked Madame Le Roy. "She is --she is " "Safe?" inquired Ismar, finishing the sentence for her, as she stopped. "For your sake, Madame, and for the sake and happiness of numberless thousands, I sincerely hope so," he added hoarsely. Then suddenly half rising and grasping her wrist- "There are just two avenues open to you, Madame. One is the torture chamber, with its lingering forms of a thousand deaths; the other is a true, detailed report of the disappearance of the princess, and her present whereabouts, and let me assure you further, Madame, that a full confession from you is now in order." "What assurance have I," asked Madame Le Roy, "that you would shield, and not destroy me after I have told you all?" "You have the assurance that your presence here denotes; otherwise, why should I interrogate you? By turning you over to His Majesty, I could have avoided this very unpleasant situation." "And about the threat that brought me here?" "Your indiscretion with a certain person will never be divulged to Prince Sigismund." "And what about my brother?" asked Madame Le Roy, "will you protect him also?" "Only in proportion to the reward that is meted out to you." 

After another moment's hesitation, Madame Le Roy, with a sigh and shrug, began her confession. "From the first day that I heard of the betrothal, I began to fear that my position in Prince Sigismund's heart would be supplanted by another; and as I could not bear the thought of losing his love, I prevailed upon my brother to try to remove the princess from the scene. On the day of her disappearance, she walked almost within

arm's reach of where he had been hiding and watching her for days. Jules chloroformed her, enveloped her in his long cloak, and took her away in his plane." "Where to?" "To Palms Royal, at least that is what I understood." "That, I believe, is about twenty miles from here?" 

In silence, Madame Le Roy nodded. "Is the princess with him? He has not-murdered her?" "Oh! no! He was not to harm her." "Are you telling the truth?" "Yes, I am." "Very well, please be seated at this desk," said Ismar, rising, "and write to your brother as I dictate."

For a moment, a look of rebellion came into her eyes; but thinking better of it, with a shrug of her shoulders, she picked up the pen and at his dictation wrote— "Dear Brother Jules:

This will introduce to you agents of a person whose 

For my wish to see you here must not be disregarded. sake, Jules, as well as for your own, come at once." "Now, sign it in your regular way," ordered Ismar.

Whereupon Madame Le Roy added: 

Your affectionate sister,

LENORA." 

After she had also addressed the envelope, the priest picked them both up with the words: "By this time tomorrow, Madame, your brother should be here, and you will honor me by remaining until he arrives." "Thank you, sir, for your hospitality, but I do not care to remain." "I am sorry, Madame, very sorry. When preparations have already been made for your comfort, it is really unfortunate."

Without waiting for any further objections, Ismar again summoned Giggi, and ordered him to conduct the lady to 

Madame Neilson.

"Your attendant will be presented to you by my secretary," Ismar informed Madame Le Roy. "You will find her a very estimable woman. Again let me thank you for your decision to remain until your brother arrives. In the meantime, do not hesitate to ask her for anything your heart desires, within reason. "Now, if you will kindly follow my secretary, he will conduct you to her." 

On the point of turning away, a thought struck Madame 

Le Roy, which she at once put into words. "If my brother fails to put in an appearance, what am 

I to expect at your hands?" "We will not discuss so unpleasant a subject, at least not at present! Rest assured your brother will be found, if you told the truth; and when found, he will come," he added grimly, showing his well-matched teeth over a square set chin in the cold smile he gave her.

Soon after Madame Le Roy had left the room, a side door opened to admit two stalwart men. At a sign from the High Priest, they advanced to within a few paces of the desk behind which he was seated. "I have decided to again entrust you with a very important mission, one that will require tact, foresight, and perhaps more or less force. Do you understand?" 

At a nod from them, he continued, "In the consummation of your commission, even should your lives be placed in great danger, you are not to divulge your errand or make any appeal to me. If you relish not this undertaking, you both have my leave to withdraw before hearing anything further." After a slight pause he asked: "What is your decision?" "I serve Ismar, High Priest of Dovette!" responded 

Shubert, the older one of the two. "And you?" asked the High Priest, glancing at Shubert's companion. "I also, am yours to command, sir," replied Crimo. 

With an approving nod and smile, Ismar resumed: "You will leave here within the hour, for Palms Royal.

At the address herewith written (handing them the sealed

333 letter written by Madame Le Roy to her brother Jules) you will doubtless find a man whose real name is Jules Le Roy. He may not be known there by that name-he may have shaved his moustache, and otherwise disguised himself. This is a published picture of him taken several months ago. You must be thorough in this matter. I desire his presence here by peaceful means, if possible-and by force, if necessary. Do not harm him. I want him alive. 

You are to use discretion and initiative in the fulfillment of your mission. No publicity is to be given to anything concerning this affair. This is important. There is the possibility of a lady being with him; if not, try to locate her. You both knew the missing princess by sight?"

At another nod from them he continued, "If you find the lady to be the princess, you are to bring her here. No one is to see her enter here or recognize her while en route. 

She is to speak to no one! You are not to harm her! Do you follow me?" "We do, sir."

Pulling open the top drawer at the right of his desk, Ismar removed a well-filled bag of gold. "Here is a purse for both of you. It will be ample for all your needs. The roads are bad. You will have to use horses. Another purse of gold for each of you will be yours upon your return. If you also bring the princess, I will double it. That is all," said Ismar, rising. "You may go."

Without a word, both men bowed, and with a look on their faces that said they would stop at nothing to win the amazing reward, they turned about and left the room.

CHAPTER IX. 

At Palms Royal, in a modest lodging house on an obscure side street, a man sat in a small bed-chamber, bowed in thought. A loud krock brought him instantly to his feet.

Drawing an automatic from his pocket, he approached the door. Standing to one side of it, he called out: "Who is it?" "There are two men to see you downstairs," answered a voice he at once recognized as that of the landlady. "Shall 

I have them come up, sir?"

After a moment's deliberation, Jules Le Roy pocketed his automatic, opened the door, and replied: "I will come down." 

No sooner said, than Shubert and Crimo, the two men in question, brushed past the woman and entered the room, they having followed her on her way up. Motioning the landlady to get out, Crimo closed the door, while his colleague, Shubert, withdrawing his hand from his inside coat pocket, said: "Mr. Le Roy, here is a letter for you."

Recognizing his sister's handwriting, Le Roy hastily tore it open. As he read, a frown, and then a worried look overspread a face that portrayed more than the average amount of determination and daring. Turning to one of them he asked: "When did you receive this, and where?" "This morning, at Arcadia," answered the man addressed. "Who gave it to you?" "Does not the letter state?" "Never mind-it does not matter. Please tell the person 

I shall call within the next few days."

At this, Crimo, the younger of the two visitors, interposed: 64

"I believe you were to come with us, sir." "It would not be convenient for me to do so. I will call tomorrow." "I am sorry, sir, but we have our instructions." "And they are-?" asked Le Roy. "That you must accompany us!" said Crimo. "And if I refuse?" belligerently. "Our orders are to persuade you," Shubert added, with a significant dropping of his hand to his coat pocket. 

For a moment, the thought flashed in Le Roy's mind that someone was baiting a trap for him; and the impulse was to draw his gun and shoot it out with his unwelcome visitors. But then, the letter was really from his own sister -of that he was sure. Whatever desire might still have remained in the back of his head to refuse and force the issue, he now saw would be a mistake. The newcomers had anticipated his thought, and with visible automatics, were ready for him. "All right," said he, "when do we start?" "Right away, sir," answered Shubert. 

Crimo, standing nearest to Le Roy, then stepped up and disarmed him. "We have no objection to your bringing along your lady friend, Mr. Le Roy," said he. "Thank you," replied Le Roy with a worried look in his eyes. "What makes you think I have a lady friend?" "Oh, everybody has one or two," answered Crimo, with a grin. "In my case you are mistaken. I am alone."

At this, Shubert made a sign to Crimo to stay on guard over their prisoner, while he looked into the closet. Except for several male garments, it was bare. 

On their way down, the landlady was questioned as to whether or not Mr. Le Roy had a female companion, and, if not, if a lady had ever called to see him. Upon being told that, as far as she knew, her lodger had never been so honored, they placed Jules Le Roy between them, opened the door, and passed out.

The first streaks of dawn were creeping above the

horizon, when three tired, dusty men on three mudsplattered panting horses, drew up to the side door of Ismar's abode in Arcadia. A peculiar knock upon it by one of them caused it to shortly swing noiselessly open. Once on the inside, they were immediately ushered into a somber, high-vaulted room. Within a few minutes of their entry, another door opened to admit Ismar, the High Priest of Dovette. 

Addressing Shubert, Ismar asked: "Is this the gentleman you were to bring here? Is he alone?" "There was no one else there, sir," was the reply. 

With a wave of his hand, Ismar dismissed his two hirelings.

As the door closed behind them, Jules Le Roy, abductor of the missing princess and brother of the favorite mistress of the Crown Prince of Atlanta, found himself alone, in the presence of a ruthless, determined man. 

The High Priest motioned him to a seat, and exclaimed: "So you are Jules Le Roy!"

Receiving no answer other than a nod from the man addressed, he continued: "You look even more intelligent than I anticipated, and

I would rather have it so. Have you digested the contents of your sister's note? Are you ready to answer questions, to answer truthfully?" "It will depend on the questions." "Don't you realize it is sheer madness to kidnap a princess of the realm, and that a word from me would subject you to a death of a thousand tortures?" asked Ismar, half menacingly.

As the Priest was speaking, little rivulets of perspiration gathering on Le Roy's brow flowed downward over his blanched cheeks. "But, of course," continued Ismar, "it is not my intention to surrender you, if you are willing to be reasonable, and work with me." 

Still receiving no answer, he added: "Doesn't life and liberty appeal to you? Speak, I command you!"

At the priest's order, Jules Le Roy, looking squarely into 

Ismar's eyes, saw that the only hope of salvation lay in complying with the attitude of this incomprehensible man, who, unquestionably, now had him at his mercy. "Where is my sister?" "She is here, and you may see her later." "What do you wish of me?" asked Le Roy. "Where is Princess Clarita?" inquired Ismar. "I do not know, sir." "What did you do with her?" "I left her in the United States." "Could you find the place again?" 

Le Roy nodded in assent. "With whom did you leave the princess?" "She ran away." "I don't quite understand; suppose you start from the beginning and tell me the whole story.' 

Le Roy, after a moment's hesitation, began his tale. He told of the abduction of the princess; the flight across the ocean in his aeroplane, her injury and rescue by some unknown man in California, who, in reality, was John Desmond. For reasons that he knew would eventually cost him his life, he withheld all allusion to the attentions he had tried to force upon the princess, in California.

After he had completed his story, Ismar asked: "Do you know just where this place is, of which you speak?" "Yes, it's near Shasta, a small town within a few hundred miles of San Francisco." "The princess-do you believe you could find her?" "Yes, if she is still there." "How long did it take you to fly over?" "About sixty hours.' "Anybody else with you?" "No, sir." "How did you manage to keep awake?" "The princess helped some, she can fly." "Do I understand that she made no objection to goingto being kidnapped?" asked Ismar, unbelievingly.

"Well, she did at first, but not after I reasoned with her." "What did you tell her?" "I told her the truth, told her the motive was selfish, that I was acting for my sister who wished to keep the prince all to herself." "Did she believe you?" "Yes, I believe she did. Why shouldn't she?" "What makes you so positive?" "Well, there are two reasons," answered Le Roy. "First, because of my sister's very reasonable motive in removing her from the scene, and next because of Princess Clarita's mood. She was ready to believe most anything that would take her away from Prince Sigismund and her coming marriage to him." "Nevertheless, she may even now be on her way back to Arcadia?" said Ismar. "No, I do not believe so." "Then tell me, what made you hide upon your return? 

Were you afraid that Princess Clarita would appear and accuse you to his Majesty?" "No, I did not believe she would return-at least not right away, but you never can tell. Besides, someone may have seen me at the time I abducted her, and reported it. 

I was playing safe by laying low for awhile." "You said you made the trip over in sixty hours. That is very good time. What route did you take?" "The northern." "That, I believe, is by way of Belgium to Newfoundland and Canada," remarked the priest. "Yes, sir." "Is that the best way to California?" "Perhaps there is a better one," this dubiously. "But if so, I don't know it. I am familiar with this one, and like it. This is a map of the route," said Le Roy, reaching in his pocket and handing it to his interrogator. "Then, giving you an allowance of two weeks, you should have ample time to look over the ground, secure the princess and return," stated the priest. "I believe so."

"How many persons could your plane carry on a similar trip?" "Five with safety, including the pilot." "Before sundown, you shall start on another flight to 

California. You will take as your companions, Shubert and 

Crimo, the two men who accompanied you here. You can make good use of them. They are excellent pilots. Upon arriving at your destination, you are to locate, and again secure possession of Princess Clarita. Once you have done this, waste no time in taking her to my country place, the 

Chateau Minerva, at Kasalin. No one is to see her. Her success in making herself known to anyone in Dovette will incur my enmity. And let me assure you it would be deadly," said he emphatically. "As soon as you have fulfilled your mission, I wish to be so informed by one of the two pilots who will accompany you. One more word-upon the success of your mission, the reward will exceed your expectations. Fail, or betray me, and your demise will be only a matter of days. Is my proposition agreeable to you?" "Yes, I will do it willingly," was the ready response, "there is no alternative." "You are wise-very wise," remarked the priest, complacently, "and I trust you will also prove to be as competent. Better get yourself another map. I am retaining this one." "By the way, what is to become of me upon my return?" asked Le Roy. "The princess would never forgive me for the double kidnapping." "Upon your return you will be at liberty to go wherever you wish, or you may abide here in seclusion until such time as I can arrange things to your satisfaction." 

The secretary then was summoned, and at a command from Ismar, the two agents, Shubert and Crimo, again made their appearance. Again repeating his instructions about the trip to California, he admonished them as they were about to leave: "Remember, I shall expect all of you to work together. 

Any discord or failure to get results will earn my displeasure."

Then, bidding his two henchmen to remain- -as soon as 

Le Roy had left the room-he said to them: "I do not trust him, watch him closely all the time. I am depending on both of you. If you need another man, take Rocco Mattli, but don't tell him that I am back of this expedition."

Upon their assenting, he continued, "After you have secured the princess, should Le Roy's behavior ever cause you uneasiness, see that an accident befalls him that sends him where he will be less annoying. Do you understand?"

With a grin, both men bowed, and with another that was almost diabolical, Ismar dismissed them.

CHAPTER X. 

FOLLOWING the report of the pistol shot, just as Tom Norton, gun in hand, turned the corner of the ranch house, he saw a man jump from one of the first floor windows. 

Two other men, giants in size, made up the trio. One of them was carrying a limp form in his arms. Upon noting that they had been discovered, one of them fell behind and opened fire upon Tom.

Tom Norton, who from the very first, had withheld his fire for fear of hitting the girl that one of the big men was carrying, now returned the other's fire. A bullet from the kidnapper's gun just nipped the lobe of Tom's ear. Another took his hat for a sail and dropped it ten yards in the rear; a third from the man's gun went wild.

Tom, who had been shooting as he ran, now stopped, knelt, and taking careful aim, fired. A cry of pleasure escaped him when he saw he had not missed- -saw the man crumple and fall.

Norbert, and several of the cowboys, with their guns out, now came running up and joined Tom in the chase for the other two. Just as the pursuing party felt they would shortly overtake and secure the bandits, a furiously driven automobile intercepted the fleeing kidnappers. It took but a moment for the two men to pass in the girl's limp form, slam the door, and drive off in a cloud of dust; no shots were fired for fear of hitting the girl.

As none of the pursuers were mounted, the ruffians succeeded in making their escape. 

Worried and chagrined at their failure to apprehend the thugs, the party retraced their steps. Some made for the house, others ran for their cars in order to continue the pursuit.

In the drawing room the servants were wringing their hands and rushing aimlessly to and fro, in their bewilder- 71

ment. John Desmond had been shot, and lay dying, upon the drawing room lounge.

Uncle Richard met them as they entered. At the sound of the first shot in the ranch house he had rushed inside. 

One glance at the unconscious bleeding form of his beloved nephew had shown him that the situation was serious. At a sharp, snappy order from him, John was gently lifted, carried to his room and placed upon the bed. 

Before he could do anything further, Norbert and Tom both burst into the room. John Desmond's upper garments were then quickly removed, and the wound in his left breast exposed. After an examination that seemed endless, though it lasted only a few minutes, Norbert straightened up and said: "I'm afraid it is rather serious-pretty close to the heart. 

Better get a doctor; it is too much for me." 

A call to the local doctor got his promise to come at once. 

Uncle Richard then telephoned Doctor Riccardo in San

Francisco. He was about to leave on his vacation but promised to come at once, by plane. 

Upon his return to the room where his nephew was lying with closed eyes and bloodless lips-Richard Wallace found that the local doctor had arrived and was in attendance. 

Orders were immediately issued, and notices were posted at the various approaches to the ranch, requesting silence. 

All unnecessary noise in and about the house was tabooed. 

Lillian, who had become sincerely attached to Clarita, was now sobbing softly at the loss of her friend, and the murderous attack upon John.

In fact, the servants and guests were all more or less upset by the outrageous kidnapping and shooting. "What seems to be the matter, boy?" was Norbert's inquiry, putting his hand gently on Tom Norton's shoulder, as he was about to pass Tom, who in utter dejection, was silently sitting on the veranda's railing, looking out into the inky darkness. "I was thinking," replied Tom, "that if I had only wounded the man I shot, we might have made him talk." "Yes," said Norbert, "we might have learned about his companions and got a line on where they are taking the

133 little lady. Anyway, son, you certainly deserve credit for what you did. At least one of the skunks got what was coming to him!" 

Then, for the first time since the shooting, Norbert 

Miller got a good look at Tom Norton. "Why, Tom!" was his startled exclamation. "You've been hit-it's your ear-let me see it. Well," he said, looking closer, "it's not so bad-just nicked the lobe. Better come along and let me fix it up."

Lillian, who had been hovering about, and who had quickly approached on hearing that her Tom had been wounded, now recollected that she had miserably failed to pay homage to her hero, for his share of the night's occurrences. So she now edged up between them and looking up into Norbert's eyes, implored: "I can fix him up, Mr. Miller, really I can." "Miss, I am very sorry," was Norbert's answer, as he smiled down at the girl, whose upraised eyes beseeched him. "It's all right with me, but I don't think Tom would like it so you see, Miss, while I hate to, I'm afraid I will have to refuse you." "But, oh! say now!" spoke up Tom, "I don't mindreally I don't. I-I think that Miss Lillian could do it up fine." Then, getting more confident and more warmed up to the matter at hand: "I'm sure she could!"

Without answering, Norbert walked away with a deprecating shrug of his shoulders, and a knowing little smile. It was then and not until then that both the youngsters realized that Norbert Miller understood.

It was with a bewildered little laugh, that Lillian with a crimson face dropped her gaze beneath Tom Norton's adoring grin, as he followed her inside.

CHAPTER XI. 

THREE hours after the shooting of John Desmond, the doctor arrived from San Francisco in the fastest aeroplane of which the Trancontinental Air Transportation Company could boast.

He was a tall, elderly man, with a kind, intelligent face. 

Doctor Riccardo was reputed to be one of the best surgeons on the Pacific coast. Upon being conducted to where 

John Desmond lay, still unconscious, he lost no time in making a thorough examination.

All guests and retainers were excluded from the room, except Uncle Richard, Norbert Miller, the local physician, and the two nurses that accompanied Doctor Riccardo. 

In the room across the hall, several men and women from the neighboring ranches were gathered. In one corner, slumped in a big armchair, sat the little whirlwind of sunshine and humor, Lillian Douglas. With her merriment gone, she made a pathetic little picture of limp helplessness.

All activity in and about the ranch house, had been practically suspended, in the anxiety to hear the doctor's verdict. In the sick room, Doctor Riccardo finally straightened up, drew a long breath, and with a smile, said to Uncle Richard: "It is not mortal." 

At the words, Richard Wallace grasped the doctor's hands and pressed them warmly. The momentary silence that ensued was broken by Uncle Richard: "Tell me, doctor, he will recover-be himself again?" "Yes," was the reply. "The bullet just missed his heart.

One inch lower," and the doctor shrugged his shoulders and remained silent. "How long before he will regain consciousness?" asked Norbert. 74

"Not before tomorrow, but I shall have to extract the bullet at once. So if you both will kindly retire, I will call you after the operation." 

Again addressing John's uncle, the doctor remarked: "Mr. Wallace, I wish to assure you that barring unforeseen complications, as far as this matter is concerned, your nephew will live to a ripe old age." "Doctor, I thank you for that assurance. I have implicit faith in you," responded Uncle Richard, as he and Norbert left the room.

An hour later, ably assisted by the local doctor and nurses, the operation was completed and the bullet extracted. Making sure that his patient was resting comfortably, Dr. Riccardo retired for the night, leaving one of the nurses on duty. It was understood that he was to tarry at the ranch until John was out of danger.

CHAPTER XII. 

On the following morning, John Desmond regained his senses, but only temporarily. He soon again lapsed into unconsciousness. The patient's unsatisfactory condition kept the doctors and nurses on their toes for several days more. On the fourth day, the crisis passed and John once again opened his eyes to look about him with a full understanding. Though weak and haggard from his ordeal, his first words were: "Where is Clarita?" "There there now," said Doctor Riccardo, soothingly, "you must not tire yourself today. Your questions will keep until tomorrow. Everything will come out all right.

What you need now is sleep-natural sleep and plenty of it."

While saying this, Doctor Riccardo laid his soft, cool hand upon the patient's burning brow. In a few minutes, two drooping eyelids closed into what was to be a slumber lasting until the afternoon of the following day. "Who is Clarita?" inquired the doctor. "Is she here?" "No," was the disconsolate reply from Uncle Richard, "1 wish to the Lord she was!"

Doctor Riccardo was then told in detail all about the shooting and kidnapping. "Of course, you notified the authorities?" Doctor Riccardo asked Uncle John. "We did that immediately after the occurrences, but it is a forlorn hope. Under the circumstances, we do not expect much." "I am inclined to agree with you," admitted the doctor. "However, the patient is not to be disturbed or told anything before I see him again.' 

Among those who heard the news, June Ostend was the 76

only one who did not deplore Clarita's disappearance. Believing that with her rival removed from the scene, the coast was clear for her assault on John Desmond's heart, she determined to again win him.

With this in view, on the following day, immediately after breakfast, she approached the Baron with the request: "Reggie, please take me for a stroll outside where we can talk without being overheard." 

Baron Van Guard bowed his rather good-looking, aristocratic head, and offered June his arm. But not before he had looked at her with quizzical, inquiring eyes. Once outside and out of earshot, June began: "Baron, it is my belief that you are not only a gentleman, but also a business man. Am I right?" "June," was his puzzled rejoinder, "what are you now trying to put over?" "Do listen to me, please. I am really in earnest." "All right--let's hear the story."

Looking at him steadily, in a manner that would enable her to decide how her next remark would register, she asked: "Could you use $10,000?" "Yes, with the greatest of pleasure." "Well, you can acquire that sum by forgetting our differences, and helping me in what I wish to do." 

Receiving no reply, June inquired: "Are you listening?" "You bet I am! Continue the story, June, I am very much interested." "I believe that John Desmond must know something about what transpired in the arbor, on the night of the masquerade." "I heartily agree with you, June; and if you would go a little further and ask me why, I could tell you, but I don't believe I will." "Reggie, how can you! And you say you love me? 

Please go on, please do tell me everything!" pleaded June.

"Well, he knows about you and me-knows what happened in the arbor." "Did you tell him?" "Of course not. He saw us." "And you kept it from me? Reggie, I would not have believed it of you! Why did you not tell me before?" "I am no clairvoyant, June. How did I know you were engaged to him? How could I tell that you were only amusing yourself with me?" retorted the Baron irritably. "I was not amusing myself with you. You-you must not talk to me that way-I won't listen to you." "Then, what was your object?" "I-I really don't know," replied June, thoughtfully.

Then, impulsively-contritely, laying her hand lightly upon his coat sleeve, she implored: "Please, Reggie, don't feel that way about me. I am not wholly to blame. There is a certain something about you -that is that is irresistible to a woman." "Well, well, June," said he, somewhat mollified, “we will not quarrel over this; but how is it," he continued suspiciously, "that John did not tell me of his engagement to you? He and I were pretty much together, back there." "It was to be a secret until John's mother returned from abroad. I wish you would tell me, Reggie, just how much 

John Desmond knows." "He knows plenty," replied the Baron. "As you lay in my embrace--looking over my shoulder, I saw him come around the corner of the arbor, glance inside, and then give a start at what he saw. Even at that time, I felt he knew it was you, June, and was decidedly shocked." "You say I had my back to him? He never saw my face?" "No, I don't think so. I am sure he could not from where he stood." "Thank you very much for telling me, Reggie. It really is not so bad. I think we can yet arrange matters so that he can be made to believe he was mistaken in thinking he saw me in your arms." "How are you going to do it? If you know John Desmond at all, June, you must know he is nobody's fool."

"Neither am I; and if you will listen, I think you will agree with me." After a slight pause, June began. "At the first opportunity, without appearing to do so, I wish you would make it a point to get into a friendly conversation with John. Tell him that you gather from the remark he made at the dinner table, in reference to an arbor, that he must be laboring under a misapprehension as to the identity of the lady whom he saw in your embrace." "Do you think he will believe me, June?" "Yes, I do, after we furnish him with the proof." "What proof have you?" "I am about to tell you." "Well, June, granting your plan will work, it all simmers down to this; I am to lie like a gentleman, to smooth things over between you and John, so that you may again become engaged and lead him to the altar." "Precisely, Reggie. At times you are really brilliant," responded June, banteringly. "Not for $10,000." "What do you mean?" "I mean I won't consider your proposition for any such sum." "How much do you expect?" asked June, with a worried look in her eyes. "It is worth $50,000, is it not?" "No, it is not!" "John's reputed income for only one day far exceeds that amount." "What has that to do with me? It is not mine." "If it were, I would ask a million." "Impossible! The sum you ask is utterly impossible. It's preposterous!" "I do not see why. I know you possess many times that amount in your own right. However," said the Baron, turning away, "that is my offer. Take it or leave it-and 

June, dear, don't forget it includes the return of all your letters to me." "Wait!" commanded June, grasping his arm. "You still have my letters?" 

His answer was a courtly bow.

"Why have you kept them? Why did you not destroy them?" "Oh, I don't know, perhaps it was because-becauseoh, well, just because!"

At this, June laughed, and it seemed a trifle hysterically. "You are in excellent humor. Would you mind telling me what makes you so merry?" "I was just thinking of the adage I learned when I was a little girl. 'Fools rush in where angels fear to tread.' I got myself into an awful mess. I am in a trap, and I suppose-I must bay." "There now-you are really making use of your very pretty little head. I am letting you off cheap, dear, very cheap. When do I get the money?" "When I am Mrs. John Desmond." "Don't make me laugh. If I waited until then, you might renege-might have me arrested as as a blackmailer." "I would not do that, Reggie, and you know it." "Then it's a go-you accept?" "No, I don't-I can't pay you the money all at oncethat's final. I could not even if I wanted to. You must be reasonable, or it's all off." "Tell you what I will do. Could you pay $10,000 now?" "Perhaps." "Then," continued the Baron, "you give me $10,000 cash before I see John Desmond, and I, in return, to show my good faith, will give you all your letters. The balance you can pay in two installments-$15,000 when you are again engaged to marry John Desmond, and the remaining $25,000 within thirty days after you have become his wife." "That sounds good, Baron, but if I accept, what assurance have I that you will not demand more and more from time to time?" "You have none, other than my word. Remember it was you who asked for this interview."

As the tears welled up into her eyes, the Baron impulsively took one of her hands in both his own and said: "Now, look here, June; please don't do that! I am not altogether a cad!”

"I have never believed you one, Reggie; but at times you bewilder me-you seem so mercenary." "What would you have me do? I have to live. Would you have me turn bandit? Besides, do you consider my losing you, high at $50,000?" "Why, Baron, with your education, do you mean to say you could not earn a living like other people?" "I will let you decide that for yourself, if you would care to listen. Would you?" "Why, of course you know I would."

After a moment's hesitation, Baron Van Guard began to speak in a voice that was soft and low, while a far-off, dreamy look settled in his eyes.

CHAPTER XIII. 

"My earliest recollection is of a large grey stone castle, situated in the midst of extensive grounds. My father, who, at my birth, was quite wealthy, surrounded me with all the luxuries and care that money could procure for an only child. The servants, and my tutors, taking their cue from my doting parent, indulged me to a point where I grew up in the belief that idleness and pleasure were mine by divine right-that my whole life was to be just one long, glad song." After a slight pause he resumed: "Of my sainted mother, who died when I was born, I have no remembrance other than her portrait which hung over the huge fireplace in the living room. "The war found me at college. I was among the first to enlist. One year before the armistice, my father passed away. With his death, went all of my dreams estate was heavily mortgaged. the "I went through the war without a scratch, and that is something I have always marveled at, because of my then state of mind, I—I—well, I rather invited being in the midst of that man-made hell. "As to my accomplishments," continued he, now turning his gaze upon June, and showing his faultless teeth in a winsome, boyish smile, "I can dance, golf, play bridge, and billiards. I am a fair marksman, an indifferent swordsman, a licensed pilot, and a rotten card player." 

Throughout this narrative, June was an attentive listener. 

As the Baron stopped abruptly to ask, "Do I bore you?" she shook her head. "Shall I continue the entertainment?" "Please don't be frivolous, Reggie." "Very well, June, as you will," answered the Baron, with a deprecating shrug of his shoulders. "After my five years in the army, through a friendship 82

I made in the trenches, I was fortunate enough to spend two years in my country's service as assistant secretary in the Diplomatic Corps. A change of cabinets threw me out in the cold. I tried several other things after this, things that I shall not bore you by reciting. I traveled about a good deal-made money, and spent it, at times lavishly. Had I been of a saving disposition-Oh, wellwhy whine over it?" concluded he, with a grimace. "You see, my dear, I can't save money. I suppose it's in the blood." "Even so," broke in June, "you could learn, by restraint and self-denial." "No, not I. I was born indolent, brought up useless, educated to a life of leisure, and then suddenly left with my bare hands to carve out my future. A marriage that would have kept me to the grindstone, and perhaps made me a success, I lacked the courage to undertake. Suppose it missed fire and fizzled! No, the experiment would be both hazardous and unsporting. I would like to accomplish things, be somebody, soar to the heights, but, without incentive, am afraid of coming down with a thud." "Baron," replied June, with a gathering moisture in her eyes, "perhaps you are not altogether to blame. I can now see how circumstances, environment, and other causes, may create a deplorable situation, and yet," in a voice that showed increasing firmness, "an American young man in your position would at least strive all the more, with a determination to succeed, if only for the zest of doing something. Americans are brought up to believe that work -work--and more work, is a panacea for all evils. However, I think we had better get back to the object of our meeting." "Yes, I suppose we had better," agreed the Baron. Then without any warning he suddenly clasped her about the waist, drew her toward him and as she, unresisting, laid her head upon his breast, he raised her chin, and tried to look into her half closed eyes. "June, June," cried the Baron, "you are killing me. I cannot give you up, I cannot! I love you, love youlove you!"

"Don't Reggie-please don't. You only make things harder," was her low, muffled reply. 

Then as he would have kissed her-she pushed him away, with the words: "No-no-Baron, it could not be. But I am sorry-very sorry for you. Under different circumstances, I might have loved you, but I, too, must look after my resources and plan for the future.' "Why, June! You will some day inherit several millions from your own parents, besides the half million you already have." "Which would be only chicken feed to Mrs. John Desmond," replied she. "June-June," spoke the Baron, "I don't believe it I won't believe you could be so mercenary. Besides, it would be vicious. John is a fine fellow. He deserves the affection of the woman he marries." "Why, Reggie," said she smilingly, "I would never have believed you to be such a sentimental dear. But don't be silly about my attitude toward John Desmond. I really do like John, and believe I love him as much as I ever could love any man. Besides, what is to be, shall be! So now, please do pay attention to what I say." The tones of her voice, which, at first, rose and fell in cadence with her emotions, now lashed into a cold business note that was not lost upon the Baron. As he bowed and nodded, she 

"You are to tell John that you espied him on the night he glanced into the arbor and saw a lady in your arms.

Make him understand that I was not the woman. Also impress upon him that had you known he was engaged to me, and mistrusted he believed it was I, you would have made it a point to tell him who the lady was.' 

"And do you think he will swallow it?" inquired the Baron. "You must make him." "All right-tell me how." "Say to him that you can produce the woman; say she wore a similar costume to the one I had on."

"Fine; but suppose he asks me her name-suppose he goes even further, desires me to produce her-what then?" "Then you are to produce her!" "Where will I get such a woman?" "Goodness me, Reggie! 

I sometimes think you argue just for the fun of it. Would it be too great a task for you to get one of your acquaintances in the Latin quarter to represent she was the woman in the case?" "Why, of course I could. There are many girls who would do that for a consideration." "But don't forget, Baron, she must look something like me- -at least, resemble me in build." "Of course, she must-I am not altogether dumb. Ah! --by all that's good and lovely-I have it. I know just the little girl who will turn the trick-Trixie Monahan! 

She is Irish, and red-headed like you, June. I believe it was her resemblance to you, upon our first meeting, that puzzled me as to where I had seen you." "When will you get in touch with her?" "Immediately. I will telegraph her to come to San Francisco where she will be handy when wanted—and that reminds me, it will cost you a couple of thousands more for her story." "All right-you can pay that out of your first installment when you get it," said June. "I have gone my limit." "Well, we won't haggle over trifles; but really, June, you must advance the expense money to get her here.--It will save time." "Yes I suppose I shall have to, inasmuch as I have heard your finances are-are-" "Rotten-June. Say it," grinned the Baron. 

Instead, June laughed and the Baron joined her. "By the way, June, suppose John should ask you, how will you explain your seeming understanding of his remarks to you about the arbor episode? You know you are supposed to know nothing about that situation!" "Yes, Baron, I am glad you mentioned it. Let me see- 

Oh! I have it! I shall tell him that following his apparent desertion of me in New York, I surmised he wanted to be free of his engagement and I aquiesced to his puzzling in-

ferences rather than create a deplorable scene by appearing mystified." "Hang it all!" said the Baron, "June, I do not know whether I should charge you considerably more for my cooperation in losing you, or do it for nothing for you, dear."

Then, upon noting the puzzled little wrinkles gathering on her brow, he pinched her chin, waved his hand with an unconcerned air, and with a void in his heart, grinningly walked off to join Eddie Pickett, who was signaling him.

CHAPTER XIV. 

JOHN DESMOND awoke greatly refreshed. With the doc tor's permission, he was told all that had transpired from the time he had been wounded. He listened silently all through the narrative. At the conclusion, a sigh of anxiety escaped him. Turning to his uncle, he said: "Clarita, poor girl, is again in their clutches. How I wish

I could get up and search for her! If I only knew who she is, or where to look for her! Has any effort been made to find her?" "We have notified the authorities," replied Uncle Richard, "but as all parties are unknown, I don't expect much. 

Don't you know something about her, John?" "No, Uncle, not much more than you do. She was practically unconscious all the time I was with her at the cabin. 

And that reminds me that I am indebted to the owner of that cabin for its use, and for the food we both consumed. It surely solved a very serious problem." "I can set your mind at rest upon that score," said his uncle, smiling down upon him. "It belongs to me." "To you-why Uncle Richard, I never heard you mention it.' 

"For the very good reason that I have never had the inclination to occupy it. I built it with the intention of spending a week or two there in the spring of the year, with an old school mate. He died soon after it was finished. 

It would please me, John, if you would accept it. I thought that you might like it on account of-on account of—” then as he stopped and remained silent, John grasped his uncle's hand with the words: "Thanks for your thoughtfulness. I shall never part with it," and then added: "Perhaps, some day, somewhere, I shall see her again. I would give considerable to know that she is safe to have her once more-back here at the ranch." $7

"Perhaps, if you tell us all that preceded the shooting and abduction, John, it might give us a clue." "I hardly think so-but I will do it gladly. When you left me, Clarita looked worn and tired. I suggested getting her a drink. Out in the hall I told one of our boys to bring me a couple of cold lemonades. Re-entering the drawing room, I saw those three scoundrels stealthily approaching her from behind. I was not armed. Just as I cried out a warning, I felt something pierce me like a red-hot iron. I heard her scream. Then my legs gave way beneath me, and I knew I was shot. That is all I remember, nothing else." come 

Before Uncle Richard could reply, who should rushing headlong into the room, with flushed cheeks and bated breath, but Lillian Douglas. Close behind her was 

Agnes Grant. Excitedly holding aloft a lady's handkerchief, Lillian cried: "A clue I have a clue. And it's hers-it's really hers!" "What in the world are you so excited about, child?" asked Uncle Richard, as he seized her by the shoulders. "Why, John's princess," replied Lillian. Then, getting somewhat embarrassed at the break she had made, Lillian floundered around and stammered: "I-I--well-you see,-I mean-anyway," said she, straightening up her shoulders as if throwing off a heavy load, "she really looked like a princess and—and she did love John." "How do you know?" quizzed Uncle Richard. "Why, I could tell---besides, she as much as told me sothere now!" was the positive retort as she tossed her head, superiorly.

At her declaration, John Desmond reached out and grasped her about the wrist. Drawing the little madcap to him, he murmured beamingly: "Just for that, Lillian, I'll be your slave for life!" and then John and Lillian both joined in the laugh that followed.

Examination of the handkerchief showed it was made of a very costly and flimsy material. "Where did you find it, my dear?" asked Uncle Richard.

"I was walking with Agnes, when I saw it in the bushes under the window through which those horrid men took Clarita." "Yes," said John, "it is her handkerchief." "What does that little dove, and the word 'Dovette' mean? Do you know, John?" asked Agnes Grant. "It looks like a coat of arms." "No, I don't. I wish I did."

While the others were talking, the door being open, Eddie Pickett had silently entered-and for once during his stay at the ranch, he was thoroughly sober. Looking at the handkerchief, he said: "I don't kn-know what the da-dove means, but I am sh-sure Dad and I, at one time in our tra-travels, stopped at some place called Da-Dovette-I cannot see-seem to recall wa-where it was." "Please try to remember," coaxed Agnes Grant. "It might be the means of locating her." "Yes, it might help some," urged Uncle Richard. "Oh, I say!" exclaimed Eddie Pickett, "by-by Jove, why not get in ter-touch with your bah-bally state department at Wash-Washington. They should have the do-dope on this. Eh-what?"

Within the hour a message was delivered to the State 

Department at Washington. On the same afternoon, Uncle 

Richard received an answer, which he read in private to his nephew. The telegram read: "Dovette' is a small, independent kingdom buried in the Balkans, and at present ruled by King Augustus of the reigning house of Veloris. The national colors are a white dove with outspread wings, alighting on a green sheaf of olive branches in a field of amber."

While the message conveyed the requested information, the solution of Clarita's identity and whereabouts was still remote. In desperation, John Desmond chafed at his impotence to act while in his present physical condition. His love and fears for Clarita's safety drove him almost to distraction. It was Uncle Richard-good old Uncle Richard, who, placing his firm and still masterly hand, despite his

three score years, on his nephew's heated brow, declared: "During the short time the little lady was here, I, too, admired her immensely. John, I am going to send two young men of our organization over there. They shall visit this little kingdom and report to us. These two boys were brought up in the Balkans and are regular ferrets when it comes to getting data-and yet," he mused on, "if we only knew her full name, it would greatly simplify matters. In 

Dovette, 'Clarita' may be a common name, even among the women of quality such as our late guest undoubtedly was. Have you a photograph of her, or a snapshot?" asked he, looking directly at his attentive nephew. 

John despondently shook his head. "Too bad-it's really too bad. If we only had something." "Just a moment!" exclaimed John, grasping his coat sleeve. 'Why did I not think of it before? And yet-it may not help us at all," finished he, dejectedly. "What is it, John?" "When I first brought her to the cabin and started to fix up the sprain, I noticed the exact duplicate of the emblem on the handkerchief, tattooed on her leg just above the knee. At the time, I wondered what it signified." "That might help some, and again it might not. And yet, it does not seem to me that a girl of her education and breeding would mar her body with such stuff, unless it was through some religious or family tradition." 

For answer, John grasped his uncle's hand, looked up into his eyes and remarked: "Uncle, now I know why mother always said you were a wizard!" "Pshaw!" said Uncle Richard, "your mother as a little girl didn't think so well of her big brother when she would stamp her feet and shout, 'You think you know everything, don't you?' However," continued Uncle Richard with a twinkle in his eye, "like excellent wine, I may have improved with age, or, your mother has grown less discerning. "But let us get back to the business on hand, John. This girl may be of high rank and distinction, and then again,

she may not. If she is of lowly birth, or or not desirable, would you want to marry her, just the same? Of course 

I'm presuming that you love her to that extent." "Uncle Richard, to my dying day, no one could make me believe Clarita is other than one of the purest and sweetest of girls. As to her birth and parentage it does not matter. Who she is, or where she came from, mean nothing to me. All I want is to find her. I love her. Oh! how I wish I could get up and dig into it—but I am helpless. Until I recover-I shall have to depend upon you. Of course, I realize that merely finding Clarita does not mean that she will have me-but at least, I will have a chance. 

All I ask--is an even break." "John, my boy," said Uncle Richard in a cheery voice, grasping one of his nephew's limp, burning hands in both his own, "we will find her; and when we do, you will not be bothered with rivals. Yes, I would gamble on it!" 

John Desmond looked up quickly at his uncle, with a half-framed sentence on his lips, and a question in his eyes, but Uncle Richard refused to be interrogated further. Withdrawing his hand, he smilingly patted his nephew on the shoulder, and left him wondering- "What did the old boy mean by his remarks? Had

Clarita told him anything in confidence, over which his uncle was apparently gloating?"

CHAPTER XV. 

THE automobile that drove away in a cloud of dust with 

Clarita and her kidnappers, with grinding brakes came to a sudden stop about a mile away, within several yards of an aeroplane hidden behind a depression, caused by a cluster of sand dunes.

It took but a moment for the three men to jump out, carry the terrified girl through the plane's circular door and disappear inside. Instantly, 'mid a medley of explosive roars, the aeroplane's propeller began to revolve, and spinning faster and faster, it urged the plane forward with increasing speed. Taking off, in an amazingly short distance, it soared gracefully into the atmosphere. It was then, and not until then, that the scoundrels within heaved a sigh of relief.

One of them, Jules Le Roy, stooped and raised the dazed, terror-stricken girl, Princess Clarita of Dovette, to one of the four luxurious, cushioned chairs. On noting this, the second of the two, Rocco Mattli, removed the cushion from his own seat and against her faint protest, he placed it so that she might rest her injured limb upon it. 

Rocco Mattli was the man who drove the automobile that had enabled the kidnappers to escape from their pursuers. Rocco was young-about thirty years of age.

He would have been considered handsome, were it not for the scar on his left cheek. It extended from the bottom of his left ear to the tip of his chin.

To say that he was new to such acts of villainy would be making a misstatement, nevertheless he was not yet hardened to a life of crime.

Noting the look of relief within her eyes that this little attention had caused, Rocco volunteered: "I am sorry, Princess, that this had to be; but let me assure you that your safety and comfort will be our first consideration." 92

Princess Clarita thereupon nodded in acknowledgement and inquired in a tone that discounted her situation: "Who is responsible for this outrage?" "That I am not able to tell you, Princess." "Where are you carrying me?" "Back to Dovette, Princess." "To my parents at the palace?" "I am not the leader. Ask him," said Rocco, pointing to

Le Roy, who was watching them closely. 

Turning to Jules Le Roy, Clarita remarked: "I was in hopes I had seen the last of you."

Then, for the next several hours, she sat looking out, lost in thought----and her thoughts were mostly about the friends she had left behind. Finally, with a sigh, she moved in her chair, shifted her cramped position and looked about her. 

In doing so, she caught the gaze of Jules Le Roy, who, at that moment, was eyeing her intently. In order to ward off the feeling of uneasiness which his stare evoked, she inquired: "There was another man- -a giant in stature. I do not see him here. It was he who shot my benefactor. Did -did he kill Mr. Desmond?" "That I do not know," answered Le Roy, "but Shubert, the giant to whom you refer, was shot in the fracas and left behind."

At this point, further conversation was suddenly interrupted. The plane gave a great lurch, wobbled like a wounded bird in flight, and started to plunge earthward.

The fourth of her abductors, the man now piloting the plane, gave vent to an oath as he tugged frantically at the controls. "We are in for it!" he shouted back to his companions. "We will have to chance a landing." 

Suiting the action to his words, he very cleverly threw the plane out of a threatening tail spin, and dipping her nose downward, started a volplane.

The aspect of the country below them was decidedly menacing, with its miles upon miles of rugged formation and timbered hills. Here and there, small sheets of water

were visible, skirted by level spaces that were inaccessible to their crippled plane.

With blanched cheeks and pounding hearts, they sat rigidly in their seats awaiting the outcome. The bloodless knuckles of their fingers, as all hands firmly gripped the armchair in silence, indicated the extent to which their tingling nerves were tensed.

Crimo, the pilot, observing a suitable clearing, leveled out and gave her the gun. But the engine failed him, went dead. Even at that, had he seen it just a few seconds sooner, he undoubtedly would have been able to make it. But the maneuver had been just a little too long delayed. The plane lost altitude. Its landing gear scraped a tree top, got away, swooped onward and with a roar that sent a drowsing grizzly bear scampering through the dense undergrowth, it hit another pine squarely in the middle of its wide spreading branches.

The force of the collision snapped the propeller, dismantled the engine, and crumpled up both wings. As the wings impinged against the gnarled branches they entwined and held. The aeroplane's tail then slowly dropped until it touched the ground. 

The pilot Crimo, at the first impact, was dashed against the instrument board, receiving a concussion of the brain, from which he died instantly. The other men, outside of a bad shaking up, and a few minor bruises, escaped without serious injury.

It remained for the princess to demonstrate that miracles are still possible in the twentieth century. Frail, injured, and helpless, thrown from her seat with the others, she had escaped without a scratch.

A moment's survey of the situation showed both men that the easiest and best way to get out of the wreck was to cut a hole in the tail from the inside. This was soon accomplished with the aid of a sheath-knife. Upon emerging, they helped the princess to alight. Fortunately, the plane did not ignite. Had it done so, it would have been their funeral pyre. "I wonder where we are," inquired Rocco of his companion, Le Roy.

"I am not sure, but from the speed and time elapsed since we started," replied Le Roy, consulting his watch, "I should say that we are somewhere in the northeastern part of Canada." "If that is so, we are in for it-are we not?" "Yes, this part of Canada is a howling wilderness and almost entirely devoid of habitation." "Then, what are we to do?" "We will have to camp here tonight, and in the morning we will look around and try to decide." 

From boughs which they cut down with the sheathknife, the two men soon busied themselves building a shelter for the princess. After a light supper taken from provisions which they removed from the aeroplane, they constructed a couch of twigs and leaves, upon which they spread two blankets. After the princess had retired, they threw more logs on the fire, lit their pipes and began to converse in low tones. "It is going to be a tough proposition to hike with the princess," said Rocco. "In her condition we shall have to carry her. I don't think it's feasible." "Yes," replied Le Roy, "you are right. We could carry her on a stretcher if we were within a few days' distance of somewhere, but to carry her, together with a sufficient amount of food to last us on a journey of weeks, would be impossible!" "You are not suggesting abandoning her, are you?" "No, of course not," was Le Roy's reply; then, musingly added, "Death by starvation here is much more to my liking than the death I would experience should I return without her." "Say, Le Roy, would we forfeit our lives if we failed?" "Worse than that; we would be tortured." "Would the brotherhood go that far with its own?" "This is not a brotherhood affair," vouched Le Roy. "To hell it ain't," said Rocco. "Who, then, is the guy back of this expedition?"

For a moment, Le Roy was on the point of telling him, but did not. "I, too, am in the same boat as you. They never told

96.

me. Of course, I have my suspicions, but I would rather not talk about it, at present."

The look in the eyes of Le Roy, seen by Rocco by the sudden flaring up of the smoldering campfire, told Rocco that Le Roy was lying. Besides, was not Le Roy the leader of this venture? Of course, he knew! It warned

Rocco that he must be on the alert, to watch his step and feign unconcern. He was not trusted. Why not? Was Le 

Roy hatching up something? "Oh, well," said he, "what difference does it make if neither of us knows who it is? We may never get out of this alive." "Have you a family?" asked Le Roy. "No, I never married, but I had a sister in England who looked so like the princess that when I first saw her back there, it gave me a decided shock." "You are not getting mushy, are you?" asked Le Roy, insinuatingly. "Over what?" "Why, over the princess." "Yes, but not in the way your tone implies." And with the words, Rocco half arose angrily and menacingly; then hesitating, changed his mind, and with a slight twitching of the scarred side of his face, he sank slowly and silently back to his former sitting position. 

"No need for you to get sore," soothed Le Roy, "I meant no harm." "It does not matter," was Rocco's response. "But remember this Le Roy, never, as long as you live, ever make another such remark to me concerning a helpless woman!" Knocking the ashes from his pipe, he arose, yawned, picked up a blanket, wrapped it about him, lay down, and within several minutes was sound asleep.

For about an hour, the glow of Le Roy's pipe was in evidence through the gloom; then rising and putting some more wood upon the fire, he, too, rolled himself up in a blanket, sank down beside the fire, and slept.

CHAPTER XVI. 

BRIGHT and early the next morning, Jules Le Roy was awakened by the pleasant aroma of coffee. Rising to a sitting position, he saw Princess Clarita seated on a log beside the fire, watching Rocco cooking something that smelled very appetizing. "Good morning," said he, addressing the princess. Merely inclining her head in recognition without speaking, the princess turned her eyes on Rocco as he moved lithely about, preparing breakfast. 

Le Roy bit his lip, and scowled at the landscape. Within a few minutes, he yawned, arose and walked over to where she sat. "Say, princess, if you believe in diplomacy, now is the time to practise it. We are a thousand miles from nowhere and practically stranded here. Our getting out alive is very uncertain. Under such circumstances, it should behoove you to be a little more companionable," added he menacingly. "I don't associate with beasts," was the reply, with a hauteur that for a moment nonplussed him. But it was only momentarily. Recovering, Le Roy showed his teeth. in a sneering grin, as he retorted: "Even beasts at times are tactful, and especially when at their water holes." As she remained silent, ignoring him -stepping closer, he grabbed her by the wrist, and in a voice hoarse with passion, whispered-"God! but you are beautiful." 

As the grip of her wrist made her cry out with pain, the reeling form of Jules Le Roy catapulted from her and fell several feet away, sprawling upon all fours.

From the very first, Rocco Mattli had noticed Le Roy's unwelcome approach toward Princess Clarita and her repugnance at his remarks. Rocco was a globe trotter. Ever 97

since he could remember he had had the wanderlust. His travels had brought him in contact with many men and women-good, bad and indifferent. To Rocco Mattli, a good woman was a good woman regardless of race, age or creed. Rocco did not like Le Roy. For some reason or other, which he himself could not explain, he now knew he had disliked Le Roy from the very beginning. Whether it was because of the other's domineering attitude, his egotism, or the lustful gleam that occasionally flickered in his otherwise attractive eyes, made no difference to Rocco. To him, Le Roy was insufferable. So when the frail, injured girl cried out in pain at the brutal clutch on her wrist, in just three long springs, Rocco had cleared the intervening distance and struck out with all his might. His clenched fist had caught Le Roy on the point of the jaw just as he was striving to kiss the terrified girl. 

For several seconds, Le Roy lay as if stunned; then, springing up with a yell of rage, he rushed upon his opponent. The men were not equally matched in size, but what 

Rocco lacked in weight and muscle, he more than equalled by his rapidity of movement and in his knowledge of the manly art of self-defense.

Rocco eluded the rush, and as he sidestepped, connected with a short arm uppercut to Le Roy's stomach. It took some of the wind out of Jules Le Roy, cleared his brain and made him cautious. Realizing he was no match for his smaller and cleverer opponent in a stand-off fight, Le Roy suddenly rushed in, in order to get a grapple hold. 

Rocco, just as warily, eluded a crushing embrace by his dexterity and marvelous footwork.

After several more exchanges in which the defeat of Le 

Roy seemed to be a foregone conclusion, Rocco unhinged a wallop that, had it landed would have then and there terminated the fight; but unfortunately, the tip of his shoe in the advance to follow up his swing, caught in a gnarled runner and tripped him. In a flash, Le Roy's foot shot out and caught Rocco's falling body in midsection.

Writhing in the throes of a pain that, from the distortion of his features, must have been excruciating, Rocco lay almost unconscious from a broken rib.

"There, damn you!" exploded Le Roy, "let that be a lesson to you. From now on, you will take orders from me, or I will know the reason why." Then, gloating inwardly, he cast a conceited look in the direction of the terrified princess, walked toward the fire, filled a plate with all the food it would hold, sat on a tree stump and began to gorge himself in silence.

Without looking in his direction, the princess picked up a canteen of water, crept toward her now unconscious champion, and forced some of it between his lips. It was with an inward sigh of relief that she saw him stir, open his eyes and then smile up at her. "Are you badly hurt?" she asked him anxiously. "No, princess. I think it is only a few broken ribs. I'll be myself again in a few days," said he with an effort. "Oh! I am glad you are not seriously injured. Is there anything I can do for you?" "No, but I thank you for your kindness. Don't bother about me. I will be all right, princess." Then spying Le 

Roy, gasped- "You certainly fight as fair as you are manly-don't you?" "Oh, shut up!" retorted Le Roy. "I don't claim to be a saint--besides, we shall need each other if we are to get out of this. You can have a return match, upon the asking, if we survive." Then turning to the princess, "Your highness, I ask your pardon. It won't happen again.”

Le Roy then secured a strip of cloth that he had cut from the plane and started in to bandage Rocco's broken ribs.

At a council that afternoon, in which the princess joined, it was unanimously decided to build two shelters, as substantially as possible with the material and tools at their command, and to camp there until the princess was able to walk. On a high knoll, a short distance away, they decided to keep a fire burning daily, in the hope that someone would see the smoke and come to their rescue.

Luckily, besides the food stuffs in the aeroplane, which would now last the reduced party about five weeks, they found a repeating rifle, three automatics, and a large supply

of ammunition. The surrounding country seemed to be pretty well supplied with game, and with plenty of running water, all felt they could not starve for some time to come.

CHAPTER XVII. 

ON the tenth day after the departure of Le Roy and his three companions for the purpose of again forcibly taking possession of Princess Clarita, Ismar began to get uneasy at their prolonged absence. On the twenty-first day, just one week after they should have returned, even Ismar's iron nerve began to tingle with apprehension. Fretfully, he paced about in his study while his scheming brain fairly hummed with more or less daring thoughts and plans, only to be almost as instantly discarded as unfeasible.

In the midst of his perturbation, a side door at one end of the study, hidden by dark velvet drapes, noiselessly opened, and a woman entered. 

She was of medium height, plump, and about thirtyfive years of age. Her features were comely, if irregular, and her perfect teeth of ivory whiteness made her positively pretty, as she now smilingly advanced toward the nervous preoccupied master of the house. 

On observing her, the frown that covered Ismar's brow was replaced by a more congenial look. Putting his arm around her waist, he said: "I am always glad to see you, my dear. But just now I am very busy. I must think over a very perplexing matter." "Ismar, my love," said the woman, raising herself on her toes, and puckering her lips for the expected kiss, which the High Priest gave her as a matter of course, "I have had a terrible dream, and am dreadfully worried."

Receiving no reply other than his look of inquiry, she continued: “I dreamt I was going down in an elevator from the top of a very--very high building. It was so high that it reached above the clouds. Halfway down something broke, and it shot earthward. I wanted to scream-to cry out, but my tongue clove to the roof of my mouth. Glancing 101

sideways, I saw a strange lady standing beside me. She was crying. At her feet lay several dead men. One was all crushed and mangled. She pointed upward, and as I looked, I saw smoke and flames pouring forth from a moun tain top. Turning around to look at her again, I discovered she had disappeared. I then awoke, all atremble and in a cold perspiration. I could no longer sleep-it frightened me so. Ismar, you who are so learned and wise, do you think it is an ill omen to us-to the fulfillment of your plans for the throne of Dovette?" "No I think I know what your dream portends," answered he, after a moment's thoughtful silence. "Some accident has happened to the men and plane that I sent to get the princess. The elevator going down probably signifies their return trip. The flames and smoke may denote that the plane was either destroyed by fire, or they are marooned, and are trying to draw attention to their predicament by building signal fires. Yes, that must be it!" said he, with a positiveness that decided him upon his course, "they are either dead or marooned, otherwise they would have returned ere now." 

Turning to the woman beside him: “Esther, I am leaving within the hour. I may be gone for a week or more. Keep close to the house, and no matter what happens, keep your own counsel. Don't divulge my whereabouts. If my men arrive before my return, see that the princess is kindly treated, but closely guarded."

Kissing her again, he led her to the draped door which he opened and closed after her. Striding rapidly to his desk he pushed a button that instantly brought to him his secretary, Giggi Du Mont. 

"Giggi, has my new plane arrived?" "No, sir, but delivery is promised a week from today." "Well, see that my present one is conditioned and provisioned for a fortnight's absence. Also notify both of my pilots, Andre and Cyril, to be ready to leave upon my arrival at the field." "Anything else, sir?" inquired Giggi.

Ismar shook his head, upon which the secretary bowed and left the room.

Several hours afterwards, Ismar arrived at the flying field. Within ten minutes of his appearance, in answer to the rapidly revolving propeller, the plane sped down the field and clearing the ground, began to climb for altitude for its seven thousand mile trip to California.

Ismar's instructions to the pilots were to follow the course on the map he had taken from Le Roy, to keep at such an altitude as would enable them to see smoke signals over the largest possible area, and when overland to fly from sunrise to sunset only. Making himself comfortable in one of the large easy-chairs which the aeroplane contained, with folded arms he sat silently, staring at the ever changing landscape, lost in thought. 

Five hours after crossing the Atlantic Ocean, in the forenoon of the fifth day of their departure from Dovette,

Cyril, who was now piloting the plane, drew the attention of the High Priest to a thin column of smoke which was seen to rise lazily toward the sky, away to the northwest.

Through a pair of powerful field glasses, Ismar watched it intently for a few minutes, then turning to Cyril, ordered: "Full speed ahead! Get there as quickly as possible!"

Under the spur of the advancing throttle, the plane seemed to jump forward under the pull of the whirling propeller. Faster and faster sped the plane, unt the indicator showed they were darting through space at 170 miles per hour. At this point, the needle quivered, but remained stationary. Thirty minutes after sighting the smoke signal, the plane had covered the eighty intervening miles and at greatly reduced speed began to circle around and 'round in its closer approach to terra firma. All three strained their eyes trying to discern objects on the ground below them. When less than one thousand feet above the ground, in a small clearing almost directly beneath them, three figures were seen gesticulating. One of them was waving something white, to attract their attention. 

Looking about for a place to land, Cyril saw a spot that appealed to him. Shutting off his motor, he glided down to a perfect landing. The three figures were already advancing to meet them. One was a woman. Princess Clarita walked with a slight limp, leaning on the arm of one of

the men, Rocco Mattli. The other man was Jules Le Roy. 

Although both men, despite their growths of beard, were immediately recognized by Ismar, while yet some distance away, he gave no sign of knowing them as they drew up. 

The High Priest of Dovette was well known to Princess Clarita, and it was with a glad cry of relief that she approached him, believing that her deliverance from her captors and the wilderness was now assured.

Ismar was not slow to perceive her state of mind, so, drawing her to him he chanted: "My dear Princess, I thank the Lord for guiding me to your side! How comes it, child, that I find you here in the wilderness with these uncouth men? Are they the ruffians who tore you away from the castle?" 

Turning quickly toward Le Roy and Rocco, and facing them so that the princess could not see, he made a facial signal that they were not to undeceive her. "Only that man there," said the princess, pointing to

Jules Le Roy, "is the one who abducted me from the castle grounds. With several others, he again tore me from my benefactor in California, the gentleman who had previously rescued me from him. I fear him, Ismar, and I warn you to guard him closely. The other man has watched over and befriended me. See that no harm befalls him!" "Thankful am I, princess, that you are found, and doubly thankful am I that you were found by me, your most humble servant. And you may rest assured that your instructions regarding these two rascals will be obeyed. But first, please tell me the whole story. We have been much wrought up and worried over your disappearance." 

Trustfully, Princess Clarita then related all that had happened to her the first abduction from the palace grounds, by Le Roy-the temporary disablement of Le Roy's aeroplane in California, and her injury, her rescue by John 

Desmond, and his subsequent kindness-her second abduction from the Desmond ranch house, by Le Roy and three other men-the shooting of her benefactor, John Desmond -the manner of the death of one of them during the ensuing fight-the destruction of the plane on their homeward flight-the death of their pilot, Crimo, and the ob-

noxious attentions of Le Roy. She also dwelt on the injuries sustained by Rocco Mattli, on her behalf, in the fight with Le Roy.

During her discourse, Ismar listened intently, and at times, a gleam of genuine admiration shone in his eyes, for the extraordinary fortitude and courage of this little slip of a girl this hothouse orchid born to the purple. But such kindly thoughts were of short duration. Ismar's ambitious scheme to make himself the ruler of Dovette would have caused him to shed even the blood of this innocent girl in the fulfillment of his dreams. "May the Lord forgive me for my unkindness of spirit," said he, "if I, dear Princess, rejoice in the knowledge that at least two of the villains met their just deserts. As for the other two, we shall see. Their fate will occupy our proper consideration." "But I do not wish bloodshed," said the princess, "and I especially desire tolerance and consideration of Roccothe one with the scar, whom I have forgiven. He has watched over me like a brother, and has guarded me from evil." "Very well, Princess," replied Ismar, "your wishes shall be complied with. It is for you to command, and for me to obey."

As he was about to walk away, he was detained by the 

Princess, who asked: "Pray tell me I crave news of their Majesties, my father and mother. How fare they?"

Drawing her to one side, out of hearing of the others, he told her: "They are well in health, Princess; but much worried about your mysterious disappearance. The peoples of several nations are searching for you. Prince Sigismund is heartbroken over your disappearance. I verily believe he loves you, and your marriage to him may yet bring you happiness."
