"Thank you very much," responded the princess, "for your assurance of my parents' health; but if you would earn my everlasting gratitude, mention not his Highness, but take me back to California. I would again see my

friends at the Desmond ranch, and tarry there awhile. On my arrival there, my parents shall immediately be notified as to my welfare and whereabouts. Besides, I believe my late acquaintances sincerely liked me, and shall worry much from the manner of my departure." "Your own kin and friends in Dovette worry also, Princess; think you more of these strangers?" "No, but my return to Dovette would mean my marriage to Prince Sigismund, whom I do not love-I detest him, and with reason. Wilt thou do my bidding?" "Alas! Princess, it grieves me deeply that I cannot so serve you. I, Ismar, High Priest of Dovette, and faithful subject of His Majesty, King Augustus, would betray my priestly oath, and a Judas to my country be, if I favored your whim by my compliance. 

Besides, dear Princess, granting your caprice would cost me my head."

And with a bow, which, under the circumstances, was a trifle lower than the one ordinarily required, Ismar, the 

High Priest of Dovette, schemer, kidnapper, and traitor, turned abruptly away to prepare for the return trip to Dovette. 

It was with a superhuman effort that Princess Clarita held back the mortifying tears which welled up in her eyes.

Torn between conflicting emotions-the love of her home and parents and her love for John Desmond-for it was now that with a start it dawned upon her, and with a force she could not deny, that she loved John Desmond, loved him with all her heart and soul, and had loved him fondly and devotedly from the very beginning! 

In her dilemma and flurry, without really knowing how it would benefit her, she entered the plane, and unobserved, hastily penned a note. Inserting it into a bottle, she then threw it out of the window. No one saw her do this but 

Rocco. He was facing the plane, while listening to Le Roy rave about Ismar's peculiar attitude. 

Ismar, who had been standing a short distance away, heard his name mentioned. Turning around, he seemed to know, by the expression that flitted across Rocco's countenance and by Le Roy's violently animated gestures, that Le Roy's remarks were of a nature derogatory to him.

It would be a wise policy to watch Le Roy or get rid of him, thought Ismar. "That's it!" he muttered. "Why not?"

It should be easy on the return trip, and yet, that would necessitate a disturbance in flight, which the presence of the princess would make inadvisable. 

And yet, Le Roy, alive and at liberty, was dangerous. 

Besides, as the brother of Crown Prince Sigismund's mistress, Le Roy was in a position to some day ruin him. For that matter, so was his sister. But then, she too was deeply in the affair and therefore dependable; besides, she knew not of his instructions to bring the princess to him secretly. 

Dead, her brother could tell no tales. If it became necessary she would be told the mission was fruitless, the princess had disappeared. Therefore, the High Priest decided that Le Roy must die, and in his journey into the unknown, Rocco should accompany him.

Ismar felt sure that they had been exchanging confidences. Under such circumstances, the death of one was now as desirable as that of the other. With this object in view, he beckoned to his two pilots, Andre and Cyril. Out of earshot of the others, he sounded them out as follows: "You have both heard the story of the princess; also her charges against Le Roy and Mattli, which they fail to deny. Now, while I should very much prefer to bring them to Dovette and turn them over to their Majesties for punishment, I don't fancy being in such close quarters with them. To leave them behind and set them free is out of the question. I might take their punishment into my own hands, shoot them before we start and have it over with; but that, to the princess, would appear to be a bloodthirsty act. I have called you in conference, wondering whether you could suggest a way out of the situation." "I think," said Cyril, "that if we shot them down when the princess was not looking, and told her that they were trying to escape, it would get by." "No," said Ismar, "your plan, although meritorious, will have to be modified." Adding, "If it were possible to have them make an attempt to escape, and they were shot down while running, their deaths would be justified. "On the other hand, there is Rocco to be considered. He

has befriended her-and to have him killed might raise her ire or suspicion-something which I could not afford to do, especially if she ever carried it to the king, her father." "Then why aim at him at all?" suggested Andre. "Why not allow him to get away?" "How will they be induced to make the attempt to escape?" asked Andre of Cyril. "Leave that to me," interrupted Ismar. "I will drop them a hint."

CHAPTER XVIII. 

ABOUT ten minutes before the time of their approaching departure, Ismar drew Le Roy and Rocco to one side and began. "I suppose you both understand that if you return with me, I shall have to surrender you to King Augustus and the lingering death that will be meted out to you?"

Receiving no answer to his surprising question other than their looks of amazement, he resumed: "And your only chance to live and enjoy life is for me to leave you here. We are in Canada, and as near as I can figure it out, about seven hundred miles from the Atlantic seaboard. By traveling east, you should, in less than three weeks, get back to civilization and liberty. I propose to leave you behind, if you care to accept the chance I am now giving you.' 

"How shall we live during the march?" inquired Le Roy. "I will leave you all the provisions I can spare; also firearms and ammunition. What you lack, you could easily acquire en route, by the game you bring down." "What if we refuse?" inquired Le Roy. "Then I shall take you with me and turn you over to 

His Majesty. I would have no alternative." "What about our arrangement?" again inquired Le Roy, belligerently. "When you get to the sea coast, send me word as to where you can be reached, and you will have no regret as to the reward you both shall receive." "What about my sister?" "No harm shall befall her. She will be suitably rewarded." "I still believe you should bring us along and then turn us loose in Europe," spoke up Rocco. "That is something I would rather not do," replied Is- 109

mar, "because the possibilities are fraught with exceeding danger. An accident en route, or a forced landing, might be the cause of my having to treat and guard you as real prisoners. The opportunity to escape that is now before you may never again be possible. So you see, if you accompanied me, regardless of my inclinations, for my own safety I might eventually have to deliver you to His

Majesty. Therefore, it is now up to you both to make up your minds, and to do so without any further delay." "Under such conditions," answered Le Roy, "I shall have to accept-but I don't like it." "And you?" asked Ismar, turning to Rocco.

For a moment, Rocco seemed on the point of refusingof telling Ismar he preferred to go to Dovette. But on second thought, he wondered if the pardon of Princess 

Clarita, in his behalf, would also be granted by her father,

King Augustus. If denied, his goose was cooked, and in dying, his death would be by torture. 

Turning to Ismar, he said: "I, too, don't like being left here. Nevertheless, I'll stay with Le Roy." "Good!" exclaimed the High Priest. "Now, pay strict attention to what I say. Just as we are about to depart, I will call you to come aboard. That will be the signal for you both to make a break for yonder thicket. Andre and 

Cyril will fire upon you, but do not be afraid. running. They shall be instructed to aim high." 

Keep on

Without another word, he turned and left them. Rocco looked at Le Roy and said, flatly: "Well-that's that! What do you think of it?" "I don't like it. That damn priest is crooked- as crooked as hell! I know enough about him to make my death desirable. I don't trust him. He's a snake and a devil incarnate." "Then why is he giving us this chance to get away?" was Rocco's surprised inquiry.. "I really do not know," was the hesitating answer, "unless he is afraid we will talk when we get back. I could say enough-if I wanted to-enough to cost him his head."

"But he is giving us a chance to get away. He would not do that, if he feared us!" "That is just what he would do, if he believed we never could make it, and starve to death." "Then you think he will double-cross us, as to the provisions and firearms?" "Yes, I do! It is my firm opinion that he will leave us without a scrap of food or means to get it. Besides," continued he, in a burst of confidence, "I don't believe he intends to surrender the princess-at least, not right away." "Why do you say that?" "Because he sent me upon this mission to get the princess and deliver her secretly to him." "But that is treason. You certainly have it on him there." "He knows it; so is holding my sister as hostage." "Does your sister also know about this enterprise?" "Only in a general way.' 

"If he seeks your life because of what you know, what is to stop him from taking hers also?" "Nothing. She is in his power and nobody knows it!" "Do you think he would have the nerve to do it?" "You don't know Ismar. He is ruthless." "What is his object--is he in love with the princess?" "Love? Hell no! Ismar is too cold-blooded and ambitious for anything like that. If he is contemplating any 

Romeo stuff, it would be to perfect his schemes. I've a hunch he would like to be king."

Their further conversation was cut short by the explosions of the aeroplane's engine. As they glanced over, they saw Ismar beckon and call to them.

With one accord, both men started a sprint for the thicket a short distance away. Neither had gone more than a dozen paces, when the spiteful cracks of three automatics split the air. At the first volley, Rocco saw Le Roy throw up his hands, pitch forward and fall, with a surprised, agonized look upon his face. At the second, he felt a redhot iron on the side of his head.

Like a flash, it occurred to Rocco that Le Roy's presentiment of foul play had been well-founded. Instantly,

he also threw up his hands and fell to the earth. With his heart racing to the point of bursting, he lay motionless, feigning death; and it was fortunate that he did so, for contrary to the original plan, Ismar had added to it. Believing it unwise to spare either, he had instructed his pilots accordingly.

Turning to his men, and to the terrified princess, Ismar said: "Well, they are both dead; and perhaps it is just as well -yet I am sorry for the poor devils; let us go aboard and leave this place. It oppresses me.' 

"Do you intend to leave them without first seeing if they are dead?" asked the princess. "But I am quite sure they are." "Then, why not bury them?" "Because, princess, we have no time to lose." "Then permit me to see if they need further aid. I could never be happy in thinking they might still be alive and that we left them here, to die." 

Noting that she was bent on making a personal investigation if need be, and not wishing to cross her at this early period, Ismar gently pulled her back with the words: "Princess, I admire you for your charitableness of mind. 

But both men are dead. I am sure of it. However, knowing it is your pleasure, I will personally do what you desire."

Without another word, with a diabolical grin, he started toward the body of Le Roy, which lay several yards nearer than the motionless form of Rocco. One look at the man's face, and the priest knew that death had come to Le Roy suddenly--so suddenly that he never knew what had hit him. Looking down upon the man he had so treacherously murdered in cold blood, the High Priest muttered: "It is well that you are really dead, because I certainly would not report you to the princess as needing any further aid." With a satisfied look he stepped over Le Roy's dead body as he headed for Rocco Mattli. Lying flat on his face, with his hat partially obscuring his features, through half-closed eyes, Rocco had been covertly watching Ismar while beside Le Roy's dead body, and wondered what he himself should do to turn the scales on the priestly assassin.

Should he jump up and make another run for it, or should he lie perfectly still, feign death, and try to bluff the High Priest? The first, he immediately abandoned, because of the gun in Ismar's right hand. At such close quarters, Rocco knew that Ismar could not miss him. The second plan he also decided to discard. He felt he could never carry the bluff through! Ismar would be sure to see through his subterfuge and shoot him as he lay. Whatwhat should he do?

The perspiration was pouring down his face as he lay thinking-waiting--worrying. The blood flowing down his face from the wound on his head, now almost blinded him. With his nerves on edge, it finally came to him-came with such force that he almost betrayed himself in the fierce exultation and resolve to see it through. "At least," muttered Rocco to himself, "if I fail, I will die fighting, and not like the beast caught crawling on his belly." 

His plan was to grasp Ismar's legs, as he came within reach, and bring him down. After that was done, he must beat him in the scramble for Ismar's gun. Rocco figured that, in the surprise and violence of his assault, the Priest would drop it. But if the High Priest, by any chance, still retained it in his grasp-well, then, it would still be a fight between them for its possession. And because of the two pilots, Andre and Cyril, who naturally would instantly rush to Ismar's assistance, his assault would have to be short and definite. 

If he could only get possession of Ismar's weapon without having to kill him. With the muzzle prodding the priestly ribs, Rocco felt that he and not Ismar would be master of the situation. He would compel the High Priest on the threat of instant death, to order his men to keep away, and to do whatever he, Rocco, might then deem advisable for his safety. But what if he failed? "Oh, well," thought Rocco, "nothing ventured, nothing gained-it's my only chance."

Out of the corner of one of his now half-closed eyes, Rocco watched and waited for the approaching unconcerned

murderer. Only fifteen feet separated them when Ismar was seen to stop, hesitate as if undecided, and then again resume his forward journey.

Ten feet now separated them as Rocco, in a sort of fascination, dropped his stealthy gaze from Ismar's face to the high boots he wore. "One-two-three-," Rocco counted the approaching strides, and just as he tensed himself to rise on his hands and knees, to make a forward spring at those menacing boots- 

Again Ismar stopped-hesitated-"Hang it all!" said he to himself, looking down at Rocco, "you must be dead, all right. You have not moved since you were hit. But, if you are still alive, turning you over might cause a movement that would not escape the eyes of the little minx behind me. She is mistrustful, as it is, and I cannot take any chances—not until I get her where I want her. Anyway, you are dead all right, and if not, you will never be able to move from this spot-the spot upon which you will rot!"

Ismar, the High Priest of Dovette, thereupon turned on his heels and rapidly walked back to the plane. Encountering the inquiring eyes of the princess, he said, calmly: "I am sorry to report to your highness that both men have gone to be judged for their misdeeds. I further wish to assure you they both died instantly and painlessly."

Then, pursuant to his orders to depart, the engine's exhaust again split the still and silent air of the Canadian wilderness. As they grew louder and louder, the propeller spun around faster and faster until, with a shrill whir that seemed to be a furious cry of rage at leaving, the plane suddenly darted ahead, and in a matter of seconds, cleared the ground, as it soared into space. With her nose pointed due east, Rocco saw her climbing and traveling at a terrific pace, on her return trip to Dovette-thousands and thousands of miles away across the Atlantic.

From his reclining position on the ground, Rocco Mattli waited until the plane disappeared from view, before rising to walk over and examine the extent of the injury to his companions. Jules Le Roy lay perfectly still, upon his face.

Turning him over on his back, one glance told Rocco that he was dead.

With a sigh of regret at the fate which had overtaken him and thanking God for his own escape from the assassin's bullets with his bare hands and sheath-knife, he dug a shallow grave beside Le Roy's body. As a sunbeam, crossing the top of a nearby fir tree, crept over the hole's edge, he gently rolled the body into it, placed a handkerchief over Le Roy's upturned face, and began to fill in the soil. Smoothing the top of the grave, he next covered it with a pile of rocks and stones, against the depredations of wild animals.

On completion he knelt down beside it and prayed to the Almighty Father to receive and forgive the earthly sinner, whose soul was even now speeding through space to come before him for judgment.

It was dusk when he completed this last service to one whom he had reason to despise, but whose loss increased his loneliness. He also missed the presence of the lovable maiden, the princess, whom he had learned to love and honor in the same measure that he would have accorded his younger and only sister, were she alive. 

He then remembered about the bottle he had seen the princess throw out of the plane. As it was now too dark to search for it, he resolved to do so the first thing in the morning, when he would also look for the food and firearms. His last thought, as he fell into a restless slumber, was that Le Roy was right-he would find neither food nor firearms on the morrow. 

The following morning at daybreak, he located the bottle without much trouble. But when the promised food and arms failed to materialize, Rocco thanked his stars for having secreted at odd times, unbeknown to Le Roy, some canned provisions, with two automatics and a supply of ammunition, for the safety of himself and the helpless princess. From this cache of provisions, he cooked himself a breakfast, and while eating remembered he had failed to read the note in the bottle. He now proceeded to do so.

For several minutes, he deliberated as to whether he should make for the Atlantic seaboard 700 miles away, or

for the Desmond ranch in California, three times as far. 

While still undecided, he suddenly arose with a glad cry on his lips. "Mary Mary dear! What are you doing here? I was told that you were dead!" For, before him, stood the beloved girlish form of his only sister, who, with a smile on her lips, was looking at him and pointing westward; and as he, spellbound, stretched out his arms to enfold her, she faded away.

With drops of perspiration on his brow, he stood tensebewilderedly gazing at the spot where she had come and gone then, without a word, Rocco Mattli hurriedly broke camp, and wrapping all his provisions in a strip of canvas cut from one of the wings of the wrecked aeroplane, he swung the bundle over his shoulder and started grimly and silently westward-on his two thousand mile jaunt, to deliver Princess Clarita's letter to John Desmond-with the determination that he would make it, and that nothing could stop him!

CHAPTER XIX. 

FOUR weeks after Clarita's kidnapping, John Desmond was up and about again. Twice a day he heard from his uncle, now in New York. Clarita was still missing. The several detective agencies employed to find her had fruitlessly followed and investigated a hundred clues from the four corners of the earth.

The whole New York crowd was still at the ranch. John was pleased and happy to have the party linger. June Ostend's everlasting persistence in hovering around him, and her more or less ostentatious little attempts to please, amused, but at times also annoyed him. Nevertheless, he felt sorry for June-sorry because he had misjudged her so on a certain night in New York, several months previous. 

John Desmond now believed it was another woman whom he had seen in the arbor, on the night of the masquerade, clasped in Baron Van Guard's arms and smothered by his kisses. At first, he had listened to the Baron's tale of the affair with inward disgust, at what he deemed was a clumsy falsehood. But, when the Baron said that he could prove it, and then at his own expense had undertaken to have Miss Monahan come to California just to corroborate him, John was convinced that he had been mistaken.

He also thought it was fine of Miss Monahan to go to so much trouble in order to square a misunderstanding brought about by circumstances of which she had been the innocent cause. 

John Desmond was sensitive, emotionally. He was humiliated by the knowledge of the injustice he had done June and the deplorable position he had placed her in in having believed her a cheat.

While the old feeling for her could never be revived, he felt guilty and sorry for her. Every time they met in or out of the ranch house, June's sad smile and cheerful greeting tugged at his very heartstrings. 117

On the other hand, June was not only pretty, but since the night of the shooting she had grown almost ethereal, in her humility and willingness to please him. Naturally, in return, her good-fellowship was reciprocated by attentions that he would not have shown her under different circumstances.

While June could not decipher that which had not as yet crystallized in John's mind as to her, she sensed that there was something in the air, and made it a point to be with him whenever possible. She knew that when a man's heart is harassed by the thought that he has wronged the woman that loves him, the combination invariably and unexpectedly creates at least one opportunity to reclaim him, and in this June was not disappointed. The opportunity came even sooner than she anticipated. 

Just two days after Trixie Monahan's departure from the ranch, while sitting on the veranda in the moonlight, before John knew it, he was holding June's slim, white hand and asking her to forgive and forget the wrong he had done her.

June Ostend was a twentieth century product of feminine resourcefulness. She was clever, alert and energetic.

June knew the power of her charms on the opposite sex and never allowed the grass to grow under her feet by sleeping at the switch when the gong struck. Before John knew what he was in for, with a glad little cry, she threw her well-molded bare arms rapturously around his neck, and with a sob, puckered up her lips, as she clung and cuddled to him.

John was young-impressionable the moon was shining -and June could be an alluring siren beneath the spell of its silvery glow. So he naturally returned her embrace. "John, dear," whispered she, drawing a perfumed handkerchief daintily across her eyes, "I forgave you long ago. 

I could never, never be angry at you, because I love you, dear--I shall always love you." 

And then the skies fell for June Ostend. The precarious tete-a-tete was interrupted by the unexpected appearance of 

Baron Van Guard. His presence broke the spell that June was so cleverly weaving.

"Hello!" greeted Van Guard, "am I intruding?" "No, not at all," was the somewhat relieved answer of 

John Desmond. "Come over and sit down," requested John on noting the Baron about to retreat. Reluctantly Baron 

Van Guard advanced and sat down. His presence inwardly enraged June. Shortly after, with a side glance at the 

Baron, that could have annihilated him, she arose, pleaded a headache and left them. Once in her room, she relieved her anger by kicking a hassock that lay in her way and upsetting a chair that likewise offended her.

During the remainder of her visit, June tried again and again, but again and again failed to get John into a similar situation. For many days after, her emotions arose and seethed every time she saw the Baron, because she believed that through his impromptu entrance, she had lost her golden opportunity to corral John Desmond and the untold Desmond millions.

Such was the state of affairs at the ranch, when John Desmond, out on his afternoon ride, saw the weary figure of a man approaching along the sun-baked road that cut through a corner of the Desmond domain. As the stranger drew nearer, John noted he was young, dusty, and extremely tired.

His emaciated condition, ragged clothes and worn-out shoes, indicated that he had been long on the road, and had come from afar. "Hello, buddy," greeted John Desmond, as the man came abreast of him. "Want a lift? Where are you headed for?" "For the Desmond ranch," was the tired response, as he stopped and gazed at Spitfire, with admiring eyes. "I say," said he addressing John, "that's some mare!" 

As if she understood the man's remark, Spitfire threw up her head and started to prance about, making it necessary for her master to tighten up on the rein and talk to her caressingly. "Yes," said John, "I am very fond of Spitfire. Climb up behind me and I will give you a lift. It is only five miles to the ranch, and Spitfire can easily carry two."

While en route, the few remarks between them were confined to the weather and the country through which they rode. Arriving at the ranch, they dismounted. John turned 

Spitfire loose, and with a playful kick of her heels, she made a bee-line for the stable. "Whom do you wish to see here?" asked John, addressing his companion. "I came to see Mr. Desmond," replied the man, none other than Rocco Mattli. who was "Well, you are now looking at him," replied John. "What can I do for you?"

At his words, a look of surprise and pleasure overspread Rocco's face-one that made John Desmond smile in return. "You are Mr. Desmond-Mr. John Desmond?" 

For answer, John nodded. "The same man who was shot about four weeks ago?" "Yes, the same one."

A look of relief came into Rocco Mattli's eyes, on learning that the man before him was actually John Desmondthat he was alive, and had not been killed at the time of the kidnapping. The fact that he, Rocco, had refused to harbor the thought during his long, weary journey, did not mean that it had not worried him. "Gee, Mr. Desmond, but I am glad-glad to see you alive!" "Thanks, Buddie. What is your name?" "Rocco Mattli, sir." "Hey! Tom," called out John as his chum hove in sight. "Come here, will you?" 

Tom Norton hastened toward them. "Tom," said John, "this is Rocco Mattli. Please conduct him to the cook, and see that his wants are supplied." 

Then turning to Rocco, he continued: "We really won't need extra hands until the round-up, but I guess we can find something for you to do around here, if you care to linger. See me again after you have had something to eat, and rested up!" "Thank you, sir, for your kindness. I really am hungry and very tired, too; but it can wait. I've come almost two thousand miles-part of it afoot, to see you, and give

give you" and with these words Rocco suddenly collapsed. Tenderly they raised and carried him into the house. Norbert, on being sent for, took charge of the situation. He had the unconscious man undressed and put to bed. "Rest, and plenty of it, is what this man needs most. 

He is all in," said Norbert.

The morning after John had emulated the Good Samaritan to Rocco Mattli, he was accosted with the words: "Sir, may I have a few minutes in private with you?" "To be sure you may," assented John, recognizing Rocco, who had just risen from a chair on the veranda. "How are you feeling?" "Pretty well, thank you, sir." "What can I do for you?" "What I have to say concerns you personally, and the lady who was taken away at the time you were wounded." "Yes?" eagerly inquired John, now intensely attentive. "Proceed, man-go on, I am listening!" "First, I must have your word that, no matter what I say to you as to what I know or did, you will forgive and protect me." 

At his words, John turned red and then pale. Grasping the man with hands that shook with agitation, in a subdued, intense voice he replied: "Man alive! I promise you anything-I will protect you to the fullest extent of my power, if you will only give me news of Clarita. Tell me where I can find her! Is she well? 

Is she safe?" Then, as a thought struck him, "My promise to you holds good only if you have not harmed her. 

Can you assure me on that point?" inquired John, looking him squarely in the eyes. "Yes," said Rocco, returning look for look. "I went even further; I protected her."

At these words, John let go of the man's shoulders, and grasping Rocco's right hand in both of his, pressed it warmly, with the words, "For that, I am your debtoryours for life. Now let me have the story." "Very well, sir, I am one of the kidnappers." "What? You!"-for just a moment, John Desmond

seemed upon the point of forgetting his promise. He ached to take the law into his own hands, and then and there throttle the man who stood before him. But as he looked, his muscles relaxed, and a look of pity spread over John's face as he was heard to say: "Poor fellow---you must have had a trying experience. 

Better go in the house and lie down. Keep out of the sun as much as possible-at least until you feel like yourself again."

Believing the man's narrative was just an hallucination caused by his hardships and run-down condition, John 

Desmond turned gently away. He had taken but two steps, when he heard: "Mr. Desmond, perhaps this will convince you that I am in my proper senses." Putting his hand in his pocket,

Rocco drew out a soiled and much-rumpled scrap of paper. 

It was the note that Princess Clarita had thrown out of the plane at the time of her departure with Ismar. 

John took the letter. As he read, the expression on his face convinced Rocco that the two-thousand-mile journey and the attendant hardships and hunger that he had experienced en route, for the purpose of delivering the letter, were justified. 

This is what the letter said: "John Desmond, Desmond Ranch, Shasta, California. 

My dear Beloved:

They are taking me far-far away, to marry a man whom I despise. I pray that you are alive, and that this letter may reach you. I shall never-never forget you. 

Who I am, and where I go, cannot matter now; but I want you to know, John dear, that I love you-love you dearly. I shall never love another. Goodby, think of me sometimes, and may God bless you, is the prayer of your heart-broken

CLARITA.” 

A round splotch, made by a tear drop, almost obliterated the signature. 

As John perused Clarita's despairing note of farewell, cold beads of perspiration gathered and stood out on his

smooth, massive brow. Again and again he read and re-read her beloved letter, as if he would, for all time, engrave on his throbbing brain, her each and every word. Turning to 

Rocco, he said in a voice surprisingly calm, and entirely out of keeping with his turbulent feelings: "Where did you get this?" "In Canada, about seven hundred miles from the Atlantic seaboard." "When did this happen?" "About four weeks ago." "Did-did she give this to you?" "No, sir, the princess threw it out of Ismar's plane as he was taking her back to Dovette." "The princess? Who is she?" "The lady we kidnapped from here. Princess Clarita of Dovette." 

Bewildered, John then asked, "And who-who is Ismar?” "He is the High Priest of Dovette."

On the point of questioning him further, John desisted, put his arm about Rocco's shoulders, and said: "Please come in the house and tell me all about it."

Into a room that John called his den he conducted Rocco 

Mattli. Then sitting down opposite him, he said: "Please go on with your story; tell me everything-everything, no matter how trivial, from the beginning to-to the very end."

Rocco thereupon told him that he had driven the car which picked up the fleeing kidnappers on the night of the shooting-told about bringing them to the waiting planeabout the flight, and the destruction of the plane, in the 

Canadian wilds-the death of the pilot, Crimo, at the time of the impact-the camp in the wilderness-Ismar's arrival in another aeroplane-the treachery of Ismar-the death of Jules Le Roy-and his own narrow escape. 

Modesty made him omit any reference to his own injury from his fight with Le Roy on behalf of the princess. This, John Desmond learned some time afterwards, from another source.

After Rocco ended his recital of the above events, John asked: "How did you make the trip here in only four weeks?" "By foot and by a lift whenever possible." "Where were you born?" "In Atlanta." "Are you married?" "No, sir." "Do you speak the language of Dovette?" "Yes, quite fluently." "How would you like to work for me?" "Very much, sir." "All right, consider yourself hired. Now please answer a few more questions. How did your party locate the princess in so short a time?" "I don't know, sir, other than what I heard Le Roy say about a previous trip here, and a fight. He said that when he had climbed several hundred feet, he spotted a cabin, which he believed belonged to his assailant. He returned the next day and saw him there. He said he was sure that the princess was somewhere inside the cabin." "But how did he discover I had taken the princess to this ranch, over twenty miles away, on the same night that I removed her from the cabin?" "Well, sir, we probably would not have found you-at least not right away, had it not been for the bonfire. We made a landing a short distance away, and Le Roy came over to investigate. He learned that you had returned with a girl who had been injured in the desert several days before. We thereupon laid our plans. I was to get hold of a car and be ready to intercept and pick them up. They figured on being pursued." 'Anything else?" asked John, as the man stopped talking. "That is all, sir." "Thanks for what you have told me, and for what you have done in reparation for your connection with the affair. 

Please leave me, as I want to think the matter over. You are now in my employ, so make yourself at home. Take it easy and keep silent as to what you have just told me."

CHAPTER XX. 

TEN minutes after Rocco had left the room, John sent the following wire to his uncle at the Desmond offices in New York: "Have important news regarding Clarita and her present 

She is of royal birth. Please run over. whereabouts.

Urgent. JOHN."

On the very same day, a few hours after this wire had been dispatched, John received the following code message from James Holland and Henry Higginson, the two young men that his uncle had sent to Europe: "Have learned 'Dove' on olive branches, tattooed on right knee of both sexes, is a prerogative of Dovettian royalty. 

Same emblem on field of amber is national flag of 

Dovette. Believe late guest is Princess Clarita, only daughter of Augustus, King of Dovette. Have sent you telephoto of Princess Clarita. Several others by mail. 

Princess was kidnapped from castle grounds on day of her wedding to man she despised, Crown Prince Sigismund of Atlanta. Prince is a flagrant sensualist, illlooking and aged. Atlanta is an adjoining and much more powerful kingdom. Await instruction.

J. H. & H. H." 

The telephoto of Princess Clarita was soon followed by another code message that said: "Ninety days' truce granted by King Modoc, for appearance and marriage of missing princess to Crown Prince

Sigismund, terminates in six weeks from today. Armies of King Modoc on Dovette's frontiers. Exodus of King 

Augustus' subjects getting serious. Dovette's army well trained, capable, but much too small to offer serious resistance. Royal family and court ready to flee, possibly abdicate. J. H. & H. H.” 125

John immediately sent this code wire in return: "Telephoto of Princess Clarita received. She is the person you were sent to find. 

High Priest of Dovette. 

Princess in custody of Ismar, Should have arrived there several weeks ago. Don't tell authorities. Watch his residence. Rescue her if possible. 

Keep me informed, at least daily. 

Spare no expense.

J. D." 

Two days later, Richard Wallace arrived from New York in a special aeroplane. He immediately went into a conference with his nephew that lasted the rest of the day and well into the night.

Bright and early the next morning, despatches began to come and go every hour. Many of them were in code. An air of feverish activity and mystery enveloped the ranch house with that endless flow of telegrams and telephone calls. Capable looking men came, were closeted with John and his uncle for a short time, then hurrying to their waiting automobiles or aeroplanes, soberly entered and sped away in a cloud of dust. 

One week after Uncle Richard's arrival at the ranch, the following notices appeared in the daily papers of the larger cities of both continents. "Men with World War flying experience, and other licensed pilots, will find it to their advantage to call at once, and ask for 'Aviation' at the following address."

The address in each and every city where such a notice appeared was in some centrally located building. Next, the large aeroplane stocks of Europe and America were depleted of planes of certain designs. Many aero factories began to hire extra hands and hum with an air of prosperity. 

For a while, this state of affairs went on unnoticed, but gradually all kinds of rumors began to circulate about the mysterious activity. Reporters, on failing to get all that they wanted in the way of information at the various factories, in many instances called upon their imagination and began to predict another world war.

Others contended it was the approaching "end of the world" heralded by the prophecy of the "war of the races.' 

Yellow journals began to let the public in on the general nature of all the known and whispered new engines of destruction, the strategic position of the numerous coaling stations of the various powers that would be involved in this greatest of conflicts, and their respective naval strength based on their tonnage, battle units and gun-fire. The number of men that each of the opposing nations could mobilize and the amount they could at once put in the field under arms was also enumerated.

Other periodicals stated that a number of international multi-millionaires had created a fund amounting to one billion dollars, in order to equip the young men of all the nations of the Caucasian race with aircraft, which in turn was to be sold to them at cost, on an easy payment plan. 

The prime object of this amazing stunt was for the purpose of making the white race invincible in the air in time of war. Other reports said that the American bankers were financing another revolution in Central America, in order to corral still more of its mineral wealth, trade and economic life. 

Getting the Vickers machines, Lewis and other rapidfire guns, together with the necessary enormous tonnage of bombs and ammunition for all these planes, caused considerable trouble and delay. But in divers ways it was accomplished without undue publicity. 

In several European countries, training flying-fields were acquired under names of different firms, and put under the jurisdiction of ex-service men of wide experience and unquestionable ability.

Almost daily, large consignments of aeroplanes, machine guns and ammunition, arrived at the various flying fields. 

In remarkably short order, a swarm of mechanics removed the planes from their crates and assembled them. 

As soon as conditioned, the planes were at once apportioned to the waiting list of accepted pilots, with orders to pitch in and pile up their flying hours, in order to acquire the highest possible flying efficiency.

Twice a day at different periods under the direction of able commanders, different squadrons participated in battle maneuvers.

CHAPTER XXI. 

SHORTLY after daybreak, almost three weeks after the assassination of Jules Le Roy, Ismar's aeroplane alighted in a small, obscure clearing several miles from Kalasin. His long delay was caused by a blown-out piston head while still in the Canadian wilds.

The princess, in a long cloak which entirely enveloped her, surmounted by a veil hiding her features, was hurriedly escorted to the limousine that during the plane's absence was always parked in a camouflaged hangar. 

Traveling rapidly, five minutes elapsed before the car crossed a drawbridge spanning a deep chasm. The bridge guarded the only avenue of approach to the isolated country estate of the High Priest of Dovette.

As the car stopped before this picturesque, yet formidable appearing habitation, perched upon the summit of a rock-strewn mound, Ismar alighted, and conducted a veiled lady, Princess Clarita, through the door. No one was seen about at that early hour, excepting the two gardeners of the place a middle-aged man and his assistant, a much younger one.

Unnoticed, the latter was a much interested observer of 

Ismar and his several companions.

As the door closed behind them, the assistant gardener threw down his hoe and walking around to the back of the house, he entered the basement. For a few minutes, in the gloom of the cellar, he stood perfectly still, watching the entrance through which he had come. Satisfied that he was not followed, he pulled out a pocket flashlight and cast its rays against the farther wall. The circle of light fell upon a small, heavy, oaken door. 

Opening it, he stepped quickly inside and then closed it behind him. A flight of steps stood directly before him. 

These, he now silently mounted, after first removing his shoes. 129

Arriving at the top of the stairway, he heard the murmur of voices. Applying his ear close to the intervening partition, a man's voice was heard saying: "Please be seated, Princess." "Ismar," said a woman's voice, which the hidden listener correctly surmised was that of Princess Clarita, "tell me, why have you brought me here?" "For your own safety, my dear." "I fail to see how that can be," replied the princess, "and I shall thank you for the use of your telephone to notify His Majesty of my safe arrival here." "Sorry, princess, that I cannot grant you so small a request, just at this moment." "And pray, why not?" "For reasons of state, I cannot answer you now-but 

I beg you to trust and believe in me to have patience." "Do you mean that I am to be a prisoner here?” indignantly inquired the princess. "No! No! Not at all, Princess. Let us say, a temporary guest, for only a few days more or less." "But this is an outrage!" cried the bewildered princess— "one that will cost you your head when my royal father, the King, is made aware of it!" "Your royal father, Princess, will be fortunate if he saves his own head!" retorted Ismar, somewhat sneeringly. "You alarm me, sir," replied the startled princess, "with your evil insinuations. I fain would not believe you. Fór some unknown purpose, you falsify to frighten me." "Nay-nay," replied he brusquely, and then added thoughtfully-"perhaps it will be better, much better to tell you. Know you then, Princess, that a state of war is about to be declared by Atlanta against the kingdom of Dovette. "The armies of King Modoc for weeks have been intrenched on the borders of Dovette, awaiting the word to over-run us. Even now, your father is making preparations for his departure and abdication. Your appearance on the scene at this time would be a great misfortune." "Why should this be so? Conditions were all one could wish at the time of my disappearance."

"Your absence is the exact reason for the present state of affairs. Believing that the rumor of your abduction but a pretense to abrogate the consummation of your marriage to Crown Prince Sigismund, your disappearance aroused 

King Modoc's ire." "Then my re-appearance and acquiescence to marry 

Prince Sigismund would terminate the impending dangers threatening my father's kingdom!" "Yes and no, my dear Princess.” "Verily, sir, I fail to fathom your observations. Wilt thou be more to the point, and explain clearly?" "Princess, I again have to ask your forbearance, inasmuch as my explanation will appear preposterous to you in your present state of mind. Through channels I may not divulge even to you, I have learned that your contemplated union with Crown Prince Sigismund would not dissipate, but only postpone, the inevitable strife and bloodshed of your father's loyal subjects." "You still bewilder me by your incomprehensible information." "The anxiety and perplexity of your Highness is no more than natural," vouched the High Priest, blandly. "Have patience and hear me through."

Upon her nod for him to proceed, and satisfied that he now had her fixed attention, he continued: "The sole, sinister purpose of your betrothal to Prince

Sigismund was to secure to King Modoc your father's crown, and make the fair land of Dovette a vassal state to the opulent kingdom of Atlanta." "Your tidings are gruesome, if true, and for that very reason, methinks His Majesty should be at once informed as to King Modoc's real purpose, also as to my presence here." "That I will gladly do, but all in good time, all in good time." "But I insist it be done as speedily as possible that it be done at once." "Your father, dear Princess, is not now in a position to decide for his best interests. When I feel that he is, I shall cheerfully do so."

"What makes you appoint yourself custodian of His 

Majesty's future?" asked the princess, sarcastically. "My loyalty to my country, the Kingdom of Dovette," replied he, maliciously. "What, then, may your pleasure be regarding my person during the interval?" inquired she, now convinced of his treachery. "During this turbulent situation, you are to remain here. 

I propose to keep you until I can either safely present you to your father, or arrange for your escape into neutral territory. For reasons that I cannot mention at this time, Princess, you must not be seen.” "I still do not understand the necessity-the logic of your deductions, and I think I have grave and just grounds to believe you are deceiving me. However, I am in your power, and for the present at least, must abide by your wishes." "Thanks, Princess," said Ismar suavely, "you certainly are sensible-sensible far beyond your years." Then, bowing himself out, he left her to her thoughts.

Upon hearing him turn the key in the lock, she glanced at the windows and saw that they were all heavily barred. 

A sob escaped her as she, with tottering limbs, staggered to the couch at one corner of the room, and weeping, sank upon it.

The new assistant gardener, listening on the other side of the partition, stealthily retraced his steps, put on his shoes, and upon emerging from the basement, picked up his hoe and resumed his work. That night, another code message from Holland to John Desmond, gave a full report of what he had seen and heard.

CHAPTER XXII. 

IN answer to a summons from Ismar, Esther Neilson arrived just before daybreak at the Chateau Minerva. The 

High Priest was up to receive her. First solicitously helping her to remove her wraps, he led her to his study, sat down beside her on the couch, and said: "Esther, I sent for you because the princess is now here and I want you to watch and attend her." "Oh! I was so glad to learn you had returned. I was dreadfully worried. What delayed you so?" "I had some engine trouble. It delayed me almost three weeks."

Ismar then related to her the incidents of his trip to California and return. He omitted all reference to his villainy in the premeditated assassination of Le Roy and

Mattli, and concluded his tale with the words, "It is just as well. Alive, they might have been a constant source of trouble." "Now, my dear," ventured the High Priest, "things so far have been all I could desire. Barring a slip-up, you may yet wear a crown. "Here is the key to the chamber in which the princess is now confined. I am consigning her to your keeping. 

Treat her kindly and make her as comfortable as possible, but under no circumstances permit her to escape. Give her no opportunity to write or to make herself known. 

Should she accomplish either before we are ready, both you and I will lose our heads. I think that will be all for tonight. You must be quite tired from your journey over the abominable roads to this place?" "Not so tired as sleepy," answered Esther. "Then you had better go along to your room and take a nap. I will call you for breakfast." "Aren't you tired, too, Ismar?” 133

"No, my dear, I had a few hours sleep on the couch while waiting for you. Besides, I wish to compose a letter to King Modoc." 

He then arose and accompanied her to the door. In starting to retrace his steps, something white peeping out from beneath the couch arrested his attention. He picked it up. It was a letter. The seal had been broken. It was addressed to Esther Neilson. Ismar withdrew the enclosure, sat down at his desk, and began to read. 

As he read the perplexed wrinkling of his brow gave way to an angry frown. It ran as follows: "My dearest girl:

It is now many years since you left here. My wife has since died. I wonder if you still think of me. 

Dear, I seem to want you more and more all the time since you went away. I only wish that each time I think of you, the thought would turn into a letter, then you would hear from me every hour of the day and night.

I even sing about you when walking through the fields. 

Please don't laugh at this. 

I hope the day will come when I can join you there. 

Do write soon and let me know if I may come to you. 

Yours everlastingly,

H. H. DU BOISE." 

On completion, he turned back and read it all over again. 

Suddenly reaching forward he pressed a button. Esther 

Neilson answered the summons. Motioning her to the empty chair beside him, he silently handed her the open letter. As she scanned the first lines, she grew deathly pale and the letter dropped into her lap. "Do you care to make an explanation, Madam?" asked 

Ismar, in a soft, deadly tone of voice. 

As she made no attempt to speak, he asked harshly, "Well, are you tongue-tied?" "Wha-what do you wish to know?" "Who is this man?" "One of my former employers." "What else is he to you?" "Nothing but a friend." "So his signature would imply," this dryly.

"Where is he now?" "In France." "When did you see him last?" "Not since I left his employ, many years ago." "Been corresponding?" "No." "You once were sweethearts?" "Yes, I suppose so. We kept company before he married." "Why did he not marry you?" "I did not care enough to do so." "The letter is not dated. When did you receive it?" "Just as I was leaving to come here." "How did he know your address?" "I suppose he got it from my aunt, back there." "I am inclined to believe it to be as you state, nevertheless! This this letter gave me quite a shock. Whatever possessed him to write you after all these years?" "I really don't know. I gave him no encouragement and 

I do not see what makes you talk to me that way, Ismar. 

I swear to you he is nothing to me." 

Then as the tears rolled down her cheeks, she sobbed, "I think you you sometimes hate me--you are so horrid." "There, there now, my dear," said he soothingly, "I did not mean to hurt you. I am not blaming you for receiving an unwarranted letter. So now run along and take your nap." "I-I can't sleep now," said Esther in a tired, listless voice. "Even so-to lie down will be restful."

As the woman arose and would have kissed him, he waved her away with the words: "No, Esther, not now, please go I am in no mood for it."

As the draped door closed after his worried housekeeper, the High Priest's brain was already trying to devise some way of getting rid of her.

Ismar's dreams of the future-visualizing a jeweled crown, purple robes and ermines, did not include his housekeeper as Queen of Dovette.

On the third day after Esther's arrival at the Chateau Minerva, a letter was delivered by mail to King Modoc at his Army Headquarters in the captured city of Arcadia. 

King Modoc read it and then laughingly gave it to the 

Press. The letter follows: 

"To His Majesty, King Modoc : 

I am pleased to inform you that Princess Clarita has been found. She is alive and in good health. I was not one of her abductors. They died in trying to escape. 

On condition that you give me your support to sit upon the throne of Dovette, I will at once deliver to you, Princess Clarita of the Kingdom of Dovette. If you accept, inform me through the columns of the Press, promise immunity to my person and I will call to see you.

HER RESCUER." 

The publication of this amazing document caused quite an uproar among the masses. But what could they do? 

They were under the yoke and subservient to the will of their conqueror, King Modoc.

On the other hand, Prince Sigismund, who, in his own degenerate way, was as fond of Princess Clarita as one whose passionate nature could possibly be for something not easily obtainable, finally prevailed upon his father, King Modoc, to grant the unknown's request, with the significant words, "Dovette can't run away, father. There is always a way."

Three days afterwards, the front pages of the daily morning newspapers printed King Modoc's acceptance with the command that the Rescuer call upon him without delay.

Upon that same morning, a few hours before Ismar arose from a troubled night's sleep, one of the newspapers was forced through a barred window of the room in which the princess was confined. She found it on the floor, upon arising. The news surprised and distressed her, and her agitation, threatening to end in hysterics, was only held in check by a note which was found in one of the folds. It read:

"DEAR PRINCESS: 

DO NOT BE ALARMED, KEEP UP YOUR COUR- AGE. GOOD FRIENDS ARE WATCHING OVER YOU. DON'T TRUST ISMAR. HE IS A TRAITOR TO DOVETTE. CHEER UP. YOUR DELIVERANCE IS ONLY A MATTER OF DAYS. BE SURE TO DE- STROY THIS AT ONCE." 

She was re-reading it, when she heard someone at the door. As quickly as possible, the princess tucked the papers under her pillow and with a smile turned to greet Esther Neilson, who was now entering with the breakfast tray. "Good morning, Princess! How well you look today! 

Are you feeling as well as the roses in your cheeks would indicate?" she inquired rather kindly. At heart, Esther

Neilson was not bad-she had erred, and was still leading an unenviable life, whose only ray of sunshine was her great and sincere love for Ismar, the High Priest of Dovette. "Thank you, Madame," smiled the princess. "I am quite well, although, I confess, this confinement is irksome." "Princess, I am sorry-very sorry, and sympathize with you. I gladly would take you out for a walk, were it not that my orders to the contrary are very explicit.”

With these words, her eyes swept the bed-chamber and inadvertently rested upon the end of a newspaper that projected from beneath the bed-pillow. Princess Clarita, in her haste, had not been able to completely hide it. 

Striding over to the bed, Esther withdrew it. An exclamation of apprehension escaped her as she noted what its presence might portend. Turning to the princess, she asked: "Where did you get this?" Receiving no answer she repeated: "Princess, who gave you this paper? Unless you give me a satisfactory answer, I shall have to report it!" "No one gave it to me, Madam. It was upon my chamber floor when I arose." 

For almost a minute, Esther Neilson looked at her fixedly. Then, with a muttered remark to herself, which 

Princess Clarita could not understand, she hurriedly left the room, locking the door behind her.

CHAPTER XXIII. 

HALF an hour after Esther had left, Princess Clarita saw

Ismar beneath her window, examining and measuring the imprints of a man's foot in the soft soil. Close beside him, whispering to him now and then, was Esther Neilson. 

Several minutes after, James Holland was suddenly set upon by Ismars' two hirelings, Andre and Cyril. His hands were securely bound behind him, and then, with a man on either side of him, he was marched into Ismar's study. The first thing he saw on entering was Ismar's death-like paleness and malevolent look.

Motioning his men to withdraw, Ismar with a wave of his hand directed the still bound James Holland to a chair directly opposite. No sooner seated than in surprisingly good English, the High Priest asked: "Well, what have you to say for yourself?" "What do you mean, sir?" inquired Holland. "Please don't dissimulate. You know what I am after.

I want to know who you are, where you come from, and the object of your spying upon me." "I don't follow you, sir," said Holland, all the while trying to collect his thoughts, and sum up the situation. "I'm afraid you will have to be a little more explicit." "What do you hope to gain by the preamble?" asked 

Ismar irritably. 

Receiving no reply he continued: "What is your name?" "James Holland." "Your nationality-American, is it not?" "By adoption only. My mother was German-my father, French." "Where were you born?" "In Argentina." "How long have you been working for me?" 138

"About two weeks." "What made you come here?" "I came to recuperate." "What ails you?" "Heart trouble." "You don't look to be so afflicted!" "That is because I have angina pectoris. Sufferers from such seldom look ill." "How long do you expect to stay?" "Until I feel better." "You came direct from America, to Kasalin?" "No, sir, I came from England. Lived there two years." "How did you come to pick out this particular spot?" "Well, sir, that same question might apply to many fellows in other places, under similar circumstances. I picked it at random." "What made you apply here for work?" "I had no definite object other than the need of doing something. I wished to conserve my funds, sir." "So I am to understand that you came here, a thousand miles away, to spend your vacation in an unknown town, with a rather limited purse?" "Yes, sir, if you could call it a vacation." "Do you know the identity of the lady confined here in the east wing?" "No, sir." "Why are you so interested in her, then?" "Did not know that I was, sir." "Then you know there is a woman here, besides the housekeeper?" "Yes, sir." "How did you know?" "I saw her with you the morning she arrived." "Why did you give her this newspaper?" inquired Ismar, opening the drawer in his desk and showing Holland the same paper he had thrown through the barred window of the room in which the princess was confined. "I never saw the paper before." "Ever see this slip of paper?" shot out Ismar, showing him the note he had written.

"No, sir, I did not." "Is this not your handwriting?" "No, it is not, but the handwriting is very much like mine-in fact, it is almost identical." "Young man," said Ismar, with some asperity, "you are exceptionally adroit, but it will avail you naught."

Bending down, he picked up and brought to view, a pair of muddy shoes. "Are these your shoes?" 

For a moment Holland thought of denying ownership. "Yes, sir," was his confirmation, "they are mine." "Have you any other shoes here?" "Only what I am now wearing." "Then tell me," continued Ismar, "what occasioned the necessity on your part to stand directly beneath the barred window through which this paper was thrown?" "I'm sure I don't get you, sir. There are other men stopping here." "Quite right-so give me all your attention. Allow me to inform you, Mr. Holland, that this pair of shoes exactly fits the footprints made in the soft soil beneath her window. Do you deny making the impressions?" "No, sir, I admit I may have made them." "Then will you kindly inform me as to why you were standing beneath that window?" "It is one of my duties to care for the flowering shrubs adjacent to the house, sir, and as the beds are deep, I am often compelled to step upon them." 

Sighing, Ismar shook his head and said: "Too bad you will have to die and you are such a bright chap." With hands clasped before him on his desk, Ismar sat looking at Holland intensely. But it was of short duration. The frown upon his face gave way to a smile. "Young man, I don't mind telling you that your personality and nerve appeal to me. I am disposed to spare you. 

I know I could use you to advantage in my organization. 

With my friendship, you could advance in life and prosper. 

How would you like to enter my employ?" 

For a moment, Holland almost forgot himself, almost

blundered at the bait, on the impulse to gain his questioner's confidence. "I am already in your employ, sir." "Furies take you!" was Ismar's exasperated ejaculation, "you will insist on trifling. Once again, I ask you to make a clean breast of everything-the reason for your presence here, and the names and object of the people behind you." 

As his listener remained silent, Ismar sat back in his chair and deliberately articulating each word, said in cold, monotonous voice: "Far below the street level of my abode in Arcadia, is a large, sound-proof room in which numerous persuasive contrivances are scattered about. Tonight you will be brought there. If your stamina equals your present stubbornness, I am in line to witness a very delightful spectacle, when we meet again. Until then, enjoy life as much as you can, and make your peace with God or with Satan, because it is destined you are to shortly visit one of them!" "Are you threatening me, sir?" "No, just making a concrete statement!" "Am I to understand, you intend to murder me?" "That depends upon you-will you talk-confess?" "I am an American citizen and demand to see the American Consul! I have nothing to confess." "You cannot see him. But I wish to assure you that to-morrow night you will be free to call upon him--from the other world," added Ismar with a sardonic grin.

The High Priest thereupon summoned Giggi and ordered him to send in Andre and Cyril. Upon their appearance, pointing to Holland, he said: "Take charge of this man, and guard him well. At midnight remove him to my home in Arcadia. Give him naught but bread and water. Chain him to the wall of my persuasive gallery, and let him enjoy my exhibit of medieval relics till I arrive."

CHAPTER XXIV. 

As Cyril opened the door for the others to precede him, he almost collided with a man about to enter. "I beg pardon, sir," said the man, addressing Cyril, “I did not know there was anyone in here." "What do you wish? Who are you?" asked Ismar, in Dovettian.

The stranger was smooth-shaven, young, clean-cut and self-possessed. Linguist Henry, as he had been known at college, because of his ability to speak several languages fluently, now replied, also in Dovettian: "I am the telephone man."

It was with difficulty that the new arrival refrained from betraying his acquaintance with James Holland. 

Holland in turn smothered the glad cry on the way to his lips, upon recognizing the man on the threshold, with kit of tools, dressed in the uniform of a telephone repairman, as his old friend and pal, Henry Higginson. According to a prearranged plan, Holland did not expect his pal till the following day. 

At Henry's words, Ismar dismissed the others. As the door closed behind the trio, the High Priest, who had arisen on Henry's entrance, now again sat down, and asked: "Why did you come here?" "There is trouble on your telephone line, and I have been sent to locate it." "I think you are mistaken," was the prompt response. "I have used it twice today, and it worked perfectly." "You cannot mean recently, sir! Your line has been dead for over an hour."

For answer, Ismar picked up the receiver from the instrument on his desk, and tried to get a connection. After repeated attempts, he turned to Henry (who had all the while been thanking his stars that he had had the foresight 142

to cut Ismar's private wire as it came through a wash, over a mile away) with the remark: "All right, young man, go right ahead and repair it as soon as possible. I may have urgent need of it."

He thereupon closed his desk, arose, took a general survey of the studio, strode to the door, and silently disappeared.

Henry Higginson stood rigidly in the center of the room, listening to the retreating footsteps. When he could no longer hear them, he tiptoed to the closed door and again listened. Hearing nothing, he picked up a screw-driver, opened the door and stepped boldly out into the hall. No one was in sight. With a quick, stealthy movement, he stepped back into the room and gently closed the door. 

Opening his repair kit, he drew forth a peculiar little round, box-like affair, which he fastened behind a life-size painting of King Augustus that hung on the farther wall. 

Then the two thread-like wires attached to it were cleverly concealed as he laid them on the upper side of the molding that led to a mahogany paneled side wall in the dark corner of the study.

With a small hand-drill, he bored a tiny hole in one of the panels, and passed the wires through. It was then, on straightening up, that an observer could have seen he was under a mental strain, was working against time. 

With a trembling hand, Henry Higginson wiped the perspiration from his brow, for well he knew that, to be discovered while installing a dictograph in Ismar's study, meant his untimely end at the hands of the master of the house.

Passing the wires through another hole he had bored in the ceiling, they emerged in the closet of Holland's bedroom on the floor above.

With an unconcern he was far from feeling, upon the pretense of testing the different telephone extensions and wires, he went all over the house and grounds, looking for 

Holland. Approaching the room wherein Princess Clarita was confined, his further progress was blocked by a burly individual, who, rising from the chair in which he had been dozing, asked:

"What's your hurry, boy? What do you want here?" "I am looking for the trouble on your wire. Telephone out of order. Please let me pass." "Sorry, son-my orders are to let no one pass. Better see the boss and have him tell me." "All right, I will do so," promised Henry, "if I fail to locate it elsewhere."

Chagrined and baffled, Henry went back to Holland's room. What should he do next? He felt there was some good reason for the guard being placed where he was. Could it be that the princess, whom Holland and he had been sent thousands of miles to find, was imprisoned in that part of the house? If so, Holland must know something about it. Where in the devil was he! Unfortunately, to ward off any suspicion of their being acquainted, Henry had deliberately turned his gaze away from his pal at their meeting, and had refrained from again looking at him. 

And just because he did so, he had failed to see his comrade's hands were tied behind him.

Henry felt he must have a talk with Holland. He tried to think of some way in which this could be accomplished. 

While walking back and forth, looking first at one object and then another, his glance rested upon the bed. 

Grabbing a paper wrapper he saw lying on the dresser, he wrote on one corner of it as follows: 

"I PUT THE DICTOGRAPH RECEIVER IN YOUR CLOSET, WOULD LIKE TO SEE YOU. CAN FIND ME IN LODGINGS OVER TOWN MARKET, IN VILLAGE. 

HENRY."

Then walking over to the bed, he placed the paper between the sheets, feeling sure that on going to bed his pal would find and read the penciled message. Henry did not know that his pal would never again enter that room. 

In the hallway, he ran into Esther Neilson. The unexpected collision flustered Henry, temporarily. Esther acknowledged his apology with a pleasant nod. "May I ask what you are doing in that room?" she inquired.

"I am the telephone man. Your telephone is out of order, and I am trying to locate the trouble." "But you will never find it in there. That room has neither an extension nor wiring." "That I did not know, Madame, until I investigated." "I suppose it is now my turn to apologize," responded 

Esther with a smile. "Have you discovered the trouble?" "Yes, Madame, insofar as knowing it is not on the inside. It is somewhere between here and the village," was 

Henry's rejoinder.

With a bow and an inward sigh of relief, he then shouldered the kit he had been carrying under his arm, and walked away. All the way along the hall, until he reached the head of the stairs and disappeared from view, Esther stood where he had left her, thoughtfully watching his receding form.

Esther Neilson was, in many respects, a clever woman. 

Habit, as well as a streak of inquisitiveness, now caused her to open the bedroom door and look in. Her scrutiny of the interior showed everything in its proper place all but the wrinkle in the bedspread. Esther was an exacting housekeeper-one that fidgets at things that are not just so-so she immediately entered to smooth it out. 

Running her hand over the spread with that object in view, the rustle of the hidden paper arrested her attention. 

With a start, she jerked off the bed covering, exposing the paper to view. Hurriedly reading Henry's penciled note about the instrument in the closet, she rushed out to give it to her master. Half way down the hall, the impulse suddenly waned. She stopped, hesitated, and then quickly retraced her steps. In the center of the room she again stopped and hesitated. But the innate curiosity of the female ran true to form in Esther Neilson. Entering the closet, she discovered the receiver. Taking it off the hook, she placed it to her ear. Suddenly, she reached out behind her and grasped the door knob. Pulling the door shut so that anyone entering the bedchamber would fail to see her in the closet, she again put the receiver to her ear. "I depend upon you!" she heard the familiar voice of the 

High Priest-the man she idolized-admonish someone

else, "to watch Madame Neilson, as well as the princess, while I am away. I do not trust Madame as much as I have in the past." "Why?" asked a man's voice which she knew belonged to

Boris Zanzee--one of the Temple priests. "Something has come to my attention that leads me to believe she is infatuated with an acquaintance, and both may soon leave for parts unknown. Of course, I have no proof; in fact, there may be nothing to it; still, her disappearance would not surprise me." 'Does she know about the Brotherhood's plans and preparations to secure the throne?" inquired Zanzee. "Yes, of course-how could I have kept her from knowing? She has charge of all my records and correspondence." "Would it be safe to let her go, Ismar, before we are ready to act?" "That is something to which I have already given considerable thought." "As one of the Brotherhood, may I ask you a personal question?" "Most assuredly you may, and I will promise you an answer, if it is possible." "Well, then, I would ask you how much do you really like Madame Neilson? If what you heard should turn out to be untrue, would you marry her when you become king?" "Of course not!-how could I? She is not of the nobility." "But you as king could first ennoble her." "Yes, I could do that, but it would be preposterous!" "Why so?" "Because while Madame Neilson is a fine woman in many respects, she is now my housekeeper, and, in the eyes of the people would always be so. They would never accept her as Queen. Besides, how could I marry her then, if I did not care to do so now? Would you have the king crown the woman whom the High Priest retained as housekeeper?" "I mean no offense, sir. The Brotherhood also feels that it would be a mistake for you to marry her. Your subjects

might never accept her as queen. She would be out of place at court." "Oh, pshaw! don't worry any more about it. But I don't mind telling you I liked Esther-liked her very much, the first few years. I suppose her cheerfulness and minute attentions for my comfort, carried me off my feet.

But in all women, as well as most men, time changes their tastes and devotions." "I would not be too sure about her loving someone else," said Zanzee thoughtfully-"in fact, I can hardly conceive it." "And why not?" asked Ismar. "Because she seems to worship the very ground you walk on." "Which does not always signify the true state of affairs, with the female of the species." 

Then, putting his hand on Zanzee's arm, Ismar continued, with a cynical smile: "The ways of women are complex-unfathomable, Boris. 

You will find that out some day; we all do." "Still, you may have a hard time getting rid of her." "Fie, man! You magnify things! I have always been careful; and you can rest assured that when I decide to drop her, there will be neither scandal nor comeback," said he significantly. "Ismar," the agonized listening woman heard Zanzee say, "it really is a pity. Madame has always been so thoughtful and considerate of all those about her it is really too bad, but then-what is even a dozen such lives compared to the stakes involved!" "Very little, Cyril, in fact, infinitesimal," was Ismar's decided reply. "Have you any idea as to just when she is liable to go away with this-this other man?" "No, I have not. And I have been thinking it is dangerous to wait until it is too late." "You mean that you would—” "I really don't know," answered the High Priest of 

Dovette. "Come, Boris, let us take a walk, the air will do us good."

Following this last remark, the listening woman heard the closing of a door as the two scoundrels left the room. 

With a moan that came from the bottom of a broken, disillusioned heart, Esther Neilson's knees gave way beneath her as she collapsed and sank into a huddled heap, on the closet floor.

CHAPTER XXV. 

FOR almost an hour, Esther Neilson lay as if dead. On recovering consciousness, she rearranged her disordered clothes and hastily left the chamber. Locking herself in her own room, she sank upon the carpeted floor, to think-to think and ponder, in order to avert the drag-net that was reaching out to ensnare and destroy her. 

In the seclusion of her room, Esther went to her dresser and from a little rosewood box, removed a faded letter. It was written in a bold hand and over a decade before. It was from Ismar, now the High Priest of Dovette. With the open letter in her hands, her thoughts raced back to the scenes of her girlhood, back to the old vine-clad cottage, the house in which she first saw the light of day. What a fool she had been to exchange the dear, dreamy, happy home and town of her young womanhood for the noisy, sordid glamor of a city's confusion!

What a fool she was to linger on from year to year, trusting, hoping and waiting for what?

The High Priest would never wed her. Of this, she was now convinced. What a fool she had been-what a fool!

Opening the letter, she again read it, even though she knew it by heart. It read: "Dear Esther: 

I enjoyed your letter very much, and read much between the lines. Before long, I expect to be in line for the service of a woman, so, if you then are willing, you might make me a good housekeeper. I believe you would do. We could get along fine. I am sure of it. 

You see, a priest likes to have as housekeeper someone who is devoted enough to him, that she will stick to him and work for him. 

However, my needs are not pressing, so in a year or two, if you are willing, we may give it a try. 149

I realize that a lot may happen between now and then, but just the same it is nice to think of. 

Most cordially yours,

ENOS ISMAR." 

And one year afterwards, she had bade good-bye to her folks and to the home of her birth and had gone to him. 

And she had gone willingly, not because of the wages offered, but because she believed to serve a priest was next to serving God. What a fool she had been, murmured she to herself, as she crumpled the letter in her hand. She had read the letter over and over times innumerable, and she had always failed to read between the lines. But she was now awake to the full realization of the High Priest's duplicity. Ismar had always been a Judas to her in return for the best years of her life-the life she had spent in her loving service of toil, devotion and sin. . .

She had been in Ismar's house only three short weeks, when she realized she must choose between her church and the devil-between her position as housekeeper or a woman out of employment. And she had stayed-had submitted to his demands, upon his promise to make her his wife as soon as he could get things properly arranged, as soon as he could get the king's consent.

He had swept aside her scruples and repugnance to surrender to his gratifications, on the statement that, as a priest-as High Priest of Dovette, their intimacy was not by half so sinful as when carried on among laymen-in fact, it could hardly be called sinful, in view of the extenuating circumstances, where it required the sanction of King 

Augustus before a Temple Priest could marry.

Trusting and believing in him, and his annual statements that King Augustus was not inclined to give his consent to their union this year, but might be prevailed upon to do so in the following one, she now realized she had given everything, missed everything, and in return had received nothing but tolerance and hard work. Work that entailed long hours in the filing and care of numerous letters, pamphlets, secret reports and other things, things that frayed her nerves and scorched her very soul.

Had she ever been presented at court, which, as his fiancee, would have been her due? Had he ever taken her to the cinema or theatre, to a picnic, party or wedding, to the races and other gatherings, all of which he was continually attending? No-to be seen with his housekeeper would be scandalous-it would cause people to talk--to suspect an intimacy, and destroy his prestige. 

Did any of his friends ever speak to her as an equal? 

Perish the thought! How could they, when even the most charitable, believing the worst and overlooking it, still remembered she was a housekeeper, a servant, his paid menial.

And then-there was her baby-the baby who had been torn from her arms as its little dimpled hands clutched into space. Just two weeks after its birth, her tiny baby girl had been spirited away, to be brought up by strangers. 

Just once a year, on Christmas Day, was she allowed to see her child. In the home where the little one was being indifferently brought up and likewise educated, her mother heart yearned and bled.

Twelve years had come and gone since the child's coming, years of hardships, expectations and disappointments. 

Every year in the most convincing manner, Ismar had promised, and she had believed he would marry her the following spring. And now, she must constantly be on guard, for her very life was threatened.

But, to be forewarned was to be forearmed. And with the help of the hidden instrument in the clothes closet, she would elude the baited trap, until such time as she could make arrangements to fly and take her child with her. She thanked God for her discovery of the dictograph and for the impulse to listen in.

CHAPTER XXVI. 

THAT same evening, upon entering the dining room, she was pleasantly greeted by the High Priest. The dinner, though exceptionally good, went along quietly, almost silently, until the appearance of the dessert. "My dear," spoke up Ismar, as the coffee was being served, "matters that you would not understand, will necessitate my taking precautions for an immediate flight." 

Receiving no reply, he continued: "I have decided to have you leave here tomorrow. Without you to look after and worry about, my anticipated flight, should it become necessary, would be considerably simplified.
