Therefore, I have made arrangements to have you leave here secretly at midnight for a place where no one would ever dream of finding you." 

At her startled look, he hastened to add: "At the first opportunity, I will send for you. And when

I do, our long deferred marriage shall be consummated, because I and not Augustus, shall then be king." "What is to become of me if you find you have to flee?" "I shall either join you there or send word where you are to meet me. Then in a distant clime, under another name, we shall be always together."

For a moment the poor bewildered woman before him, whom he was so basely deceiving, felt a qualm of remorse, at having misjudged him, but as he leaned over to pat her hand, her confused brain began to clear, to see and to remember the dictograph. "Was it a plot to kill her?" thought she. "No, she would not go, she would not." "Thanks, very much," said she, "but I don't care to go and leave you, at least not right now." "Why, what is to hinder you, Esther?" "I can't tell you. I just don't want to go away from here at present, and I won't." 152

For a moment the High Priest transfixed her with a piercing, angry glare. Slowly, the viciousness died out of his eyes, and his lips twisted into a quizzical smile. "Well, well, Esther, I never thought you cared for me as much as all that. It is gratifying to know that you prefer to risk your life by my side, rather than leave me." 

And as she remained silent, staring blankly at him, she heard him say: "Esther, dear, I have just received a bottle of very old 

Burgundy. We two-just you and I are going to enjoy it."

Going into the next room, he returned with two glasses and a bottle of wine. Removing the cork, he then proceeded to fill both glasses with the deep red liquid. Upon completion he slid one toward her. 

The keen eyes of the suspicious woman had, from the beginning, in silence observed his every move. She, however, had failed to see the moisture that one of the glasses had contained when empty. And even if she had noticed it, she doubtless would not have suspected that the moist film inside of that glass included the deadliest of poisons. 

Watching her closely, Ismar noticed a slight reluctance on her part to drink first. He thereupon raised his own glass to his lips and half drained it. "It is decidedly mellow, and of excellent vintage, my dear," said he, smacking his lips. "Why, Esther, what ails you are you sick?" he asked solicitously, upon noticing her trembling body. "I am not angry at you. Still, if you have changed your mind, your departure would please me." "No, no--I am not ill, Ismar; I'm only worried." "Worried? What about?" "About my having to disappear. Can I take my daughter with me?" "No, not now. Later on we shall all be together-here, or somewhere else. It's a promise." "Will you let me see her, before leaving?" "I don't think it would be safe. You correspond frequently, don't you?" "Yes, I write her once a week." "Very well, write her now and tell her."

"No, I must see her-I cannot go away without her! I will not!"

Now, Ismar, the High Priest of Dovette, while not particular as to one's demise when it contributed to his plans, nevertheless would have preferred to have Esther leave peacefully rather than poison her. He had prepared a place where she could be guarded until such time as he could make a definite decision concerning her future. 

Taking their daughter with her into captivity was not in accordance with his ideas as to the child's future. After all the girl was of his own flesh and blood. Once upon the throne he would look after her properly. But with her mother in the offing, it would not be feasible. Therefore, as long as Esther would not go away alone, she should drink the wine now, and have it over with. "Really, my dear," said he to the listening woman, "inasmuch as you lay so much stress on having her accompany you, I withdraw my objection. When do you wish to go?" "As soon as I can get ready, tomorrow." "Very well, my dear, and now that that is settled, do drink your wine and see if it comes up to my appraisal. To the fulfillment of all our desires," said he, now again raising his glass.

Esther, believing that everything must be all right, was about to do his bidding, when a knock on the door interrupted them. With a gesture of impatience, the High Priest set down his half-filled glass, and stepped across the room to the door. Barely opening it, he asked: "What is it, Giggi? No, tell him I cannot see him. I shall not see anyone until tomorrow. That also applies to you."

Ismar then closed the door and returned to her. He was exceedingly vexed, so much so that even a forced smile failed to obliterate his annoyed expression. "What was it?" inquired Esther. "Nothing of much consequence, my dear," said he, as he glanced at the table and saw only one glass containing wine. Quickly shifting his eyes to look at her again, he realized that she could not be standing there smiling at him if she had partaken of the wine in her own glass. She must

have drunk his wine instead. Why, of course, that was it. 

His glass was empty. Why had she done so? Did she suspect anything? If she did, she was dangerous. It would not do to let her out of that room. If necessary, he would throttle her where she stood.

Turning toward the poor, half-deluded woman, who, in a hazy, despairing sort of way, down in her heart still hungered for his affections, he asked: "Esther, why did you drink my wine instead of your own?" "Because I did not care for so much," said she truthfully. "The wine is good, but really quite strong. Even now, I am beginning to feel the effects."

For a few seconds, he thought she was deceiving him. 

Thoughtfully-intently, he met and held her eyes, eyes that were almost paralyzed with fear, under his concentrating, questioning gaze. "You--you are not angry with me, are you?" she inquired in a quaking voice. "No, of course not!" was the instant retort. "Why should I be angry with you, my dear?" "You-you look at me so fiercely, you-you are so different, you frighten me." "I am sorry to hear you say that. You must go out more. You are a bundle of nerves, and all unstrung. So, tomorrow, my dear, you may go and visit your daughter. If you wish, you may stay with her over night. Upon your return, we will again take up the matter of your safe departure, and finish this bottle of really excellent wine."

CHAPTER XXVII. 

THE next morning, right after breakfast, Henry, dressed in smart tweed strolled over to the drug store, intent on getting into a friendly conversation with the town druggist. He felt he might glean something of importance in connection with his mission if he could get the druggist to divulge the town's gossip.

On stepping across the threshold he saw that besides the clerk the place was deserted. Ordering an ice he sat down at a nearby table.

The proprietor was old, rotund and jovial. It was no task to get him to talk about the town folks. "I hear you have a famous personage here as one of your neighbors," Henry vouched as the druggist stopped for breath, "in His Excellency, the High Priest of Dovette." "Oh! quite right, quite right," answered his listener. "He surely is a great man, a very great man. Kind of uppish and queer, but for all of that, he surely is a great man, a very great man.' 27 "Why queer?" asked Henry.

For a moment his informer stood undecided, then with a shrug, said, "There can really be no harm in telling you, even though His Excellency is a bad man to talk about lightly. "One day about a year ago, he was taking one of his rare walks by here when just right before my door he slipped on a banana peel and almost fell. His antics to regain his equilibrium were such that it caused a small boy to roar out loud. The laugh incensed the High Priest, for he grasped the youngster and cuffed his ears, right smartly. Of course the child howled and fell to the pavement. A crowd gathered. On the point of walking away His Excellency turned about and without looking at the fallen youngster 156

threw a handful of silver coins in his direction as he sauntered away. Now if you don't call that queer, what is?" "Yes, it is rather strange, looks like he is somewhat impulsive.' 

"And oh! I was forgetting about his purchase here yesterday." "Yes," said Henry, attentively. "What do you think of a man of his greatness coming in here just once since I have known of him, and then only to buy rat poison. Yes, rat poison for the rats at the Chateau. It may be so, but I have my doubts. He was a little too cold and kind of reserved like."

Then with a start, the druggist sort of came to, seemed to realize he had talked too much. Turning about abruptly he walked away, with the words, "After all there must be quite a lot of rats there. They are everywhere." "Yes," answered Henry, "and they do a lot of damage."

Arising leisurely he paid his bill and stepped out. Once again on the sidewalk, his wandering gaze was arrested by what seemed to be a familiar figure. A trim looking, slender woman of medium height was just about to cross the street. 

Arriving on the opposite side, he saw her turn and glance at the windows over the market.

Her act intrigued him to the extent of wanting to see her face. The lady entered the depot. On his entering the station, he looked for her in vain. She was not in sight-had disappeared completely. Mystified and undecided as to his next move, he felt something hard hit him square between the shoulders. It almost knocked him over. While trying to recover himself, he heard a woman's voice say: "Oh, I beg your pardon, sir-did I hurt you?" and there coming out of the booth whose out-swinging door had hit him, was Esther Neilson, the woman for whom he was looking. "Why, how do you do!" exclaimed she, speaking in English, and holding out her hand. "It is the telephone man." "Yes, Madame-how do you do," replied Henry, smilingly, grasping the hand offered him. "Are you going away?" "I was, but missed my train, so telephoned for a taxi.”

"And you speak English," quoth Henry, "I'd never have guessed it." "Does that surprise you?" "Yes, somewhat. But what made you think I could speak it also?" "I didn't. At least not till you answered me." "Hem!" said Henry, "you are somewhat of a sleuth. We should become better acquainted." And then at her interrogating look, he asked, "I wonder if you could give me a few minutes of your time, Madame? There is something 

I must say to you-it is something I believe you should know."

As she, undecided, looked queerly at him, he hastened to add: "Please don't misunderstand me. I am serious; and my information is important."

Esther remembered that before her stood the young man who had installed the dictograph that had enabled her to discover the villainy of Ismar. The installation of the instrument, unbeknown to her master, was not a friendly act, therefore, there might be something more to learn to her advantage by assenting to this man's wish. At least, there could be no harm in consenting to listen to what he had to say. "All right, young man. Where shall we go?" "Would a walk to yonder trees be too far?" asked Henry, pointing to a place in a clearing, half a mile away, marked by several giant oaks. 

For answer, Esther Neilson shook her head and said: "No, it is a beautiful day and the walk will do me good."

Henry thereupon offered her his arm. With a faint little smile and nod, Esther accepted the courtesy. Arriving at the rendezvous, she sat down upon a log, and folding her hands in her lap, looked up at Henry, with the words: "Well, sir, I have done my part. What have you to say to me? I confess I am rather curious, or I should have declined your invitation. So don't be timid, for you have already done me a favor," added she, insinuatingly. "Why, what do you mean?" Henry was at a loss to understand the import of her words.

"I found the little arrangement you put in the closet." "Did you tell your employer?" "No, I did not," answered she emphatically. "Ah, then-am I to count on you, as a friend?" "Don't be too hasty, young man, to jump to conclusions.

Just because I kept it from him and am here with you may signify a lot--and then, again, may mean nothing. Real friendship is built on a solid foundation. There must be justifiable circumstances." "Please excuse me," was Henry's rejoinder. “I did not mean to presume. I was just hoping we could work together to save the life of an innocent girl. For that matter, a full report has already gone forward to my superiors, and if anything should happen to her, the High Priest and his accomplices would be hunted down in the four corners of the world, and made to answer for the crime." "This is getting very interesting, but serious, sir. Would you mind telling me why you think the High Priest and others would commit murder, for such, I believe, is your insinuation. Who is the young lady? Why should he wish her harm?" "May I ask you a question?" "Yes, but I do not promise to answer. 

"What was your reason in not telling the High Priest about your discovery of the dictograph?" "What does it matter?" "It would explain a lot to me." "All right, I will tell you. For personal reasons, I intend to make good use of it." "Madam, I am glad to hear you say that, and I hope it will pay you for the trouble it cost me to install it."

Henry thereupon told about Ismar's poison purchase and ended up by saying, "I don't believe he bought it for rats." 

For several minutes, Esther Neilson remained silent. 

From sidelong glances cast in her direction, Henry could see she was deep in thought. At last, with a sigh that was almost a groan, he heard her exclaim: "I see I see it all! My God! He would kill me. He has tired of me. And after all these years!" Then crossing her hands over her breast he heard her murmur, "Blessed

Virgin, guide and watch over me." Turning suddenly, and raising her eyes to Henry: "Where could I again see you?" "I am stopping here, over the town market." "Thanks for all that you told me. I will have to be going now. Let us walk back. No, we had better not be seen together-let us separate here." Extending her hand to him, "I give you freely, the friendship you desire, and don't be alarmed about the death, of-of anybody. I will see to that! Goodby! We shall meet again. Do not try to see me--it's too dangerous." "Very well, Madame, I shall do as you wish, but please won't you first answer a few questions?" As she hesitated, he grasped her elbow and said "Please." "What would you know?" asked Esther. "I would know about Princess Clarita and my friend, Mr. Holland. Is the Princess in the Chateau? Where is Holland?" "I will see you, tomorrow, here at this time. Ask me tomorrow," said Esther.

Without another word, she turned and left him. Upon arriving at the Chateau, she told Ismar that she had missed her train, and because of a severe headache, would postpone seeing her daughter until the following day.

CHAPTER XXVIII. 

JUNE OSTEND still lingered at the Desmond ranch. With all the wiles at her command,-and June was exceptionally well equipped with such-she strove to rekindle John Desmond's interest in her. As the days passed, she had to admit, even to herself, she was slipping more and more—that her cause was getting hopeless.

From the first receipt of definite information regarding his beloved Clarita, John Desmond's thoughts, morning, noon and night, were of her, and her only. To cap the climax, John and his uncle were making preparations to go to Europe.

With a burning rage at the state of affairs, June believed she had been unjustly treated. It instilled in her heart a desire for vengeance. 

Tom Norton was in love with Lillian Douglas. Lillian knew it even before it dawned on Tom, himself. Besides her many lovable and tormenting qualities, Lillian was, by nature, endowed with an abnormal amount of inquisitiveness. It invariably enabled her to secure all kinds of news, without any of her informers harboring the slightest idea that they were being squeezed for information. 

Tom Norton, owing to his family connections and John's sincere friendship, was often present at the numerous conferences now taking place at the ranch between John and his uncle.

Tom Norton could keep a secret when asked, but as no request was ever made of him in this respect, he did not feel bound to refuse to answer Lillian's innocent questions. 

Even then, he might not have said anything, were it not that Tom was in love-head over heels in love with a girl who could wind him around her little finger.

Therefore, without much effort, Lillian learned all about the discovery as to who Clarita really was. Like most of 161

her sex, she could not keep such information to herself. It was too wondrous--epoch-making-alluring. So without delay, she told Mrs. Grant and the Baron all about it, but -in confidence!

Upon receipt of this information, the Baron at sought out June Ostend. Finding her seated all alone beneath a shady tree in a secluded corner of the ranch estate, occupied in reading a book, he sat down quietly beside her, and grinned across into her face as she looked inquiringly at him. "Well, Mr. Sphinx, why all the mystery? You have something on your mind. What is it?" inquired she, laying down her book. "Clarita has been found-at least we know who she is." "Found?" asked June, clutching the Baron's arm. "Is she here?" "No, but we know who kidnapped her, and just about where she is at present. She is a princess of a reigning house-Princess Clarita of the Kingdom of Dovette!" "Ha! Ha!" laughed June. "That certainly was good, Baron-you certainly had me worried for a minute. Dovette-you said Dovette, did you not? Well, I declare,-I never heard of such a place! But it's rich, just the same. 

Really, Reggie, you have a marvelous imagination. You could be wonderfully funny, if you only had the necessary thoughtfulness to make your ideas convincing-they lack foundations." "Perhaps if your geographical knowledge were as broad as your tongue is sharp, you would realize that the joke is on you," said the Baron peevishly. "Oh my! you don't say so. Well, I give up. What's the answer?"

Disdaining to notice her capricious mood, the Baron slowly resumed-slowly, in order to let each word penetrate. "Dovette, though small, is nevertheless an independent kingdom. John Desmond has just had a confirmation from your State Department at Washington, to this effect. Now, if you are really interested, I will tell you all about it-

tell all I have learned," said he, drawing his lips into a severe, straight line. "All right, Reggie, I am listening," said she soberly.

He thereupon told her about the Holland and Higginson mission to Dovette-of their code reports to John. He also told about the kidnappers-who they were and about Rocco Mattli, now stopping at the ranch. Noting her silence upon the termination of the information divulged, he added: "It is a matter of record that scions of royalty have married commoners. What objection could any small kingdom entertain against John Desmond, backed by the tremendous power and prestige of his fabulous wealth? On the other hand, Clarita is of noble birth; loves him, and he is crazy about her. What is there to stop their marriage? 

Now laugh that off, June-laugh it off!" he exclaimed, somewhat gloatingly.

At the termination of his discourse, the receding color in June's cheeks during the recital now left her deathly pale. 

Only the luminous glow of her staring wide-open eyes and agitated breathing, told her male companion that she had heard, understood, was pondering and weighing the situation. "Perhaps," he finally heard her say between lips that barely moved, "there is a way-in fact, I know there is!" 

Catching his glance, June asked: "Reggie, do you love me? Do you still love me?" "Yes, of course, I do-but what has that to do with it?" "When a real man loves," continued June, ignoring his remark, "he will sacrifice a lot-will run risks to please to make his beloved happy." "I'm still listening, June," retorted the Baron, as she paused. "Would you take the risk of incurring John's hate to serve me?" "What would be my reward, June? Would you marry me?" "Of course not! Don't be silly! If I wanted to do that you would be more in line to receive John Desmond's blessing instead of his possible enmity."

"Well, then, what do you wish of me?" inquired the 

Baron, somewhat abashed. "I want you to go to Europe-to this little kingdom you speak of, and tell her father where his daughter is." 

For just a moment, Baron Van Guard stared at the eager, determined face of the girl beside him-then, at the full realization of her words, a look of genuine admiration crossed his features. "By Jupiter, June, you certainly have brains! What a pity you were born a woman. As a statesman, you could have reached the heights." "Why deplore our sex, Baron,-now that we have the vote. Women are even now shaping the country's policy. 

But-thanks just the same. There is really nothing to it. 

It is all so simple. You see, Reggie, when His Majesty finds out where his daughter is, he will at once send a force to get her. When rescued, she will be guarded every minute of the day and night until she's actually married to 

Prince Sigismund. Once the wife of the prince, John's aspirations in that direction would be only a matter of memory." "And that would leave the field clear to you, June," said the Baron. "Yes, Reggie, and it would leave you fifty thousand dollars richer! Don't forget that." "Oh Hell, June! Don't razz me so. What is fifty thousand dollars compared to the loss of you? Besides, you are now adding more work to our original agreement. 

"I will give you fifty thousand dollars extra within the year that John Desmond marries me!" snapped June. "Thanks, June. That makes me a one-hundred-thousanddollar man. Somewhere in this world there must be a girl who for that sum, plus my title, could be made to say 'Yes' to fortune's fool," said he, half banteringly, half dejectedly. "Don't, Reggie-please don't! It makes a delicate situation precarious. You and I could never be happy. You are too domineering-too much like me, in that respect." "Do you think John is a mollycoddle?" "No, John is John, and I am June."

"I fail to get the ensemble, June. Just what do you wish to convey by such an ambiguous remark?" "I'm afraid I could not intelligently explain it to you, at least not at the present moment. Please forget it, and let us get down to the business before us." "That is O. K. with me, June; go ahead with the story." "I wish you would make your excuses here, and leave at once for this little kingdom. Upon your arrival there, you are to inform the king that his daughter is a prisoner, in the hands of the High Priest. I shall advance you five thousand dollars, for expenses. You may have to use part of it to bribe your way to an interview. "Do you realize that it will take me a week to get there, even by air lines?” "Yes, but how can we help it?" "Why not send a cable?" "Would he get it?" "He would if he were there-if he had not abdicated and left the country." "If he had-what then?" asked June. "Well, the chances are the message would be forwarded to him. Anyway, information of such import would not be pigeon-holed. The press would get it, or the authorities in power." "You have just given me an idea, Reggie. Why not send two similar cables? One to King Augustus and one to King Modoc?" "Fine! That surely will bring results. There won't be any need of my going then, will there?" dubiously. "I think you should go, just the same, to guard against a miscarriage of our plans. Upon your arrival, you must secure an interview with one or both of these kings—that is, if you find no action has been taken on account of the cablegrams." "Oh, I forgot to tell you, June; John and his uncle, including this new arrival, Mattli, are leaving tomorrow, for 

Europe. Tom Norton will be left behind to superintend some private business for them, but is to follow in several weeks."

"Then, you had better wait until they go. It will not necessitate making any excuses, nor excite suspicion." "Shall we send the cables to their Majesties today?" "Yes, the sooner the better."

After several attempts, they finally composed and sent the following cable addressed to Augustus, King of Dovette, and to King Modoc, King of Atlanta. 

"PRINCESS CLARITA IS BEING HELD A PRISONER BY ISMAR, HIGH PRIEST OF DOVETTE, AT HIS COUNTRY PLACE NEAR KALASIN. MAKE HASTE IF YOU WISH TO SEE HER ALIVE. IGNORE THIS MESSAGE, AND LIVE TO REGRET IT. 

AN AMERICAN WELL-WISHER."

CHAPTER XXIX. 

IN THE Kingdom of Dovette, the passing of each week added to the general uneasiness of the masses. The country was a seething cauldron of unrest. The chaos of the populace hampered the civil authorities and made lawlessness run rampant, especially in the outlying and rural districts.

Business was practically at a standstill. Property owners were feverishly trying to dispose of their holdings, at sacrifice prices. Moneyed interests were calling in all unextended loans and foreclosing others. All were striving to turn their currency into gold. The run on the banks by the frantic depositors had caused numerous failures, which necessitated the drastic closing of many others. A sixtyday moratorium was put into effect by order of the crown. 

The flow of gold in exchange for the gold certificates of the realm was also stopped by a royal decree.

On the Dovettian border, the army of King Modoc was bivouacked, awaiting the word that would cause them to cross over and over-run the peaceful Kingdom of Dovette.

Rumors of the abuses, which were current, as to the murder, rape, looting and arson of the conquering battalions during the World War, were now making all roads quite impassable by the congestion of all sorts of moving vehicles, piled high with the household goods of Dovettians making a bee-line for points in the interior, or leaving the country entirely.

Such was the state of affairs throughout the realm when the cablegram sent by June and the Baron from America, arrived at the Royal Palace in Arcadia.

It found the court on the verge of leaving. Without delay, the message was turned over to His Majesty, King Augustus. Upon reading it, the king issued orders to hold in abeyance all preparations for their departure. Then, 167

summoning into his presence the Captain of the King's 

Guard-Captain Claudis, he gave him the following instructions: "Captain, I am herewith handing you a warrant to enter the Chateau Minerva at Kalasin. It is the country home of Ismar, the High Priest of Dovette. You will undoubtedly find him there. You will make a thorough search of the Chateau and bring to me, my daughter, the Princess

Clarita, whom I have reason to believe is being held there. 

If you learn she was detained as a prisoner, also bring 

Ismar to me, dead or alive. "It is needless for me to say, Captain, that you are being entrusted with grave responsibilities. In the proper observance of our charge to you lies your country's honor, the lives and hopes of millions, and the happiness and welfare of everything I hold dear and sacred. "When you find the princess, you are to guard her with your life. Take as many men as you desire. Do not fail 

Come back with my daughter, Princess Clarita, and me. you may name your own reward."

Captain Claudis was an old campaigner-a man of intelligence and unquestioned courage. He had entered the service of his country at an early age, under the banners of King Augustus' father.

The Captain was exceedingly fond of his princess, and in this he was not alone. The populace adored her for her angelic disposition, and for her devotion and high regard for the welfare of her father's subjects. Seldom had she failed to stop to say a few kindly words to the grizzled veteran and other servitors of the Royal household, upon her daily walks through the castle grounds. 

As the King concluded, tears welled up into the eyes of this grey-haired warrior, who, now kneeling before his sovereign, kissed the hilt of his sword and vowed: "Sire, with the help of God, your command shall be dispatched as given. I shall leave at once."

Half an hour later, the castle's large bronze gates were opened wide, as the king's own Guard, two hundred grim men, armed and mounted upon wiry, mettlesome steeds,

galloped madly through the mammoth portals upon their errand of deliverance.

Thirty miles away, on the top of a rocky promontory, stood the Chateau Minerva, the country estate of the High Priest of Dovette. The approaches on three sides were inaccessible. The only means of entrance was on the north, over a drawbridge electrically operated, that spanned a chasm over a deep gorge. It was guarded day and night on the Chateau side of the cleft, by three of Ismar's retainers, who divided their time at watching. 

Basil, the bridge-tender, now on duty, was attentively watching the approaching cloud of dust in the distance. 

To his experienced eyes, it denoted a large body of horsemen travelling fast. That they were soldiers, he also soon determined by the forest of glittering spear heads in the setting sun.

Pulling the lever that controlled the operation of the bridge, Basil raised it, and then fled back to the Chateau, to make his report. Ismar, who was leaving for the Capital to arrange the final terms for the delivery of Princess Clarita to King Modoc, met Basil halfway on the road to the bridge. From his breathless retainer he learned of the approaching body of mounted soldiery. 

Now, Ismar was no coward, but a shiver of apprehension, nevertheless, ran up and down his spine at the undesirable news. Finally, pulling himself together, he ordered the chauffeur to drive forward. Arriving at the bridge, he sat in his car, at the chasm's edge, watching with conflicting emotions the rapid advance of the approaching horsemen.

Just as the cavalcade came to within several rods of the upraised bridge, the sharp command to halt, issued by their Captain, brought the forward ranks of foaming horses to a standstill upon their haunches. "Good morning!" shouted Ismar, to Captain Claudis, across the open gap, as the Captain and one of his lieutenants detached themselves from the ranks and rode to the brink. "By what chance am I honored by such a visit?"

"I am upon the King's business," replied Captain Claudis, "and I would converse with you in private."

For a moment, Ismar seemed about to deny the request, but thinking better of it, responded: "I have not quarters for so large a company in my humble abode. Come alone, and I shall be glad to receive you." 

Captain Claudis at once bowed, signifying his consent. "But first, let your men retreat twenty rods farther," cried Ismar.

Turning to his lieutenant, the Captain must have so instructed him, because the officer saluted smartly, wheeled his mount and turned back. On an order from him, the entire column was seen to turn about and gallop back the required distance.

İsmar, who had been nervously watching the maneuver, appeared to be satisfied on its completion. Nevertheless, he beckoned Basil to approach him. "Basil, stand ready to raise the bridge on the first sign of treachery," cautioned Ismar. "I am afraid they will try to rush it." With a nod that he understood, Basil walked back to his post and pulled the lever. 

As the bridge came down just as it fell into place- 

Captain Claudis was seen to jerk his mount to one side.

His purpose for so doing was made apparent by the rapidly ensuing events.

Instantly, as if the Captain's maneuver was a prearranged signal a volley of rifle-fire directed at Basil riddled him at his post. Knowing that the immediate raising of the bridge was now impossible, in the face of the now charging cavalry, Ismar shouted an order that caused the chauffeur to hurriedly turn the machine about. With accelerator down to the floor boards, the car raced back from whence it came.

One of the other bridge-tenders, in the library of the Chateau, attracted by the sound of rifle-fire, rushed over to one of the windows overlooking the bridge. The prostrate body of the riddled bridge-tender met his view. The dead man was his brother, Basil. As Ismar's car was being frantically turned about by the chauffeur, with a cry for

vengeance, Basil's brother ran over to the fireplace and pressed hard against one of the bricks. Instantly, with a roar that shook the Chateau walls, the drawbridge was seen to ascend skyward in a flash of flame.

Between the soldiery on the bridge and those who were unable to check their mounts at the brink, nearly one-half of the king's men and horses perished, through the explosion and by the eight-hundred-foot fall to the bottom of that rocky gorge. Only a dozen had safely made it. 

Amongst them was Captain Claudis, who, gun in hand, had spurred his horse for the bridge at the flash of the rifle's blasts.

The momentary pause of the few who had successfully crossed behind their captain, had enabled both Ismar and his chauffeur to reach the house, to enter and barricade the door.

The Chateau Minerva was built of hewn logs. All the lower windows were covered by heavy iron screens that, by an electrical contrivance, had simultaneously been lowered into place almost on the instant of Ismar's entrance. Eight small slits in the massive walls, spaced between the windows, were for the purpose of rifle-fire. The muzzles of as many rifles in the hands of his retainers, now burst into tongues of spiteful flames. Two of the king's men were hit before they were able to draw out of range. 

Once beyond rifle-fire, Captain Claudis ordered them to dismount, and to approach him: "Comrades," said he, as they formed before him, "the

Chateau yonder is impregnable to any assault we could make with our small number. An aeroplane bomb dropped on the roof, or, a several minute gun fire would easily reduce that abode, but it would also destroy our princess- 

Princess Clarita, who I am sure, is being held a prisoner there. "Besides, we have no time to waste. We must act quickly and in strategy lies our only hope of success. I want a volunteer to crawl up to the front door and blow it up with a charge of powder. The rest of us will make a decoy attack in the rear, in order to divert their attention.

The man who is willing to make the attempt, will please raise his right hand." 

Instantly, twelve hands went up into the air. A gleam of pride came into the moist, glittering eyes of the grizzled captain, as he looked along the arc of silent, stern-faced men before him. Motioning to the man nearest him, he said: "Gaspard, you have been chosen for this very dangerous mission, and honor. Now listen to my instructions. As soon as you hear us firing, you are to get busy. Approach the front door as quickly and as silently as possible. Place the bomb and light the fuse. Hide behind any suitable obstruction, and shoot any man who appears at the open door after the explosion has blown it in. Do not charge the opening made until we appear. Then you may join us in the rush. "Just one word more the attack will take place at three, tomorrow. Because of the darkness and weariness of their all-night vigil in anticipation of our next move, it is the best time for our attack. Surround the house at once. See that no one leaves it!" 

Turning to another man, he said: "You, Amos, go back and notify Lieutenant Drago to have his men scour the countryside for material to span the gorge. I want them to cross and join us before three o'clock tomorrow. Also, have him look after those who lie at the bottom of the ravine. If any are alive, see that everything possible is done for them. Shoot all the wounded horses.'

CHAPTER XXX 

By ten o'clock that same evening, Lieutenant Drago had rigged a cable across the eighty-foot space of the gorge. 

The line was supported by a timbered upright placed on either edge of the yawning precipices. 

Detailing six men to guard and look after both the equipment and horses, the rest were ordered to cross one by one, in the harness and sling arrangement attached to the swaying cable.

The novel, precarious means of bridging this awe-inspiring crevice consumed several hours. It was just a quarter to three the next morning when Lieutenant Drago reported to his captain. “Captain, I wish to report that all have crossed over safely. I left eight on guard duty, eighty-seven are here with me." Then with a catch in his voice, he added, “I found no one alive at the bottom of the gorge. We shot several of the horses that still showed signs of life. Two men are down there on guard. What are your further orders?"

Captain Claudis thereupon ordered them all to form before him. A few minutes sufficed to once again go over his plan of attack. Beneath the starry sky, just before the break of day, one hundred vengefully determined men formed in line for their decoy attack upon the besieged 

Chateau. But they were too late. A bright, crimson glare, followed by a dull, ear-splitting roar lit up the darkness of the night.

As a hundred pair of eyes turned to look in the direction of the terrific explosion, the Chateau walls were seen to sway, disintegrate and fly skyward in all directions. The scene once again plunged in darkness, almost immediately again became as light as day, when the ruins burst into flames. Not a single soul was seen to leave the vicinity of 173

the blazing ruins. What had become of them? Had they all been blown into eternity?

With a cold sweat on his brow, Captain Claudis thought of the princess. Had she also perished?

At his order the entire command rushed toward the conflagration. But none were able to approach nearer than one hundred feet, owing to the intense heat and smoke. 

As the sun crept over the horizon-and long before the ruins cooled, the remnant of the King's Guard, stripped to the waist, was engaged in a search for the unfortunate occupants. All day long they toiled. Upon the cessation of operations at dusk, not one of the perspiring, black begrimed, exhausted soldiers, believed other than that all within the ruins had perished, had been cremated. 

It was with a heavy heart that Captain Claudis, riding at the head of his decimated column, returned to the capital to make his report. Arriving at the palace, he was immediately ushered into the presence of his monarch. With faltering steps, he advanced toward the king-the father of Princess Clarita, the petite maiden whom he had failed to succor.

When within two paces of his sovereign, the Captain knelt down before him, unsheathed his sword and extended it toward his king, in a gesture of surrender. An escaping sob shook his still stalwart frame, as he with head bowed, said, in an almost inaudible voice: "Sire, I-have-failed!" "Arise, Captain," said King Augustus kindly, at the same time gently touching the drooped grey head of his loyal, fatigued subject, "arise, and give me thy detailed report.' 

It was then, at the kindly touch and words from the king that he revered and loved, it was then that Captain Claudis, veteran of a hundred clashes at arms, hardened to a life of danger, and a rigid army discipline-broke down completely and momentarily gave way to his emotion. But hastily recovering himself, he informed the king minutely as to the occurrences of his journey to Ismar's country house, such as the parley across the chasm-the destruction of the bridge the loss of his men the armed resis-

tance of the High Priest-the blowing up of the Chateauthe fire, and the subsequent fruitless search of the still smoldering ruins. "Perhaps my daughter was not there after all," said the king, like a drowning man clutching helplessly at a straw. "Perhaps my information was erroneous. What sayest thou, Captain?" 

Receiving no answer from the dejected veteran before him, other than a suppressed groan, the king thereupon placed both hands upon the Captain's shoulders and asked: "Captain Claudis, art thou withholding something I should know? If thou art, I command thee to divulge it." 'Alas, your Majesty! It grieves me to report that a young American who aided us in the search of the ruins, said he was positive that Princess Clarita was confined there at the time of the explosion."

At the Captain's reply, the king clutched at his heart, staggered, and fell to the floor.

The attendants who now ran in at the captain's call, laid the form of their unconscious monarch on a couch beneath the window, and tearfully notified the Queen. 

Upon her arrival, she found the palace physician applying restoratives in the hope of bringing His Majesty out of the heart attack that had so suddenly overtaken him. 

The king was still unconscious when the invading army of Atlanta crossed the Dovettian border. The resistance offered by the valiant Dovettian army would have held the invaders back, had they been more evenly matched. 

Three times, the onward wave of Atlantian soldiery was seen to waver, break, and recede. On the fourth charge, owing to the small handful of Dovettian defenders now left to oppose them, the wave of the invader's infantry came on unresistingly, swept over the barricades and trenches, and like a tidal wave, rushed relentlessly onward without further opposition.

But the castle was still holding out against the invaders, owing to the determination of Captain Claudis to hold it

until the king either recovered sufficiently to state his wishes, or until he, Claudis, died in his boots.

The screeching of shrapnel and the dull boom of cannon as they discharged their shells at the castle walls was terrific. Two of the grim towers that for centuries had proudly guarded their respective corners of the kingly abode, had already disintegrated under the bombardment, and lay piled in heaps of shattered rock. 

The cries of the wounded, mingling with those of the terrified women and children, rose to the heavens with the din of the carnage, causing many a stout heart to quake with dread for the welfare of his loved ones. 

The trivial artillery resistance offered by the castle's few and much inferior guns grew less and less in proportion to their rapidly diminishing gun crews. More than half of the castle's staunch defenders were now no longer upon their feet.

King Augustus was a humane monarch. The welfare of his subjects and country had always been his first consideration. A brave, valiant officer and impetuous on the field of battle, he nevertheless was blessed with wisdom. 

Recovering consciousness during the height of the bombardment, he foresaw the futility of prolonging the inevitable surrender. Upon his order, a white flag was immediately raised over one of the castle's massive walls. Simultaneous with its appearance, the bombardment ceased. 

The castle's great, bronze portals were then thrown open to admit the invading horde. Entering the royal chamber they placed the dethroned king under guard to await the further pleasure of his conqueror, King Modoc of the Kingdom of Atlanta. 

The gathering shadows of night saw the banners with the symbol of peace rolled in the dust, and the two crossed swords on a red shield, the colors of Atlanta, waving proudly over Arcadia, the conquered capital city of the 

Kingdom of Dovette. That same night, in a reclining chair, King Augustus was wheeled into the presence of the victor-the old and crafty King Modoc. As each glanced

at the other, it was King Modoc's gaze which gave way before the unflinching one of the defeated sovereign. "What are your Majesty's wishes?" asked King Augustus. "I would know the place of concealment of thy daughter, the princess." "I would that I could tell you. I believe my beloved daughter is no longer amongst the living." "You would still trifle with me?" exploded King Modoc, flushing. "That would I do most gladly, if the deed would bring my beloved daughter back to me," sadly responded King Augustus.

At this answer, instead of becoming further incensed, King Modoc, quite to the contrary, became convinced that the grieving father reclining so resignedly before him, was sincere in believing his daughter dead.

Arising from the chair in which he had been seated during the interview, King Modoc proclaimed: "I am inclined to think you speak the truth. Have you any information to substantiate your belief that your daughter is dead?"

King Augustus thereupon told his conqueror what had already transpired. He told about the cable he had received from America, the mission of Captain Claudis to rescue the Princess, the blowing up of the bridge over the chasm, the wanton death of his soldiers-the destruction of Ismar's Chateau and the cremation of those within. 

King Modoc, much impressed, then volunteered: "On the day after the occurrences of the incidents you mention, I too received such a letter. I shall investigate further. If the princess be alive and be shortly recovered, I give thee my Royal word that, following her marriage to 

Prince Sigismund, I shall see thee safely across the border. 

Sufficient gold for thy moderate requirements to live abroad for the remainder of thy days, shall also be given thee. But if the marriage be not consummated, nor proof of her death established, then, and not until then, shall thy fate be decided!"

An army corps of Atlantian engineers was at once dispatched by King Modoc to Kalasin and ordered to remove all the remaining debris of Ismar's Chateau. The young engineer in charge of the excavating was a capable youngster with more than the average amount of intelligence. 

Failing to unearth any charred bones or personal trinkets of the supposedly cremated occupants, it became evident to him that there must have been some hidden means of escape, which the besieged had used prior to the explosion.

A closer search finally revealed a cleverly concealed door in the foundation wall. It enclosed one end of a narrow shaft leading downward beneath the Chateau, to a point almost level with the floor of the gorge. At the extreme bottom of the shaft, it connected with a tunnel. About two hundred feet beyond, it made a slight turn to the right and entered the gorge itself, half a mile away. 

Upon making this discovery a report was flashed to King Modoc. Immediately, a cordon of Atlanta's infantry was thrown about Ismar's Arcadian home. A search of the premises failed to unearth either Ismar or the Princess 

Clarita, but it did bring to light Ismar's hidden love nest now occupied by the imprisoned and infuriated Madame Le Roy. She was at once liberated and questioned, but whether through fear or discretion, she failed to give them any concrete information concerning the matter at hand. 

In the chamber of horrors, far below the street level, James Holland was found chained to the wall and on the verge of starvation. He was at once liberated and sent to the hospital. On the day following the discovery of the concealed door in the foundation wall of the Chateau Minerva, a photograph of Princess Clarita appeared in all the leading European newspapers, stating that the Kingdom of Dovette would pay a reward of five thousand pounds sterling, for information leading to the recovery and safe return of Princess Clarita to his Majesty, King Augustus of Dovette. A further reward of two thousand pounds would be paid for information leading to the capture of Ismar, the High Priest of Dovette, and his female com-

panion, Esther Neilson. Their photographs also appeared beneath that of the missing Princess Clarita.

The excitement caused by the published rewards and photographs of Princess Clarita, of Ismar, and of Esther Neilson had not subsided, when another notice made the reading public fairly gasp for breath at its magnitude. 

In the newspapers of all European cities of consequence, photographs of Princess Clarita of Dovette again appeared.

In large, staring headlines, the masses of the continent of Europe were informed that the Bank of England guaranteed the sum of one hundred thousand pounds sterling to anyone giving information leading to the safe delivery of

Princess Clarita to John Desmond, at Salconia. The furore resulting from the offer of such a magnificent reward caused a tremendous upheaval amongst the peoples of several nations.

CHAPTER XXXI. 

ADVENTURERS, crooks, curiosity seekers, and others of both sexes, young and old, began to flock to the scene of operation from all quarters of the earth. Press notices that the frontiers of both warring countries were closed to tourist travel did not seem to deter the milling streams of humanity that came, were swirled about, and then were discouragingly returned from whence they had started. 

John Desmond now began to pay the penalty of being in the limelight. He found it almost impossible to enjoy a moment's rest, or seclusion. Whatever he said was printed, word for word, in numberless newspapers. When he refused to be interviewed-copy was often inspired and made. Reporters in droves camped at his door day and night. They followed and irritated him at his every appearance, for an answer to this or to that.

The clicking of cameras, snapping him under all conditions, began to get on his nerves. Wherever he walked, jostling crowds followed him in awe and admiration. They blocked the streets and hotel corridors. Bobbing heads by the hundreds were always in evidence from the windows along the streets through which his auto sped. He was obliged to have a mounted police guard in order to get around.

Such was the state of affairs at Salconia, a small town in neutral territory across the Dovettian border, when 

Richard Wallace, accompanied by a heavily-veiled woman, entered the richly appointed studio room of his nephew's 

Salconia residence. "John," said he, "this lady claims to know where Princess 

Clarita is being held a prisoner."

John Desmond sprang to his feet with a glad look in his eyes. "Madam." said he, "I hope your information will bring 180

results. We have had so many disappointments. Please be seated, and won't you kindly remove your veil?" As she complied, they saw the pleasing, attractive face of a woman of forty. The wan, sad features were now tinged with hatred. "You claim to know where Princess Clarita is, at the present time?" asked John. "Yes, sir, I do." "Is she well? Treated kindly?" "Yes, she is quite well, and has been treated as well as possible." "Then thank God-she was not injured in the destruction of the Chateau?" "No, sir, she was not." "What is your name, please?" "Is it necessary for me to tell?" "It would be desirable, Madame." "I am Esther Neilson, housekeeper to Ismar, High Priest of Dovette." "Yes," eagerly responded John, "please go on.” "First," replied the woman wearily, "how do I know that 

I will get the reward after you have secured the princess?"

John Desmond drew a wallet from his pocket, opened it and pulled out a slip of crisp paper. “Here is an order on the Bank of England for 100,000 pounds sterling. I shall deposit this in any bank you may name, with the understanding it is to be turned over to you if, through you, I recover the princess."

For a moment only did the woman hesitate, then looking fixedly into the eyes of the man before her, she shook her head with the words 

"No, that would never do. I shall have to trust you-I dare not be known in this affair-at least not before it is done. It might leak out, and my life would be forfeited. 

Of course, I personally cannot deliver her to you. Have 

I the promise of you gentlemen, that I will get the reward if through my information you secure the princess through your own efforts?" "Yes, I promise you, on my honor," responded John.

"You have my promise also," said Uncle Richard, as her glance in turn rested upon him. "Very well, gentlemen, I believe you are both honorable and will not cheat me. The princess is now confined in a small, rented house in the outskirts of Mohgene, about fifteen miles from here. This is the address," said she, handing John a slip of paper. "Come tonight, with a sufficient number of men to rescue her. Please bear in mind that 

Ismar is desperate, and armed. With him are two men, also armed, who would die in his defense; so do be careful. "Come to the little green door in the rear of the house. 

At the stroke of three of the village clock, you may enter. 

Do not knock-lift the catch and the door will open. You will find the princess in her room. It is the second door on the right, as you enter. "Ismar sleeps across the hall, the second door on the left. 

His two men sleep together in the room adjoining his, the first on the left. I sleep in the room adjoining that of the princess-the first on the right."

In order to make sure, John drew a sketch of the rooms, which, on being shown to her, met with her approval. "Are you going back to her?" asked John. "Yes, as soon as I leave here." "Will your absence excite suspicion?" "No, I was permitted to come to town. On our departure from the Chateau, it was not advisable to take any baggage. I came to purchase some clothes for the princess and myself. Ismar did not like the idea of my leaving, but bowed to the inevitable. He said that if I were careful and kept my veil down, I would not be recognized. He would have preferred sending one of his men, but concluded that the nature of their purchase would cause comment and possibly, discovery." 

As she was about to leave, Uncle Richard noticed she was agitated ill at ease. Placing his hand gently upon her shoulder, he said: "Madam, you are doing a meritorious act. fear as to the outcome." 

Have no

The woman, who was about to drop her veil in the act

of departing, stopped, and as a look of pain flashed across her face she was heard to say, with lips that barely moved: "I am depending on and anxious to receive the reward offered, but if I were to die to-night, I don't know but what 

I would welcome death, equally as well."

Without another word, she dropped her veil, and glided from the room. "Shall we have her followed?" asked John. His uncle shook his head. "No, John, there is nothing to be gained by it if the woman were lying. If she told the truth, to know that she was being followed might cause her to mistrust us in the matter of the reward and induce her to change her plansruin everything. Don't you see it as I do?" "Yes, I do, and you are dead right, Uncle. You always are!" "Tut! Tut!" exclaimed Uncle Richard with a pleased twinkle in his eyes. "If you really think so, John, you might tell your first born, when he is old enough to understand, why you and his royal mother named him 'Richard'."

A flush of pleasure spread over John's features as he noted the grin on his uncle's countenance. "In that event -Richard it shall be," promised John, clasping his uncle's hand. "And I promise to remind you, when it occurs," replied 

Uncle Richard with a chuckle, as he turned to leave. 

John Desmond, left to himself, began to day dream over the anticipated pleasure of again seeing his beloved, and to ponder over what could have caused that strange last remark from Ismar's housekeeper. "Oh, well! I suppose it is the same old story!" soliloquized he, with a shrug. "Hell hath no fury like a woman scorned." 

Esther Neilson's entrance to John Desmond's quarters had been noted by a man who from a neighboring doorway was watching for her reappearance. Upon her again coming into view, the man left his post and followed her.

After she had made her purchases and stepped into a taxi, homeward bound, the watcher rushed across the street and entered a cafe, to appease his hunger. The man was Cyril. 

Ismar had instructed him to follow Esther to see if she were recognized or did anything suspicious. Ismar was playing safe. He knew that he could trust Cyril to make a true report. He was not trusting anyone blindly.

CHAPTER XXXII. 

On the following day, three hours after midnight, in the village of Mohgene, a group of twelve armed men approached a little green door in the rear of a one-story frame dwelling. While four of them surrounded the house, the leader of the expedition lifted the latch and silently entered, followed closely by his seven companions. 

One of them, the leader, gun in hand, took up his position opposite the door of the room in which the Princess was confined. Three of the others, finding that Ismar's door was locked from the inside, applied their weight and forced it from its hinges.

As the three men fell sprawling on their faces after the inward-flying door, a spurt of flame pierced the darkness of the room. With a groan, one of them remained on the floor, with a bullet in his thigh. A second shot from the same source went wild. Before Ismar could fire again, the other two closed in and overpowered him. 

Ismar, unbeknown to them, had been sitting up in his darkened chamber, worried at Cyril's prolonged absence. 

He did not know that Cyril, unable to resist his love for liquor, had gotten quite drunk, and in crossing the street, had been run over and instantly killed by a passing truck.

Andre, the other armed henchman of Ismar, was taken without a struggle. His door not being locked, he was unceremoniously awakened and told to get up, by four determined men, from behind the grim, round muzzles of four unwavering automatics. 

When the leader, John Desmond, received the report that he was now master of the situation, he knocked sharply on the door before which he had been standing guard. "Clarita, Clarita dear, it is I, John Desmond!" said he. "Do not be afraid. Please open the door. I have come for you." 185

At his words, they heard the sound of a bolt being drawn. 

The door immediately opened to reveal Princess Clarita fully dressed, peering out into the dimly-lighted hall. Upon recognizing her deliverer to be really John Desmond, Princess Clarita, with a cry of rapture, ran toward him, to be in turn warmly clasped in his loving arms.

John could feel the tremors running up and down the slim little body, as she hysterically clutched and hung to him. And as her upturned lips willingly received and returned his kisses, in her agitation she talked to him, both in English and the language of her own country. 

Ismar, with manacles on his wrists, and a man on either side of him, was standing directly behind John. The affectionate scene between the two lovers, coupled with his disappointment at the miscarrying of his plans, angered Ismar to the extent of making him homicidal. 

Lifting his manacled hands high above his head, he was about to bring them down on John Desmond's skull, when, with a shrill cry of alarm, Esther Neilson, standing right behind Ismar, with a lightning movement, drew a dagger from her bosom, and buried it to the hilt in her treacherous lover's back. 

For just one instant, the High Priest's uplifted hands wavered over John Desmond's head, as he half turned and faced the woman who had so unexpectedly stabbed him. 

The blazing look of hate that he saw in the face of her whom he had seduced-betrayed-would even now remove -must have collaborated with the devil within him. For with scarcely a perceptible pause of indecision, he tensed his limp-growing body, and calling upon all of his reserve strength by the last functioning of his once indomitable will, and before anyone could interfere, his steel manacles descended upon the head of the now shrinking woman. 

Without a word, the stricken Esther crumpled and fell to the floor. The towering form of the High Priest for a moment swayed, tottered, and in falling, sprawled across her motionless body.

CHAPTER XXXIII. 

John Desmond lifted the half-fainting princess in his arms, and made for the open door, knowing that once outside, the fresh air would revive her. But his egress was blocked by the entrance of several soldiers, wearing the Atlantian uniform. In their midst, was the grinning Crown 

Princess Clarita in his arms, he strode over and took hold 

Prince Sigismund. Upon noting that John Desmond held of her, intent upon her removal. 

Princess Clarita's answer was to frantically tighten her hold about her sweetheart's neck, striving the while to cuddle closer to him for protection. This little by-play did not escape the now thoroughly peeved Crown Prince, who now angrily again attempted to remove her from her American lover's arms.

John, not knowing who the officer was and not even caring, swung the princess away from the man's outstretched arms, and followed the action with a shoulder shove. It caught the Crown Prince off balance and floored him. With an oath, he sprang to his feet, grasped the back of John's coat collar and jerked him violently backwards. Barely recovering himself from being floored with the burden of the frantic girl in his arms, John Desmond kicked out backward at his raging opponent, just as he was again closing in to try again. John's heel caught the prince square on the kneecap, and sent him hopping and limping about in a circle, 'mid a medley of oaths.

The Atlantian soldiers, who now almost filled the room, forcibly tore the princess from John's encircling arms.

Prince Sigismund, who, in the meantime, had somewhat recovered the use of his aching limb, on seeing that his opponent was now being securely held by his men, limped silently toward John, and suddenly spat in his face. 

John Desmond's face grew livid at the insult. With a 187

tremendous heave of his arms and shoulders, he broke away from the restraining hands that held him, struck out with all his might, caught the now cringing prince on the point of the chin, and the heir apparent to the throne of Atlanta once again went down. Dazed and again helped to his feet, he staggered blindly toward his prisoner, drew his automatic, and pointing it at John's wide open eyes, pulled the trigger.

Twice he pulled the trigger, and twice a missile of death, followed by a loud report, just missed John's head, as he now stood alone, deserted by his captors. Again, Prince 

Sigismund pulled the trigger, but there was no responding explosion. The gun had jammed. John Desmond's star was surely in the ascendant that day, for if the Prince had not been dazed, he never would have missed in so short a range.

With an oath, the heir to the crown of Atlanta grabbed the now useless weapon by the muzzle and threw it straight at John Desmond's face. John saw it coming and ducked just in the nick of time. Missing him, the speeding weapon shot through the window behind him. The crash of the flying glass seemed to bring the Prince somewhat to his senses, for he hesitated, and then rejected another gun that a grinning Atlantian captain was now offering him. "Bind that man!" commanded the prince, pointing to 

John Desmond. "Bring him to headquarters and see that he does not escape. I will deal with you later, Mr. Americano," said he, addressing John, with a baleful look in his eyes. 

Turning to the captain beside him he asked: "Are they both dead?" pointing to the two motionless forms still on the floor. "Your Highness, the man still breathes. The woman is dead." "See that he receives proper attention. I would that he should live, so that he may pay for the trouble he has caused us. Bury the woman in the village cemetery and mark her grave. I know her not, but I feel indebted to her for the dagger thrust."

The prince then smoothed out his rumpled clothes, and with another vicious look at the American prisoner, strode out of the room. Arriving at the waiting limousine, in which the half-fainting princess was now reclining, he commanded the chauffeur to hasten back to Arcadia. Stepping inside, he sat down sullenly beside her. 

Prince Sigismund was flushed, chagrined, and outraged by his late encounter and by the spectacle he had made when floored by the American. "Is your Highness comfortable?" asked the prince in a perfunctory tone of voice, from which he could not exclude his inward rage. 

Receiving no answer he continued, sharply: "I am sorry, princess, but this is war." "A war of conquest," answered the princess scathingly. "War for your favor, princess--a war of conquest if you prefer to so call it."

The toss of her head and averted face at his aim to flatter, increased his rage. Without seeming to address her further, he mused: "Of course, there are times when conditions are alteredwhen the woman runs after the man." 

Noting her silence, he continued: "But this happens only among the common riff-raff--not among ladies of quality." 

The rising wave of crimson upon Princess Clarita's cheek was the only sign the prince received that she had heard and understood. Determined to engage her in a verbal argument, he resumed: "Even under such conditions, it is deplorable when a woman forgets her place, but when a princess of the blood condescends to stoop in the 

Before he could finish, Princess Clarita's voice rang out sharp and clear: "Stop! You beast! Stop!" "Do you mean that I am wrong? That you don't love the American?" "You have no right to ask me that," replied the princess, haughtily.

"No right! No right! Who has a better one, pray? I, Crown Prince Sigismund of Atlanta, engaged to marry you, or this upstart American stranger, whose only qualification is money? Cared I for you less, I would not be at all anxious to marry you, after what I have now seen and also heard of your doings in America." "You saw and heard nothing to my discredit, Prince. I have nothing to be ashamed of." "Well, your American friends don't think so, from what they were overheard to say by one of our secret service men!" "My American friends?" asked Clarita somewhat mystified. "My American friends? Whom do you mean?" "Baron Van Guard and a Miss Ostend." "I am surprised to learn you know aught of them-also that the Baron would talk disparagingly of me. As for 

Miss Ostend, I can understand. She loves John Desmond." "Then she is your rival!" "No, I would not consider her such," said Princess 

Clarita with a smile. "Do you mean that you don't really care for this manthis American money bag?" "Indeed I do care for him. I think he is wonderful.” "Thanks," said the prince, "you have just signed his death warrant." 

"His death warrant? Do you mean you will kill himhave him murdered?" "No, princess, he shall be court-martialed for his assault upon my royal person and punished before a firing squad." "But, that, prince, would be assassination! The man is innocent. He did me a great service, both here and in 

America. Have you no heart, no consideration, no manhood?"

With a slight shrug of his shoulders, the man beside her said: "Princess, everything is fair in love and war. I consider this American a rival-and-as it chagrins me to even think of it, he must die." "Won't anything stay your hand?"

"No-nothing!" "Were I to promise to marry you-marry you willingly, would you give him his liberty-set him free?" "No, princess, I would not. There was a time when I would have gladly granted you anything you might ask, but not now. You see, my dear, I really am not so keen on marrying. I may yet decide to wait till we become better acquainted. Then if I still find you as lovable and adorable, I may then marry you. I do not fancy a loveless bride. No, princess, this American admirer of yours shall die, if it is the last thing I do on earth." "You are a despicable cad," retorted the princess-"you are disgustingly so. I abhor and loathe you.' 

"That is simply a matter of opinion," said her unabashed tormentor, shrugging his shoulders. "Be careful, princess, or you may rue it."

CHAPTER XXXIV. 

Soon after the speeding car containing the prince and princess had disappeared from view, on the return trip to Arcadia, the men comprising John Desmond's rescue party were set at liberty, as there was nothing upon which they could be held.

John Desmond and Andre were placed under arrest and brought back to Arcadia by the soldiery. Arriving at the 

Dovettian capital, John was placed in the military prison of the invading army, and facetiously told by the scowling guard to make himself at home.

Customs, habits, languages, and religious beliefs may differ from place to place; but John Desmond knew that the power of shining gold is about the same the world over. 

Reaching down into his pocket, he pulled out a gold coin and flipped it through the bars to the burly jailer. 

John was pleased to see the man's scowl disappear, as he scrambled for the coin. Although the jailer could not understand English, John's motions for the guard to keep it were readily understood and complied with. His offer of a silver coin for cigarettes was accepted with a grin and a sign that he would get them. The coins had made the jailer amiable, so much so that he raised his hand to his cap in departing. 

During the keeper's absence, John's thoughts raced back to dwell and marvel at the rapidly changing events that had taken place since that first shrill cry for help in the 

California wilderness. He wondered what was happening now to Princess Clarita, whom he loved with a love that time would never diminish. The knowledge that he was imprisoned, and while so confined, was powerless to help her, drove him almost frantic.

He also knew that his uncle would be worried at his disappearance. His request to see either him or the American 192

Consul, met with the blank stare of his guard. He could not make him understand. His motions for pencil and paper to write a note, met with a shake of the jailer's head.

Such was the situation when, with a start that made his heart almost stop beating, John recognized Rocco Mattli, in an Atlantian soldier's uniform, approaching with a bowl of soup and some bread. Upon Rocco's entrance, the soldier on guard, with a few spoken words, arose and departed. 

Rocco watched him till he disappeared from view, then, first making sure there was no one approaching, he walked back to his employer, and cautioned, in a low tone: "Please, be careful; don't raise your voice. What can 

I do for you?" "Get in touch with Uncle Richard. Tell him I am held a prisoner here." "All right, I will do that tomorrow morning. I could never slip out of camp tonight." "Tell me, Rocco, what has become of the princess? Is she here? Is she well?” "Yes, so far as I know." "I wonder if you could get word to her for me?" "I believe I could. I know Kathryn, one of the women who now attends her." "That's fine," said John Desmond. "Have you a pencil and paper?" "I have a pencil," replied Rocco, producing a stub, "but no paper." Then, putting his hand into his coat pocket, he added, "nothing but this, will it do?" and he handed 

John a package of cigarette papers. "Yes, they will do very nicely. Step over to the door," cautioned John, "and keep watch, while I write, Rocco!" 

As Rocco stepped away to guard the approach to the jail, this is what John Desmond wrote to his sweetheart, Princess Clarita: "Dearest, keep up your courage. 

I will save you. me. 

Don't worry. 

Yours with undying love, Trust

JOHN DESMOND."

While handing the note to Rocco, John asked: "I am curious to know how you came to wear that uniform?" "I was born and educated in Atlanta," answered Rocco. "My mother was Atlantian. When I became of age, I served my time in the army. A few days ago, on account of this," said he, touching his scarred face, "I was recognized by one of my old top sergeants, and immediately drafted. I will stay until you order me to leave, then—I desert. I was going to do so to-morrow.' 

"Thanks for the information-can you tell me, Rocco, how Prince Sigismund knew so quickly, where to come in order to find the princess?" "I don't know, sir. He might have had you under surveillance since you offered that fabulous reward." "I too, have thought of that. It may account for his unwelcome presence.' 

"May I ask you a question, sir?" "You certainly may, Rocco. And I'll answer it, if possible." "Well, sir, what did you do to get into this fix?" "Oh, that-that is a long story. However, I'd just as soon tell you; it will help pass the time away.' 

John Desmond then told his man, Rocco Mattli, all about his frustrated rescue of Princess Clarita, and his fracas with the Crown Prince. Just as he came to the end of the tale, further conversation was interrupted by the sound of approaching footsteps. As they looked toward the open door, it was darkened by the somewhat tipsy figure of Prince Sigismund. With a sign to Rocco to leave them alone, the prince approached John's barred prison door and asked: "How does the American fighting-cock feel, spending this beautiful, sunny day behind cold iron bars?" "A lot better than you would, if I had you in here." "Huh! you talk big. I wonder if you will act so when your time comes. Yes, I think you will-you are rash, but not cowardly," said he grudgingly.

"Thanks for the compliment, Prince. Sorry I can't return it." "The loss, then, is all mine," retorted the prince, with a mocking bow and lurch. "But don't let that worry you.

You will need all your impertinence a few days hence." "Why," queried John, "a few days hence?" "Because you will then have to explain your assault upon me." "Who started it, Prince?" "What does it matter?" "I should think it quite pertinent." "That will depend upon the findings of the court martial." "Do I understand I am to be court-martialed?" "You do, sir-no man can lay his hands upon my august person with impunity." "But I am not in the service-not even one of your subjects. You can't court-martial me! I demand a trial before a civil tribunal." "Be thankful that you get a trial at all. Ordinarily, you would be shot without any fuss." "That means I am already condemned, does it not, Prince?" "Really, Mr. Desmond, allow me to inform you I appreciate your intelligence. However, I may see you again before I retire. In fact, I think I will not deny myself the pleasure. It may give me an added thrill to learn how you have stood up under the information I gave you," said he jeeringly. "I demand to see the American Consul and my uncle, Richard Wallace. I demand it, as an American citizen." "You are not in America, Mr. Americano. You are here, a long-long way from home, and," added the prince significantly, as he turned to depart, "you will never get there, when you leave-Dovette!"

CHAPTER XXXV. 

UNCLE RICHARD was having his hands full. His nephew had disappeared. For the first time in his long, masterful career, he was in a dilemma-worried as to his next move. 

In the midst of his uncertainty of mind, the door opened, and in walked a man in a long, flowing, civilian cloak that, with the pulled-down slouched hat, completely disguised him. On removing his hat, Uncle Richard recognized 

Rocco Mattli. "Rocco, have you seen my nephew? Have you seen John?" "Yes, sir, I know where he is." "Ah! where is he tell me quickly," said Uncle Richard, rising and striding towards him.

Rocco thereupon told Uncle Richard all that John had told him the day before. Uncle Richard ground his teeth at some of the things he heard, and inwardly vowed that not even a prince could with impunity spit in the face of a Desmond and get away with it. Commending Rocco

Mattli for the information, warning him to leave the place without being recognized by spies, and to report as often as possible, John Desmond's uncle hastily put on his hat and strode from the house to his ever-ready high-powered limousine. Stepping inside, Uncle Richard said just five words to the young, alert chauffeur: "American Consul! Step on it!"

Under similar conditions, in the States, Uncle Richard would have been stopped, travelling at such a high rate of speed, through the heart of a metropolis.

During his three score years, owing to the tremendous business and far-flung connections of the Desmond Company, Incorporated, Richard Wallace had at one time or another visited the four corners of the earth. Therefore, he knew a thing or two, especially about the esteem in 196

which American money was held by the peoples of these miniature kingdoms. 

Therefore, it was with some contumely of spirit, that 

Uncle Richard, in his dash across the city, scattered several silver coins, each time a traffic official stepped out into the street to stop them. The scramble for the glittering silver enabled the speeding car to continue unmolested on its way. 

Only ten minutes elapsed in the four mile journey to the 

American Consulate.

Springing out of the automobile in a manner that would have done credit to a man half his years, Uncle Richard rushed inside. Ten minutes afterward, he rushed out again, with the American Consul in tow.

The journey of thirty miles to Arcadia, where his nephew was held, took them over two hours, because of the congested condition of the roads caused by the passage of infantry, cavalry, heavy artillery and the long trains of ammunition and supplies.

On several occasions the car was stopped while a dullwitted or officious officer interrogated them. The presence of the American Consul helped to smooth and open a way which, without him, Uncle Richard conceded would have been closed. Arriving at King Modoc's Arcadian army headquarters, they made themselves known to the attendant and requested to see Mr. John Desmond. After a few minutes' wait, they were ushered to the American prisoner's place of confinement. "Well," boomed Uncle Richard, on meeting his nephew, "you certainly had me worried when you failed to appear, and I don't know that I am relieved to any great extent, now that I have found you! But first allow me to introduce Mr. Moore, the American Consul." 

After the exchange of greetings, Uncle Richard remarked: "Now that we are all acquainted, let's get down to business. Tell us all you know, John-tell us everything, including the worst," asked his uncle as the attendant with-drew. "Oh, it is not so bad! It could have been much worse."

"Much worse! Much worse! I'm afraid I don't understand." "Well," replied John with a grin on his boyish face, "instead of a near knockout, I might have killed the Crown Prince." "Huh! I am glad you can see a funny side to the situation," dryly retorted his uncle. "It should help to cheer you up in your sojourn here. But in the meantime, let us get down to the bottom of things. I heard the story from 

Rocco, but would like to get it from you first hand, because, whatever we may decide to do, must be done according to Hoyle!"

John Desmond thereupon told all that had transpired; his rescue of the princess; her recapture by Prince Sigismund; and of his trouble with the Crown Prince. He also dwelt upon the death of Esther Neilson, who, by stabbing 

Ismar, had saved his own life. "And for that," continued John, "I owe her a debt I can never repay." "Yes, it is too bad, John; but that is life. We have to take what comes along. Now, getting back to your own case, I think the first thing to do, is to make a formal demand for your release. How about it, Consul?" he asked, turning to Mr. Moore.

The American Consul, while a very estimable man, was not one of the calibre who could cope with such a situation on his own initiative. Mr. Moore was honest and conscientious, but by nature, of a timid disposition. "Well, really, I don't know," said he. "Of course, I will do the best I can; but you see, gentlemen—at least you must realize that a state of war exists—and that my power, under such circumstances, is rather limited." "Power! Power!" roared Uncle Richard. "Power be hanged! Of course you have no power now or at any other time, in a sovereign state. But you have authority and prestige, man-prestige and backing-the backing of over one hundred million people, that comprise the glorious republic of the United States of America-America, the
