richest country on the map and one of the great world powers! "Snap out of it and get on your toes! I want you to demand my nephew's release. He is an American citizen, white, free-born, and law-abiding! He defended himself when assaulted, which is the moral and legal right of all red-blooded men anywhere under the sun! "If these little-hole-in-the-wall kingdoms cannot function without a brass band, we will stand trial before the proper tribunal! Tell them we are ready to furnish bail, to furnish it for any amount. I will not have my nephew confined in this ill-smelling place, like a common crook or murderer!" Then on being obliged to stop for breathlowering his voice, he continued: "Between you and me, Consul, as man to man, if in my younger days. this same little affair had happened to me, the Crown Prince would have been crowned, all right! I'd have knocked his damned block off."

During the tirade, the Consul's cheeks blanched and crimsoned alternately. However, some of Uncle Richard's ire must have been absorbed by him. Grabbing his hat, he started for the door shouting- "Come along with me, and I'll show you, by heck! Come come along I'll show you!" 

Uncle Richard couldn't repress a side glance and wink at his nephew-one that caused John, in turn, to smile at him with love and admiration. It made John both proud and happy to know that he was the nephew of the grand. greyhaired Viking, who at sixtv. bucking the line, could still roll 'em up!

CHAPTER XXXVI. 

UPON arriving at the headquarters of King Modoc's army of invasion, Richard Wallace and the American Consul were immediately conducted to the head of the Intelligence Bureau, General Villidin. General Villidin was a grey-haired, hard-faced man whose domineering attitude seemed to leave everything cold within its radius. Having been educated in England, he spoke the English language quite fluently. The General was a strict disciplinarian and a zealot on the divine rights of kings. "Well, gentlemen," said he, nodding to the American 

Consul, whom he recognized, "what can I do for you?" "First, allow me to introduce Mr. Richard Wallace. Mr. 

Wallace is president of the Desmond Company, Incorporated. You doubtless know of the company's various activities and standing." Upon a nod from the General, the 

Consul continued, "He is the uncle of the young man, John 

Desmond, whom you are detaining here." "Yes," replied the General coolly, "go on." "Mr. John Desmond is an American citizen. I came to demand his release." "Are you aware of the reason for his imprisonment?" "Yes," replied the Consul, "the Crown Prince was insulting; he spat in Mr. Desmond's face, and because Mr. 

Desmond resented it, the prince tried to shoot him." "That is not the way I have it," responded the General. "The prisoner is charged with assault and intent to kill 

Crown Prince Sigismund of Atlanta." "You do not believe that?" interrogated the Consul. "Why shouldn't I? But that is not for me to say. The court-martial shall determine that, three days hence." "A court-martial?" interjected Uncle Richard. "You can't be serious? Do you mean to say that in this country, 200

a man bent on an errand of mercy, to rescue a maiden in distress is rewarded by being court-martialed?" "Of course, such a rescue would have been a meritorious procedure," conceded the General, "if the rescuer were not himself so greatly interested in the lady. However, that is beside the question. The charge is such that I cannot comply with your request," said he, turning to, and addressing Mr. Moore, the American Consul. "All right, then, we hereby apply for his release on bail," responded Mr. Moore. "That is also denied," stiffly announced the General. "Don't you realize that the prisoner's trouble was with the 

Crown Prince!" "General, what would be the outcome," asked Uncle 

Richard, "if the court-martial found my nephew guilty?" "Death before a firing-squad on the following day, at sunrise." 

A cold sweat, which had been gathering on Uncle 

Richard's forehead, now stood out in glistening drops. 

Brushing them off with one sweep of his twitching hand, Richard Wallace hissed in a voice, which though low, nevertheless, trembled with suppressed fury: "Listen, General-if just one hair of my nephew's head is harmed by such an insane act of your pompous little kingdom, every cent of the Desmond fortune, as well as my own, shall be spent to destroy it!" 

And it was well that Uncle Richard opened the door and stalked out, with the now thoroughly frightened Consul at his heels. Had he lingered until General Villidin could have recovered from his amazement, he would have found himself also detained on the serious charge of lèse-majesté. 

Once again on the outside, Uncle Richard fairly ran to his waiting car. Jumping in, with the Consul close behind him, he shouted to the chauffeur: "Back to Salconia--go like hell!"

Salconia was the city in neutral territory where Uncle 

Richard made his headquarters. The chauffeur obeyed with such celerity, that as they raced along the narrow, torn-up roads, they were accompanied at every twist and

turn by the din of the tooting horns, and the explosive oaths of the drivers of vehicles which they crowded, cut in upon or sprayed with mud as they flashed by. 

Arriving at Salconia, Uncle Richard immediately sent the following letter to King Modoc, quartered at Arcadia. 

A copy was also sent to the Associated Press for publication in the leading papers of both warring countries: "To His Majesty, King Modoc of Atlanta. 

Be it known, that John Desmond, an American citizen, is now being unjustly held a prisoner by Your Majesty. 

Therefore, take due notice that I, Richard Wallace, do hereby demand the safe return of my nephew, John Desmond, to me at Salconia, on or before the third day hence, at noon. Your Majesty's failure to comply, will cause the destruction of Helga, capital of Atlanta, without further notice. (Signed)

RICHARD WALLACE." 

The following notice was inserted directly below it:

"To the People of Helga, Atlanta: 

If by noon tomorrow, my demands upon King Modoc are not complied with, I hereby warn all men, women and children to vacate at once, the city of Helga. The city shall be destroyed two days from tomorrow, in the forenoon. (Signed)

RICHARD WALLACE." 

Following the receipt of Richard Wallace's ultimatum, the Kingdom of Atlanta at once proclaimed Uncle Richard an outlaw, and set a price upon his head. Protests were also secretly filed with neutral Governments on the grounds that even the preparations for the bombardment of property of a recognized Government, by a private citizen, was piracy, and was punishable by death. Therefore, the Kingdom of Atlanta demanded that the several Governments concerned at once apprehend the said Richard Wallace and the men now banded with him, and confiscate, intern and destroy all their aircraft and munitions of war, now

within their respective territories. Through diplomatic channels, the Dovettian secret service was made aware of the impending catastrophe.

Upon the advice of his Dovettian councillors, Uncle Richard acting for his imprisoned nephew, donated to the Kingdom of Dovette all the planes, men, war munitions and supplies within his possession. At the same time, he applied for the commission of Commander-in-chief of the 

Dovettian air fleets.

The document was secretly presented to, and signed by, King Augustus. This procedure legally transferred five thousand waiting, death-dealing aeroplanes and thirty-five thousand men, with all equipment, from the service of a private individual to the service of a sovereign people. It also lifted the ban of outlaw from over Richard Wallace's head, and made possible the removal of all planes and war munitions within the time specified by international law, to the king's preserve, The king's preserve was a plot of land comprising ten square miles. It was situated in the northeastern part of Dovette, about one hundred miles from Arcadia, the conquered capital of Dovette, where King Modoc had massed his army. It was quite inaccessible, owing to its rough and barren geographical formation and the poor roads leading to it. The military advisers of 

Uncle Richard had decided it would make a strategic and ideal aeroplane base.

CHAPTER XXXVII. 

SEVERAL hours after the soldiery had placed Ismar in a bed at the town tavern, he regained consciousness, to find a doctor bending over him. Seeing that the patient was again in possession of his faculties, the doctor remarked: "That was a pretty close shave! Just missed the spinal column. The dagger struck the top of your shoulder blade and glanced downward. If you will lie perfectly quiet, I see no reason why you should not be up and around again in a few days." 

As the doctor was talking, Ismar eyed him, at first casually, then fixedly. "Have I not seen you somewhere before, Doctor?" "I should not wonder," was the easy rejoinder. “I have been practising for a generation right here in Mohgene." 

Just as the doctor finished speaking, the High Priest of

Dovette made a peculiar sign with the fingers of his left hand. With a start, the Doctor bent down over his patient and asked in a low voice, softly modulated to avoid reaching the ears of the Atlantian soldier on guard: "Who are you?" "A pilgrim of the Temple." "What ails you?" "I am in distress." "Why come to me?" "We are all for one and one for all!" 

When he heard these words, the doctor looked up quickly and furtively at the armed guard across the room, and observing that the dozing soldier had not moved, he again turned to Ismar with the declaration: "Brother, I am yours to command! you?" 

What can I do for "I must get out of here-get out at once!" 204

"Impossible! You would bleed to death." "I will have to chance that. Bend down close and listen." 

As the doctor complied, Ismar whispered: "Go to the Temple and see my assistant, Boris Zanzee.

Tell him to get my new tri-engined aeroplane out at onceand to come here with five well-armed men to rescue me. 

Tell him about the soldier on guard. Make haste! Lose no time! tion!"

Everything depends upon your zeal and devo- Bowing almost to the floor, the awed little village physician reached for his hat, and with a face flushed with pleasure at being intrusted with such a mission by so exalted a personage as the High Priest of Dovette, he stumbled and almost fell in his haste, as he rushed from the room.

At two o'clock that same afternoon, a powerful tri-engined aeroplane made an easy landing in a smooth, green field in Mohgene. After gliding along for several hundred feet, it came to a stop within a stone's throw of the town tavern. Five determined, fully armed, dark visaged men, quickly alighted and strode toward the hostelry. One man, the sixth in the party, remained behind in the pilot's cockpit.

Upstairs, in one of the front rooms, Ismar, the nurse and the guard heard the approaching tread of many feet. Without knocking, five men quickly entered the room, and covered the amazed soldier on duty. "What do you men want here?" demanded the guard. "Be patient and you will soon learn," was the brusque response from one of the newcomers. "Be a nice boy," said another, jocosely, "or you will depart from this world, with daylight in your heart!"

Two others, at a nod from the speaker, who seemed to be the leader, walked up to the now silent soldier, disarmed and bound him. Boris Zanzee, the leader of the new arrivals, now approached Ismar, who, from his bed, had interestedly watched the proceedings. "Ismar, we are here! subserviently. 

What is your desire?" he asked

"Get my clothes. I wish to leave this place at once."

Boris Zanzee turned to the blanched-faced nurse and asked: "You heard?" 

At the frightened nod, he ordered, "Well then-hurry!"

After the High Priest had hastily dressed, his men, with weapons in hand, escorted him down stairs and out of the tavern to the waiting aeroplane, even before any of the gaping spectators knew what it was all about. 

On entering the plane, Ismar at once retired to his room -a small compartment in the rear, sat down in one of the two cushioned chairs therein, folded his arms, and began to think-to scheme-and to plan anew. 

Within two minutes of his coming aboard, all three propellers began to whirl from the impulse of the aeroplane's triple engines, and as it sped down the field, within an incredibly short distance, it took off and soared exultingly into the air.

It was in his private compartment, after the plane had climbed three thousand feet, that Boris Zanzee sought his superior. "May I come in, sir?" asked he.

At a nod from Ismar, Boris entered, sat down in the other chair the compartment contained, and asked: "What are your further orders?" "First, give me news about conditions at Arcadia."

Thereupon Boris told him of the various events, particularly about John Desmond's imprisonment and pending court-martial. He also related how Princess Clarita came to be in the custody of Prince Sigismund, and gave the location of the tower room in which she was confined and guarded. At the conclusion, the High Priest turned to his assistant, and exclaimed: "Boris, we must get the princess! The days are fleeting, and we have no time to lose, if the brotherhood is to derive any benefit from the present upheaval." "If you will pardon my presumption," spoke Boris, "I would ask, how does Princess Clarita fit into our plans?

I have no recollection of having heard her name mentioned in connection with our preparations." "That is because the princess was not considered until quite recently. She is now essential to the success of our enterprise. My plan is to deliver her to King Modoc in exchange for the Crown of Dovette." "Would King Modoc agree to such an arrangement?" "I have every reason to believe he would. I don't mind telling you, Boris, that I engineered the betrothal of the Prince to the Princess on the statement to King Modoc that I was in a position to promote or thwart an offensive and defensive treaty, with a power whom he knows is patiently waiting for an opportunity to dethrone him and annex the kingdom of Atlanta. "He knows that I may still do it, so between the two, why am I not tendering him by far the best proposition?

On the other hand, an uprising of the people, brought about by the Brotherhood, would be foolish, without the backing of Italy, the power I have in mind. "But then again, the armed support of Italy in throwing off the yoke of Atlanta by virtue of a treaty, would enable King Augustus to retain his throne. Italy would never consent to a change of rulers. But let me once ascend the throne of Dovette by surrendering the princess to King Modoc and I will safeguard the people and my dynasty by an alliance with the Italian Government." "What about the other powers? Would they stand for such a treaty?" asked Boris. "How would they know? It would be a secret one, and even if discovered, don't forget, Boris, that Italy is now a world power and again coming back to her own. Under the inspired leadership of Mussolini, she has even now regained the respect and some of the prestige and power that was once the pride and the glory of the Roman Empire." "Very well, sir; what are your instructions?" "As much as I would like to be with you, I feel I should favor my aching back, so drop me off at my lodge at Zelzer. Then, with the men you now have, go to Arcadia.

Perhaps I had better instruct you in their presence, and thereby save time and energy."

Stepping out of the compartment, Ismar looked over his henchmen and thus addressed them: "Brothers, I wish to thank you for what you have already done, but, there is still much more before us. We are now on our way to Arcadia. Princess Clarita is a prisoner in the hands of King Modoc. Before sunrise tomorrow, the princess must be rescued and placed aboard this plane. Her presence is imperative to the fulfillment of the plans and designs of the Brotherhood. While I could command, in this instance I merely request. Are there any among you who relish not the task?" 

Receiving no reply, he resumed: "Brothers of the Brotherhood of Ismarites from the bottom of my heart, I thank you for your steadfast unity and devotion to the cause. Fail not in your mission to rescue our princess, and you will find that I, Ismar, the 

High Priest of Dovette, know how to reward valor and loyalty." "Have you any definite plan as to how we are to secure the princess?" asked Boris Zanzee. "Yes, listen and memorize it. Owing to the crevices in the granite masonry of the tower, it should be an easy task to climb up to the second story, to the room wherein the princess is confined. Boris, you are the man to do this. 

You are self-reliant, young, and agile. Upon reaching the casement to her room, see that you do not frighten her. A shriek, and all would be lost. Tell her that John Desmond is waiting for her, and she will come without protest. There may be several soldiers on guard. See that your men forestall their activity and silence their cries of alarm."

CHAPTER XXXVIII. 

At eleven o'clock that night, after the princess had retired, she was startled by a hand that was pressed upon her mouth in the semi-darkness of her chamber, while a man's voice softly said: "Hush, princess, don't be alarmed. I came to rescue you. John Desmond is below waiting to carry you out of danger." "John Desmond, did you say-John Desmond?" whispered the princess. "Yes," the lie came easily-if not convincingly. "But John Desmond is also a prisoner," spoke up the princess. "I don't quite understand." "Sorry, but I have no time to explain, your Highness. 

John Desmond escaped-he is outside waiting for you! 

Will you please hurry? Every second counts." "All right," responded the princess, "please turn your back while I dress." "We have no time, Princess. Please throw on a wrap. 

The delay may spoil all, and cost me my life." "Very well, then. I am ready," she whispered, stepping into her slippers. Then reaching for her cloak beside the bed, she threw it on over her pajamas.

It took but a few seconds to tie around her waist the rope which the man had brought. With the whispered words: "Please be quiet-don't be afraid, you won't get hurt," he swung her over the window ledge and lowered her into the waiting arms of his comrades below. Then tying the upper end of the rope to the bedstead, he quickly slid down after her. "Where is John-Mr. Desmond?" asked the princess. "At the rendezvous. He sprained his ankle in escaping. 

Please hurry, if we are discovered all is lost," implored her 209

conductor. Suiting the action to his words, he grasped her elbow and, surrounded by several other dark forms, they sped silently through the blackness of the night. 

Arriving at the side of the waiting plane, the princess was lifted aboard, just as a bugle sounded and drums began to beat in the direction of the castle. Even while the plane's propellers were gathering speed, at the rapidly increasing roar of her mighty engines, the beams of many searchlights pierced the surrounding darkness. "Quick!" shouted Boris to the pilot. "They have discovered her escape! We shall soon have a flock of battle planes about us if we are seen." 

As the plane rose toward the heavens, it also rose toward the scintillating beams that hurried and scurried hither and thither in their criss-cross search through the starless sky.

At a two-thousand-foot altitude, as they still climbed and hoped they would not be seen, a beam of light located them. 

Instantly, several batteries of anti-aircraft guns opened fire and showered them with their missiles of destruction. The alert pilot saw his peril and with a daring side slip, wing turn, and volplane, sped out of their glare and danger zone. "That was a narrow escape," said the pilot, to his companions. "We are not yet clear; don't you hear them above and below us?" asked Boris Zanzee. True enough. His acute ear had heard the droning of several of King Modoc's planes, as they sped here and there, seeking them. 

Again and again the lights located Ismar's fleeing plane, and again and again the capable dexterity of the pilot drove her into the jet black walls of a moonless night. "This is what I call luck," whispered one of the men to his neighbor. "Unless we hit another plane," was the grim response. "All strap on parachutes," ordered Boris. "We may yet have need of them."

Boris Zanzee then arose and fastened a parachute to 

Princess Clarita. Instructing her as to its use, he pointed at the trap door near her feet, and at a handle at its forward edge and said, "By huddling down upon it and pulling

the lever toward you, the trap door will swing downward. 

As you fall through, count five and pull this ring." Following up his remark, he placed her fingers in the ring at her breast. "It will cause the parachute to open in the clear, and land you safely." 

Thanking the man for his instruction, Princess Clarita crossed her hands upon her breast, and prayed to God for his guidance and protection.

Ismar's speeding plane was a marvelous piece of engineering. It had been built especially for him by one of 

Europe's foremost designers. The fortune it had cost him now proved to be a good investment, for it outclimbed and easily sped away from the several Atlantian planes that occasionally saw him.

Less than an hour after Ismar's plane soared aloft from the castle grounds, it alighted upon an open field in neutral territory, just across the Dovettian border. At a word from Boris Zanzee, the side door was opened, and they all filed out. Ten rods away stood a small, cozy-looking house, and toward it the party now directed their steps. 

Entering the living room, the princess, with a startled cry of alarm, gazed spellbound into a pair of piercing jubilant eyes. "You-you here?" she articulated. "I thought I was told you were dead!" "No I am still alive," replied Ismar with a frozen grimace. "Esther tried hard enough to deprive me of the pleasure." "Why did you bring me here? For, of course, the story about Mr. Desmond was a subterfuge!" 

Before answering, Ismar dismissed the men. "I brought you here for a very good reason-I intend to surrender you to King Modoc." "I will never marry Prince Sigismund-never!" "Have you no loyalty or love for your father's subjects?" "Ha! ha!" laughed Princess Clarita, "the devil turns missionary!" "Perhaps you do not realize what might befall you ifif you persist in remaining single."

"You cannot frighten me." "What makes you so heroic, your Highness? It is very unlike you. Why this antipathy toward the prince?" "I love another!" "You can never marry the American, so forget the dream." "Why not forget your own!" 

As he failed at once to reply, she continued: "So you see, Ismar, you have failed! Better give up the idea. Better set me free and you fly to parts unknown, before it is too late." "Thanks, Princess, thanks very much for your suggestion, but I don't believe I shall profit by it. I shall hold my ground. There are other ways." "We shall remain here for several days, while my wound heals. A woman, the present caretaker here, will be your constant companion. Any attempt on your part to escape or communicate with anyone will necessitate a strict confinement to your room. Be reasonable and thereby contribute to your comfort. Ask for anything within reason and it will be forthcoming." "I would know about Mr. Desmond. Know you what became of him? Is he well? Is he still in King Modoc's power?" "Your American friend is in prison, charged with intent to kill Prince Sigismund. He is to be court-martialed tomorrow. After his trial he will be shot. Prince Sigismund will see to that part of it, rest assured," he added convincingly. "O-O-Oh! Oh!" moaned his listener, involuntarily wringing her hands. 'Mr. Desmond only defended himself.

The prince is vicious and desires his death. It is murder! 

I will appeal to His Majesty, King Modoc." "I presume on the ground that King Modoc is exceptionally considerate of human lives, even if I would permit you to communicate with him," queried the High Priest, sarcastically. "Is there no way to save him, Ismar?" pleaded Princess Clarita.

"No, none, and if there were, Princess, you could hardly expect me to tell. John Desmond's money will not save him. Rest assured he is as good as dead."

With a look in her eyes that even Ismar could not fathom, the princess bowed her head and remained silent. 

At a sign from him, an aged woman was summoned. Upon entering, she placed a kindly arm about the princess and led her from the room.

CHAPTER XXXIX. 

IN ARCADIA, June Ostend, on stepping out of an automobile, alighted almost in the arms of a uniformed Atlantian soldier, who was about to pass the car's open door. Their look of recognition was simultaneous. 'Why, Mr. Mattli, how do you do? What are you doing here?" asked June. "I might also ask that of you, Miss "Oh, I came over with my parents! in that uniform?" Ostend." "I am in the service of King Modoc." 

What are you doing "King Modoc? Did you say King Modoc?" "Yes, Miss." "But he is an enemy of Clarita's father and—and I presume of Mr. Desmond, also." "Yes, Miss. How is Baron Van Guard? Is he here also?" "Yes, he came several days ago." "Was he successful, Miss?" "Successful? What do you mean?" "Did he not come here on account of some business matters?" "What makes you think so?" June asked uneasily. "Oh, I don't know. I was just wondering," replied Rocco with uplifted eyebrows and grimace.

For an instant, June seemed undecided as to her next question, but it was only momentary. Gazing at Rocco speculatively she thought she saw in him a man who could be handled advantageously, provided the consideration was tempting. "Rocco Mattli-will you answer a question-answer it truthfully, if I promise on my honor never to betray you?" "You may ask it, Miss, but I cannot promise to answer." 214

"But if you answer at all-will you promise to answer truthfully?" "Yes, Miss." "Are you wearing the uniform of Atlanta as a spy for 

John Desmond and that-that girl, or are you really for 

King Modoc?" "Would my answer mean much to you, Miss Ostend?" "Yes, it would." "I am not a spy. I came by the uniform honestly!" "Thanks, Mr. Mattli. When could I see you? There is a matter to talk over, which, if you accept, will be to your advantage." "Won't the present time do, Miss? I don't know when 

I could get leave again." "It suits me," was the reply. "Suppose we take a drive while coming to an understanding?" 

Rocco bowed his head in assent.

June thereupon re-entered the waiting car, followed by Rocco. "Drive out into the country," said June to the chauffeur.

Once seated and under way, she turned to the man beside her and asked: "Would one thousand dollars mean anything to you, Mr. Mattli?" "It would be more than I ever had at any one time, Miss Ostend." "Well, that sum is yours, if you will do me a little service. Would you like to earn it?” "Yes, Miss, I would." "Do you know Prince Sigismund by sight?" "Yes, Miss, I do." "Could you approach him-deliver a message?" "I might. It would depend upon what my message was, otherwise, I might be censured-perhaps punished." "You would receive neither, for the message you would give him." "All right-where is the letter?" "There will be no letter. For reasons of my own, I prefer that you deliver a verbal message."

"Very well-what shall I tell him?" "Listen carefully," said June, while she impulsively grasped his arm. "Tell the Crown Prince that Princess

Clarita is again the prisoner of Ismar, the High Priest of Dovette. Tell him it was Ismar's men who abducted her from her chamber in the castle's tower." "Where is she now?" "In a small brown stone house in Zelzer. The address is 38 La Fountaine." "How do you know, Miss?" "It does not matter." "Indeed, it is quite important. Your informant may have been joking, or in turn, might have been misinformed. In either case, I should get myself in a serious position for trifling with His Highness. I would surely be severely reprimanded, if not imprisoned. I might even be shot!"

After a moment's deliberation in which June seemed to weigh the matter carefully, Rocco heard her say: "I believe you are right, and I see no real objection in telling you. Baron Van Guard, soon after arriving here, secured a position in Ismar's employ, through the influence of a friend he had made when in the diplomatic corps, several years ago.

The Baron was one of the pilots of Ismar's plane the night the princess was removed from her chamber in the castle tower." "Please don't think me presumptuous, Miss, because it really is none of my business, but I fail to see why you would want her rescued? She cannot interfere with your plans as long as she is where Mr. Desmond could not find her." "That is just the reason why I want the prince to know where she is. Let the Crown Prince once again secure 

Princess Clarita, and she will be heavily guarded until her wedding day. Let John Desmond realize that the Princess 

Clarita as the wife of Prince Sigismund, is forever out of his life and he will return to-to reason. But if she simply disappeared, I know John would never cease in his efforts to find her."

"Why don't you deliver the message yourself and save a thousand dollars?" "Oh, I have my reasons. But I may do even that if you fail me or I may delegate the Baron, as originally planned," said she tartly. "What is to become of him, when the prince starts things going, upon my information? Is he to be sacrificed with the rest of them?" "No, of course not. I will warn him to leave at once. 

I am to meet him within the hour." "Very well, Miss; sorry I will have to leave you now. 

My leave of absence is almost up." "When will I see you again?" "As soon as I have told his Highness, and can get away. 

Where are you stopping?" "At this hotel," replied June, handing him a card of the 

Arcadia Inn. 

"But remember," was June's parting admonition, "I expect you to get busy at once, otherwise our agreement is off." "Yes, Miss, I will remember." 

June Ostend then ordered the chauffeur to return. Upon

Rocco's request, the car drew up to a curb and he alighted. 

Even before the receding automobile in which June was seated, had turned the corner, Rocco was deliberating as to what might be the best thing to do. Should he desert the service now-at once, and in civilian clothes, rush to Salconia, to make his report to Uncle Richard, or should he try to communicate by telephone and save time and trouble?

After a few minutes' thought, he decided that the time lost for the trip might be dangerous. To telephone, even more so. What should he do? What should he do? "What should you do?" answered a voice within him. "Hasten back to camp. See Mr. Desmond, tell him everything and let him instruct you!"

CHAPTER XL. 

"HELLO, ROCCO," was John Desmond's greeting, when

Rocco again came on duty, at ten o'clock on the evening of the same day, following his conversation with June Ostend. "Have you any news?" "Miss Ostend is here. I have just seen and talked to her." "What-June, here in Arcadia? That is indeed news! 

Wonder what brought her here?" murmured John. "It was foolish of her to come when the country is at war. Did she say why she came?" asked John Desmond, turning to him. "To my mind, I think she did. But you can judge of that after hearing what took place." Rocco then told his employer all about the interview with June. During the recital, John was a silent, attentive listener. At his conclusion, his first words were: "Poor girl! I do hope she will some day find the man who will make her happy. I cannot condemn her. She is fighting for her man!"

On realizing that he had spoken his thoughts aloud, he first grinned sheepishly, then looking soberly at Rocco he added: "Please don't deem me conceited. It is a deplorable situation, and I am really sorry for the girl." "I think Miss Ostend and the Baron should hit it off, sir.

I've seen them together quite a bit. They make a pleasing couple, side by side." "Yes, Rocco, they certainly do. You know, the Baron and I were quite friendly in New York. I rather like him. 

Anyway, allow me to commend you for the way you handled your interview with Miss Ostend; also, for passing it on to me. I will remember." "Thank you, sir, you are very kind." "So Clarita is again in Ismar's clutches," mused John. 218

"What are the chances for effecting my escape from here, Rocco?" asked he abruptly. "Not very good, sir; but nothing is impossible if rightly planned. Have you anything to propose?" "Yes, I have thought it over and over, ever since the prince left. His Highness was in to see me this morning. 

Said he would call again tonight. Seems to take consider- 

Wants to get my nerve, able delight in tormenting me. apparently. Now, I have a plan, and I think it is a good 

How long are you to be my guard?" one. "Till two, tomorrow morning." "Shall we be alone, Rocco?" "Yes, sir, I believe so." "That is fine. Give me your attention and listen carefully. When the prince calls tonight, you are to come in and cover him. Just get the drop on him, and leave the rest to me. But if you notice he is inclined to cause a disturbance, crack him over the head, and let me out. Will you do that? Can I absolutely depend upon you?" "You can, sir!" "All right, Rocco, I shall depend upon you! What time is it now?" "About twelve o'clock, sir." "Hark! What is that?" "Some one is coming, sir-it may be the Crown Prince." 

Just as Rocco slid away from the barred opening in

John's prison cell, the outside door was thrown open to admit Prince Sigismund and one of his aides. After a casual glance around the room, the prince curtly ordered his aide and Rocco to step out into the hall and wait. 

Approaching the opening where John was standing, the prince spread his legs apart, and holding his bent swagger cane breast high, horizontally before him, he said with a sneer: "Well-well, Mr. Americano-so you are still here, and 

I see you are also still awake. What's troubling you? Can't you sleep?" 

Receiving no answer, he continued: "I wonder where you will be the end of the week.”

"Where you wish you knew, but won't-" "Good! Good!" laughed the prince, clapping his hands softly-mockingly. "That is what your countrymen call a good joke a hard nut, eh?" "It is, but your measly brain would never crack it." 

At the veiled insult questioning his mentality, Prince Sigismund took a step toward the barred opening and jabbed his cane through at the mocking face before him. 

Just in the nick of time, John sidestepped and avoided the thrust at his eyes. Instantly the Prince's hand dropped to his holster. What the prince might have done further in his anger, was halted by a sharp hiss from behind him, with the words: "Drop that!"

With his hand on the butt of his half drawn gun, the prince now wheeled around to gaze into the muzzle of a rifle in the hands of one of his own soldiers. "Put your hands up, prince, up high in the air, or I'll blow the top of your head off!" commanded Rocco. "What is the meaning of this? Are you demented?" asked the prince, angrily, but he complied with the order, and raised his hands above his head. "No, he is not demented by a long shot," spoke up John 

Desmond, answering for Rocco. "He happens to be one of my own men. If you doubt his loyalty to me, raise your voice-make just one false move and His Majesty, your father, will be minus his son!" "Where is my aide?" demanded the Crown Prince, nervously, looking at Rocco. "Outside, with a broken head," was the laconic response. "You will both die for this, with your backs to the wall!" said the prince, purple with rage. "Stop your threats, prince. Don't forget that I still have an unpaid score to settle with you," replied John, terselv. "You will never leave this place alive, Mr. Desmond!" "That remains to be seen, Prince." "What do you two mad men expect to gain by your insane action?"

"Open the door, your Highness. I wish to leave!" replied John.

As the prince remained motionless, John Desmond again addressed him: "Come on, Prince, better get busy. Don't you know that every second you waste hastens your own untimely end also, if we are discovered? Open the door!" "I haven't the key. I am not the jailer," haughtily responded the Crown Prince. "The door requires no key, Mr. Desmond," interrupted 

Rocco. "It is bolted on the outside. I shall slip the bar." "No, Rocco, no," said John, hastily. "Keep him covered! Don't you see he is armed? If he makes one false move, shoot him first, and then let me out. Prince," continued John Desmond, "I ask you for the last time to open the door; if you don't my man will have to do it himself, but only after he has cracked you over the head, with his rifle."

During the interval the princely courage had oozed considerably. It was with trembling hands that he now hurried over and slid back the bolt. 

Upon being liberated, John stepped up behind his Highness and disarmed him. With the requisitioned automatic now prodding the royal ribs of the heir apparent to the throne of Atlanta, John forced him into the quarters that he himself had just vacated. "You must pardon my inconsiderateness, Your Highness, but I shall have to change clothes with you," said John, grabbing at his coat. "Don't you dare disrobe me-don't you dare," cried 

Pirnce Sigismund. 

A none too gentle tap upon the royal head with the butt end of his own automatic, now in John's possession, stopped all further remonstrances from his Highness. "I sort of owed him that," said John, half apologetically, to the grinning Rocco. 'But I hope I have not killed him." "He will be all right in a few hours, sir. It would take more than that to crack his dome of solid ivory."

Working rapidly, they soon had him disrobed, gagged and bound. 

Ten minutes after the slipping of John Desmond's prison bolt, the heir apparent to the Kingdom of Atlanta stepped jauntily across the threshold with a soldier of the realm beside him.

Although the night was far from chilly, the prince's cloak collar was turned up and his hat pulled down, hiding his face. "It is lucky that the prince and I are about the same build, Rocco." "Yes, Your Highness-it is most fortunate, but as the homesick doughboy said when over here, 'It's a long, long way to Tipperary'!" "Oh, I don't know-just act natural, Rocco, and we will make it all right. Don't get careless, though. Be ready at all times to make good use of your gun. Do you know where we could get a plane? It's our best bid to get away." "The prince has a dandy one in the building over there," said Rocco.

Their approach was noted by several of the soldiers on guard at the hangars. They stood at attention as their prince drew near. One of them, an officer, approached 

John, saluted and said in Atlantian: "If your Highness is going up, I wish to report that your plane is conditioned and ready." 

John Desmond did not understand the language; and the threatened calamity to their bold attempt was averted by Rocco, who, addressing the officer in the Atlantian language, answered: "Captain, that is just what His Highness proposes to do."

Somewhat nonplussed at being so addressed by one in the ranks and ignored by his prince, the captain nevertheless saluted, turned on his heel and led the way to the hangar, within which stood a truly magnificent aeroplane. 

Upon the approach of the party, several men ran it out into the open, while another climbed aboard and started to warm her up.

The captain again asked the prince: "Are there any further orders, your Highness?" 

Not knowing what the man said, John Desmond, impersonating Crown Prince Sigismund of Atlanta, turned his back upon him and stepped several paces away, while his hand beneath his cloak grasped the butt of Prince Sigismund's automatic. Again, Rocco's quick wit and good judgment saved the day. Standing before the captain, at salute, he said in a low tone: "Pardon, captain, but the prince, for some reason, is very much vexed this evening. Again I beg your pardon, captain, for so informing you." "He does act queerly, now that you mention it. What is your name?” "Rocco Mattli, sir." "Report to me at your first opportunity. You will not be sorry," added the captain, smilingly, on noting the puzzled look on Rocco's face. "Thank you, captain. I will remember." 

The helmeted, goggled man in the pilot's nest of the prince's aeroplane, made no motion to step down at John's approach. Not being able to ask him to get out, John beckoned to the pilot to step down. 

"The prince desires to pilot his own plane this evening,' said Rocco, looking up at the aviator, who was, in reality, Prince Sigismund's personal pilot. 

Now, neither John nor Rocco knew that Prince Sigismund was an indifferent pilot, that he seldom took the stick in his hands. The man addressed therefore looked down upon them in surprise. Nevertheless, he complied with the request. Unfortunately, in stepping down, he stumbled and fell heavily against the prince. The shock threw them both down. The prince scrambled to his feet, minus his hat, and the features of John Desmond were exposed to view. 

With a cry, the man reached for his automatic. But Rocco beat him to it by seconds. He shot the pilot without raising his rifle, by firing from the waist. 

The captain, at the same time, went down with smashing blow on the point of the jaw from John's clenched fist.

It all happened in the space of a few seconds; but as both men went down the spellbound observers now came into action, and with yells of rage, rushed toward them. "Quick, Rocco!" shouted John. "Follow me!"

In the excitement, John got into the wrong seat. It was 

Rocco, who just behind him, scrambed into the pilot's cockpit. "My God! We are done for!" groaned John inwardly, as he whipped out his automatic, and started to answer the shots of the approaching throng, whose speeding bullets were now whining about them. 

Just as John fired his last shot-threw the empty gun at the grinning face of the man trying to climb up at him, and reconciled himself to his fate, the plane gave a jerk, and in darting ahead, threw another climber sprawling upon the ground. Faster and faster rolled the plane, as Rocco gave her the gun. With a parting volley from the chagrined 

Atlantian soldiers behind them, the prince's plane majestically took off and soared into the atmosphere. The medley of enraged cries and further gun shots were drowned by the terrific roar of the plane's whirlwind motor, as Rocco opened her up wide.

It was then that John Desmond sank back into his seat, and looked across at the grinning, white face of Rocco Mattli, who, unbeknown to his employer, was an expert pilot-one who during the World War had won his wings from the outcome of many a hard fought battle above the clouds. The disfiguring scar on Rocco's face was caused by a bullet that had plowed its way across his cheek in one of his aerial combats, over Verdun. 

Looking earthward behind them, they saw bright flares come into life, followed by long shafts of sunlight, streaming through the darkness in search of them.

But Rocco, with a master hand kept her climbing for altitude. Several planes that soon rose in pursuit were quickly outdistanced and lost to view.

CHAPTER XLI. 

LOOKING over his compartment, John discovered a speaking tube. Putting it to his lips, he said to Rocco: "Good boy! I did not know you could fly. It was a case of nip and tuck. But we made it!" "Yes, but it was rather close, sir," replied Rocco with a pleased grin, as he now leveled off and eased down the motor. "Where are we headed for?" "I was just going to ask you for orders." "I think the first thing to do is to rescue the princess. I would like to have my uncle know of my escape, but we have no time to spare. Do you know where Zelzer is?" "Yes, sir. It is one hundred and fifty-five miles from here in the direction we are now going." "That is splendid. Keep her to it, and get there as quickly as possible." "I hope the princess is still there, sir." "So do I." 

When within ten miles of their destination, Rocco called John's attention to a small object in the sky that was rapidly making altitude. It was almost directly between them and the full moon.

John Desmond immediately trained his glasses upon it. 

After a minute's survey he said: "It is a plane. quite sure of it." 

Seems to be tri-motored. Yes, I am "Bet you anything, sir, it is the plane we are after." "Why so, Rocco?" "Several days ago I heard some of the boys at the hangars rave over a tri-colored aeroplane of foreign make that had just been delivered to the High Priest of Dovette. The discussion was, as to whether or not it could run away from 225

the plane of the Crown Prince, conceded to be the fastest in Atlanta." 

"Well, let her out, Rocco, and we shall soon find out.' 

With a grim nod, Rocco again gave her the gun. Like a demon rushing through the night, the aeroplane gathered speed. Faster and faster spun the screw, and as the song of the engine increased from a growl to an ear-splitting roar the wind, rushing across the taut wires at over two hundred miles an hour, made them shrill and shriek, as they sang their unearthly song of conquest over the opposing elements. "This is some crate, Rocco," shouted John through the tube. "I'll say she is," was the return comment.

Rocco's other surmise was also correct. The plane in front was Ismar's and it certainly could travel. The distance between them, which at first was being rapidly diminished, now seemed to undergo no further change, as the other also attained full speed. If the Crown Prince's plane had the speed trophy in Atlanta, it surely had a dangerous rival in the tri-motored one they now pursued. For several anxious minutes there seemed to be no appreciable difference in the position of the planes, but after awhile, they both could see they were slowly but surely closing the gap. Barring accidents, in a few hours, they should overhaul the plane before them.

After going along for an hour, they noted the fleeing plane was bearing to the left and gradually circling, as if unwilling to leave a certain territory. "If they keep on, Mr. Desmond," said Rocco, "we shall have daylight before they land. Do you think they saw us?" "I don't know. If they did, it would explain their remaining aloft. With the proper light, they will pepper us with their gun." "Do you suppose they have a machine gun?" "It's a safe bet they would not be without one, Rocco." "But we also have one." "Yes, it is a Vickers, and a dandy," said John; and even

before he had ceased speaking, he wondered what he should do if the princess was aboard the fleeing plane. He would never engage it in combat. In an aerial duel he could not fire upon it for fear of killing the woman he loved.

Still, uncertain as to what he could do to rescue his beloved under such circumstances, he was, nevertheless, determined to keep the other plane in sight until something turned up. "What if they have a machine gun, and fire upon us?" asked Rocco. 

"Then we are to keep out of range, but not lose them.' 

The plane ahead, on noting that they were being overhauled, turned her nose upwards and started to climb. The roaring plane behind them immediately followed, and likewise made altitude. 

After several minutes of climbing, John Desmond was pleased to see that if it came to an altitude test, they could more than equal the performance of the tri-colored plane. "We are holding her," shouted Rocco. "Yes, it is lucky that we borrowed the Prince's plane. 

Any other one would be out of the running with that baby!"

As if in answer to John's statement, the other plane stopped climbing and straightened out just as the first streaks of dawn were creeping above the horizon. Even as they looked, they saw tiny flashes-little dots of twinkling red, at the side of Ismar's plane. The miniature flashes were instantly followed by the whines of a machine gun's messengers of death, as they sped by. "They have opened fire upon us!" shouted John. "Fall back, out of range. God! How I wish I knew if the princess were aboard! I don't dare fire, in my uncertainty." 

In answer to his prayer, something white fluttered from one of the rear windows of the plane before them, and as the pursued plane changed its course and bore to the left, John instantly focused his glasses upon it. A cry of joy mixed with alarm, escaped his lips. For there at the rear window of Ismar's plane, he distinctly saw her-saw his beloved, Princess Clarita-waving a handkerchief at him,

and, as he looked, he was astounded to see the plane bank sharply, on a left wing turn and head directly toward them. 

Rocco saw the maneuver and instantly followed suit.

The positions were now reversed: Ismar was now pursuing his pursuers.

As both planes straightened out-hare and hound-full speed ahead, Ismar again opened fire, but with incendiary bullets. "Look out, Rocco!" shouted John, through the tube. "He intends to set us afire!"

Even as he spoke, the tip of their right wing started to smoke. Hoping to extinguish it, Rocco sideslipped to the left. With a cry of joy, both saw the maneuver was successful. The wing failed to flame up.

With a swoop, the larger plane was now almost upon them. But Rocco was a flying ace. Owing to the superb technique of a masterful hand that knew no fear, the plane of Prince Sigismund sideslipped, weaved, rolled and otherwise eluded the shower of bullets directed at them. With burst upon burst, Ismar tried to shoot down the two human bloodhounds that for several hours had been trailing him. 

And yet John Desmond withheld his fire. His hand shook -the perspiration dripped off his brow-his nerves fairly shrieked in his tortured body, but he could not shoot. He could not dared not, for fear of killing his beloved.

Oh, if the planes were not quite so evenly matched in speed, if Ismar's were not so speedy. The handicap of having to withhold his fire would be somewhat overcome. 

He then could get out of range and keep away, without fear of losing the High Priest and his party. 

With a throbbing heart filled with despair, John felt the end was not far off. And yet he would not shoot-hecould not. On the other hand what right had he to sacrifice Rocco's life, when both he and his sweetheart owed to 

Rocco both life and honor?

His mind was now made up. He would order Rocco to chance a landing regardless of the gun-fire that he knew would immediately rake them from their tail. "Look, Mr. Desmond! Look!" The words from his companion tore him from his thoughts and startled him

into action. Following the direction indicated by Rocco 

Mattli's outstretched hand, he saw a human form-the form of a woman shoot earthward from Ismar's plane. 

It was Princess Clarita, his sweetheart, the girl he was willing to die for, and she was falling-falling downwardrushing toward the earth to a most horrible death. 

With a groan that seemed to tear his heart to shreds,

John covered up his eyes with his hands. 

But another cry from Rocco one of elation-again caused him to look, caused his heart to stop-to stop, and then again throb with hope anew. For, suspended beneath the white expanse of an open parachute floating lazingly down to mother earth, he again saw her-again saw Princess Clarita, the girl of his dreams. 

His cry of thankfulness was interrupted by a most wanton act of those aboard that satanic aeroplane. As the descending parachute was still two thousand feet from terra firma, Ismar's plane was seen to volplane toward it and open fire.

At the first rat-a-tat-tat of that murderous machine gun, Rocco banked sharply and giving his plane the gun, volplaned madly down in a desperate effort to get between the line of fire-between the fire of the murderous devil and the helpless swaving girl. But he was too late. At another burst from the High Priest's plane, the body of Princess 

Clarita was seen to sag-her arms went limp, and her head drooped upon her breast.

It was then that both men went temporarily mad. "Ram him, Rocco. Damn him-ram him!” shouted

John at the top of his voice, as he, at the same time, brought his own Vickers into her long deferred action. "You are damn right, I will!" shouted Rocco. "I owe him one!"

With roaring motor. he now headed straight for Ismar's plane, while John. frenzied and boiling with rage. showered a steady stream of lead into her very vitals. 

Just as a crash seemed imminent, the larger plane was seen to dip, swoop down beneath the smaller one, zoom, and loop in an effort to get out of the way and on the tail of its opponent. But the maneuver was not wholly successful.

For it was now that the plane of Prince Sigismund, in the superb daredevil hands of Rocco Mattli, in its challenge of death, was called upon to prove the lavish claims of her designers. With loops, barrel rolls, wing turns, Immelmans and others, the heavier plane was outmaneuvered, harassed and raked again and again by burst upon burst. 

Following an exceptionally terrific volley from the Vickers, which was now almost red-hot in John Desmond's taut, bloodless hands, the High Priest's shot-riddled plane was seen to turn, volplane and zoom in a desperate bid for a getaway.

But Rocco was not to be caught napping. He also volplaned, and followed.

Slightly above and behind the fleeing tri-motored plane, darted its single screw Nemesis-the avenger of the murdered Princess Clarita.

Try as he would Ismar could not elude the two halfcrazed, desperate men now determined to exterminate him. 

A cloud bank lay directly ahead. If the fleeing plane could reach it in time, the High Priest might yet elude them. The distance was rapidly diminishing. Ismar was drawing closer and closer to those all-enveloping clouds. 

Would he make it? Most likely! He was almost therejust a few more miles, and ah! 

John gave vent to an unearthly yell of delight as he saw the shattered rudder detach itself from its position on the tri-motored plane and shoot earthward in erratic dives and swirls. Although deprived of this means of control, the larger plane was still a menace. 

Racing erratically through space, without a moment's warning, it swerved toward them and in rushing by, the left wing struck them a glancing blow. With a loud crack, John's plane began to lose headway, and drop. "Propeller's gone!" shouted Rocco. "We'll have to land," and with his voiced purpose, Rocco volplaned. The field upon which they would have to make contact was fairly level, but rough in spots. At a terrific pace, the running gear touched the ground, and shot up into the air.

Coming down again, the plane still going fast, bumped along a hundred feet, careened, caught her right wing on a small stump, turned turtle, and burst into flames.

CHAPTER XLII. 

TILL midnight on the eve of the threatened bombardment of Helga, Uncle Richard waited at headquarters with the hope of hearing that King Modoc was agreeable to the surrender of his nephew.

All day, and all night, before the expiration of the time limit set for the bombardment of Helga, the streets of Helga were cluttered with all sorts of moving vehicles such as trucks and automobiles, carriages, pushcarts and wheelbarrows. Horses, donkeys, and thousands of human beingsall loaded down with more or less furniture, food, clothing and other merchandise, were hurrying to get as far away as possible from the threatened city, because of the refusal of King Modoc to be moved by what he called "the mad American."

That five thousand battle planes and bombers armed to the minute with the best and most modern weapons that money could buy, manned by expert pilots, most of whom had been baptized in a deluge of fire during the World War, did not, for a moment, enter into King Modoc's calculations, as to just what they could deliver, when sent en masse on a mission of destruction. 

So, instead of profiting by the warning he dispatched several divisions of cavalry and infantry with all the battle planes and anti-aircraft guns that he could spare, back to 

Atlanta for the defense of his capital. 

On the morning of the threatened bombardment, a stillness prevailed over the doomed city of Helga, that was almost uncanny. Only a few of the civilian population still foolishly remained within its confines. 

Since the break of dawn all eyes were upraised. scanning the skies for the first appearance of the scheduled hordes of devastation. At just ten minutes of ten, innumerable small splotches were seen in the east, which grew larger and larger 232

as they rapidly drew nearer and nearer. At once, the airraid alarm in Helga was sounded by the ringing of bells, the blowing of horns and the shrieking of whistles.

The few hundred gentry who still remained through avarice, necessity or bravado, now, like rabbits seeking their burrows, made a dive for basement shelters and other underground places. Others, scoffers at the threatened bombardment, also either ran or rode as they raced for the open country.

The approaching air raiders consisting of ten air fleets, comprised the five thousand planes that John Desmond had financed and given, through his uncle, to the service of Dovette.

Approaching in fifty different divisions, each one numbering one hundred planes, they made an awe-inspiring sight, as they impressively swept onward in squadron formation 'mid the thunderous drone of their revolving propellers, accompanied by the deafening roars of over eight thousand explosive motors.

As each fleet showed a one-mile front, the raiders would practically cover the entire city in their sweep of death and destruction.

First came the battle planes, and a little behind and somewhat below them, four thousand bombers carrying thousands of tons of T. N. T. bombs, which they began to drop systematically, as they flew over the doomed city.

The anti-aircraft guns trained against them, did little or no damage as the raiders spread a smoke screen that hid them from view. Such battle planes of the insignificant Atlantian Air Fleet manned by truly heroic souls, who, rather than run away, turned to die for their country, were immediately smothered by the concentrated machine gun fire of the invading battle planes. 

Within thirty minutes after the first bombs had been dropped, the once beautiful and arrogant city of Helga was a raging volcano from end to end. 

The smoke and flames from the conflagration mounting to the skies, obscured the sun, and turned a midsummer's day into a starless night. The rain of death and destruc-

tion from the skies upon the doomed city had never had a counterpart. Mere words could not adequately begin to describe the awe-inspiring holocaust. 

Fires broke out simultaneously in a thousand places. 

Church steeples, Government buildings and other proud edifices were among the first to go. Dense columns of smoke, long tongues of flames, shrapnel-like showers of shattered rock were here, there and everywhere. Acrid, death-dealing gases contaminated the air and impregnated the streets and buildings alike. Irrespective of size, place and construction, buildings in block after block, were hit, caught on fire, and toppled over. Striking the ground with crash upon crash, they formed irregular mounds of roaring volcanoes, fed by all sorts of tangled inflammable debris. 

The bursting water mains with their gushing geysers flooding the streets, added to the deafening din and confusion, to the disorder and chaos.

Majestic trees that lined the boulevards, others in public parks that for centuries had proudly withstood the ravages of storms and time, upon the sweeping approach of this avalanche of smoke and crackling flames, for just a moment cringed and seemed to sway away. 

But the devastating breath of the fiery blast would not be denied its share of the cataclysm now in the making. 

One by one they speedily ignited, crackled and torch-like, after their one respective blaze of glory, disintegrated to black, smoking stumps of glowing timber.

Streets that once spelled the grandeur of a nation's culture, no longer were a joy to behold. Seams and crevices in the earth's surface zig-zagged across them. Crater-like holes, filled with burning wreckage, or with water and slime were everywhere in evidence. Fallen poles with their still attached wires, could be seen all tangled up and sticking out of the piles and ridges of fallen masonry. 

Gigantic piles of debris, here, there and everywhere, made one's heart ache. In places they completely hid the location of the capital's once proud thoroughfares. 

By four o'clock, just five hours after the first bomb was dropped upon the doomed city, not a living thing that ever

walked or crept, remained alive in the smoldering ruins. 

When the news was flashed to King Modoc at Arcadia, that his capital-his, once magnificent city of Helga, had been totally destroyed by the air fleets of Dovette, it only made him all the more stubborn and determined to hold all conquered Dovettian territory.

Had he dared, he would have ordered the execution of King Augustus then and there; but to do so, he wisely reasoned, would be a wanton act-one that might estrange from him followers whose good will and support he could not afford to lose at this time. 

Nevertheless, he refused to comply with the demands that made the destruction of Helga an object lesson. Instead, he commanded that all available men and guns be sent against the Dovettian Air Base in the King's preserve, with orders to destroy that rendezvous together with all the aircraft assembled there.

General Constant, the man who King Modoc believed was the one best suited to entrust with such a mission, was in truth Atlanta's ablest military strategist.

Upon being made aware of what his sovereign expected him to accomplish, he lost no time in seeking an audience with His Majesty.

The king was in the midst of a banquet when the General was announced. It was a banquet born from the fear of the future. Judging from the empty bottles, boisterous voices and conversations, the dinner was undoubtedly progressing very satisfactorily. King Modoc accordingly was in a merry mood, but not so much so that he failed to see that General Constant was perturbed. "Welcome, General!" cried the king. "Come over and join us!" "Thanks, sire, but I have not the time, if I am to fulfill my marching instructions." "Then what pressing need brings you to us?" "I pray your Majesty will grant me a few words in private." "Is it pertaining to your instructions?" "It is, sire."

"Then speak up-we are among friends." 

At once, laughter greeted His Majesty's airy comment.

General Constant, cold and calm in moments of danger, almost flared up at the wine-sodden mirth of the assembled courtiers court parasites-who should have never been allowed to enter camp. Hiding his annoyance, he answered: "Your Majesty, I came to ask that you reconsider the instructions to march against the Dovettian Air Base." "Why, General, are you too getting nervous and fancy it not?" 

A red flush appeared upon General Constant's face. "Sire," answered he, "if you were not at a festive board, you would not so speak to one of your most loyal subjects."

For a moment, the air seemed charged with a high explosive caused by the nature of General Constant's reply. 

For an instant, it looked bad for the General; but the apprehension of those assembled was quickly dispelled when the King, rising from his chair, raised his glass to his lips, with the words: "Gentlemen, let us all drink to the success of a brave and loyal soldier, General Constant." 

Taking the glass of wine offered him, the General bowed and drank with them. "Hast thou ought else to say, General?" asked the king. "Sire, my divisions march tonight. But before going

I wish to state I am not in accord with the maneuver. It is ill advised. The nature of the region to be invaded makes the expedition at its best but an armed gestureproductive of no benefit to Atlanta. Besides, the march is bound to be not only slow and tedious, but fraught with grave danger to my entire command."

The king's frown deepened as the General ceased talking. Looking upon the seemingly unafraid, expectant faces about him, King Modoc failed to strengthen his half-formed resolution to revoke the order and devise some other way to exterminate the Dovettian Air Base. "On the other hand," reasoned he to himself, "why should he do so? He was in a serious situation-one from which he must in some way extricate himself. If not, his

throne would surely totter, and in tottering-crush him.

As long as he was the aggressor there was always a chance of getting somewhere. No, he would let the order stand."

Turning to his General, King Modoc remarked, "It seems to me, General, that you assume much more than the situation warrants. While the fulfillment of your instructions is no sinecure, it does not forebode the failure of your mission. Therefore, it is my royal pleasure that you march against the Dovettian Air Base at the king's preserve, and return to us as soon as possible, with the glad tidings of your victory." 

Without another word, General Constant bowed and left the room, followed by the maudlin, high-pitched voices of the banqueters. 

Just as the sun was sinking below the horizon, the General's command, consisting of fifty thousand men, together with an almost endless supply of guns, ammunition and supplies, took up their line of march for the elimination of the Dovettian Air Base, eighty miles away.

Two days after, when only half the distance had been covered, when men, horses and mules were almost completely fagged out at the continual grind from the hard going, it suddenly started to rain.

For two whole days it rained almost incessantly. The storm demoralized the advance. Guns and gun-carriages, trucks laden with food, ammunition and supplies became mired or turned completely over. On the morning of the fifth day out, the rain ceased and the sun shone brightly.

A hurried inspection showed that the storm had turned brooklets into torrents and shallow pools into fair-sized lakes. General Constant also learned that his command was no longer integral; that all of his heavy and most of his anti-aircraft guns, together with the bulk of his ammunition and supplies, wallowed in mud holes, far behind, and were at present unavailable in an emergency.

The impending danger to his army while on the march, which he sensed and dreaded from the minute he received his marching orders, was verified at his first glimpse of

the swarms of aircraft, now rushing forward from the horizon to intercept him.

Caught in an open country, entirely devoid of shelter, without proper anti-aircraft gun support, his army had no more chance to survive against these elusive engines of destruction than frightened fowl in its flight to escape the talons of the hawk. He therefore decided to surrender. 

But even before he could put his decision into effect, five thousand battle planes and bombers swept down upon him. First, came the bombers with their burdens of destruction, and then the battle planes, which, swooping down in squadron formation just above the prostrate infantry, with the rat-a-tat-tat of their machine guns, increased the destruction and carnage done by the bombers. 

Two hundred bombers at once concentrated all their fire on such anti-aircraft guns as could be limbered and trained against the air raiders. In just twenty minutes nothing but craters showed where once the Atlantian guns had stood. The havoc caused by almost five thousand armed aircraft, working together during that rain of fiery death, was stupendous sickening—appalling.

Nothing remained but a tangled wreckage of broken gun carriages, dismantled cannon, flaming trucks, exploding ammunition dumps, several scattered groups of human beings, and a few plunging horses and mules that miraculously still remained on their feet.

CHAPTER XLIII. 

THE first thing John Desmond thought of, on regaining his senses, was that he was in heaven, for there, looking down upon him, with a world of love in her marvelous eyes, was Princess Clarita, the sweetheart whom he believed the bullets of Ismar's machine gun had so brutally murdered. "So this is Heaven, Clarita, dear?" asked John, striving to sit up, "and you are here, too? Thank God for that!" "Hush, dear," admonished the smiling girl, bending down and gently kissing the bandage on his forehead, "I am not yet an angel. This is still the 'Old World' and this particular location is Esmond." "Esmond, Esmond? How did I get here?" "You were brought here by the villagers, after your plane crashed. It caught fire. Luckily it landed within a few hundred feet of a group of men, who rushed to you and saved you and Rocco just in time." "Ah, now I remember! You were fired upon by Ismar, and wounded. You have fully recovered?" asked he, anxiously, sitting up in bed. "No, dear heart, I was not hit." "But-but I saw you sag-collapse-your head droop upon your breast!" "I just pretended, dear, but I am sorry to have caused you alarm by my deception. I thought and executed it, on the spur of the moment. Something told me that if I feigned death, they would cease firing at me, and, as they say in your country, 'the bluff worked'!" said she, smiling into his adoring eyes. "But why did they allow you to jump with a parachute, if-if they intended to kill you? They could have thrown you out." "They did not know I was going to. You see, dear," 239

she continued, putting one of her slim little fingers upon his lips as he essayed again to speak, "on the night they removed me from the castle, I was instructed in the use of a parachute, because of the dangerous proximity of the pursuing planes. "On seeing that you did not fire back, I surmised it was on my account. So I decided to help you out. Unnoticed by them, I strapped one on, sprung the catch on the trap door and fell through!" "Darling, my own darling!" exclaimed John, as he tenderly and reverently kissed the slim, white hand reposing in his, "you are truly a king's daughter-you are as brave as you are adorable." "Oh, but I am not brave, John dear. I was terribly afraid; really I was. It was my first jump.' 

"That makes you braver still, dearest," said he, now kissing her square upon her warm, red lips. "It takes real courage to overcome fear. But how did you know that it was I following you in the prince's plane and wearing his uniform?" "Because Ismar told me that he had recognized you through his glasses, and when I stated he must be mistaken, he let me see for myself. Noticing my pleasure on again seeing you, dear, he said that I might wave my handkerchief at you, because I would never see you again.” "Ah, now I know why he turned so suddenly upon us.

He knew I would never fire upon him for fear of injuring you. He was using you as a catspaw to save himself. But you turned the tables on him, dear, by your unforeseen act. 

And that, I believe, accounts for his wanting to kill you. 

You are the most wonderful girl on earth! 

I love you, adore you, and with the help of God, I shall never cause my dear little wife a moment's sadness." "Are you proposing to me, John?" asked the princess, coyly. "Yes, dear, but I am only repeating to you what my heart has sung a million times. Sweetheart, I have loved you from the very first. I shall always love you. 

If you would make me the happiest of men, consent to become

Mrs.

John Desmond and promise to do so as soon as possible." 

As she made no reply, he asked: "Clarita, dear, I want you more than anything else on earth. If you can't decide-I will wait for you. Just bid me to hope." 'No, John dear, I was thinking which one of us was really the first." 

And as the inference of her answer dawned upon him, John Desmond opened wide his arms, and Clarita Veloris, only daughter of the reigning house of the Kingdom of Dovette-Princess Clarita, chaste, petite and lovable, with a glad little sigh fell into his arms and like a tired child, nestled her glorious head upon his manly breast.

Motionless and in silence, for several minutes they remained clasped in each others' embrace, while they daydreamed for the future. The spell was broken by the entrance of the Doctor. "Well, well, so we are now quite all right again," said he smilingly, at the picture they made. "Yes, Doctor, I feel pretty good," said John with a grin, adding soberly, "but I am anxious to know about my companion, Rocco Mattli. Was he badly hurt? Is he also here?" "Rocco Mattli is here and quite seriously injured," answered the Doctor. "He will recover, but will be lame for life." "Gee, but I am glad to hear that he was not killed. 

Doctor, I owe a lot to Rocco." "Yes, dear, I also owe him considerable," chimed in the princess. "I understand, darling; I understand and will remember," said John, drawing her still closer to him. "Now, what about Ismar? Did he get away, Doctor?" "No, he did not. The High Priest perished with all his men. His plane became unmanageable. All that is left of it is a tangled mass of debris where it hit the earth in its downward plunge. One man jumped clear, but his parachute failed to open." 

---

"In a way," said John, “I suppose I should be sorry, and

I am for the poor dupes that were killed with him, but not for him. He was a devil incarnate, and received his just deserts."

When news of this second catastrophe the defeat and annihilation of General Constant's imposing army, reached the people at home in the Kingdom of Atlanta, whose fathers, brothers, sons and husbands had perished in that legalized massacre, a hue and cry for peace at once was raised. The pressure that was brought to bear on King 

Modoc to end hostilities, was such that he realized his failure to comply would eventually end in his abdication and ruin. So, he at once dispatched envoys to the "Air 

Fleet's Base" in the "King's Preserve" where the "High 

Command" also made their headquarters.

It was then that Uncle Richard demonstrated how the brain of a successful American functions. He told the envoys that, as a preliminary to the establishment of an armistice, King Modoc and the Kingdom of Atlanta must show their good faith by at once releasing and delivering his nephew, unharmed, to him at the Dovettian Army Headquarters. 

No sooner had he spoken these words, than the door opened wide to admit into their astonished presence, the man of the hour-the one of whom they were just speaking and he was not alone. Clinging to his arm, was a petite maiden. 

With a pleased ejaculation, Uncle Richard rushed past the assembled envoys of King Modoc, and took them both in his arms. "Thank God you are both safe!" he said fervently. "How did you get away-how did you find her, John?" "Oh, as to that-that is a long story. I owe my escape to Rocco Mattli." "Come into my den, John, and tell me all about it. I also have great news for you. Gentlemen," said he, turning to King Modoc's plenipotentiaries, "I shall have to ask you to return at ten tomorrow. I am unable to proceed

further today. But, before you go, it gives me great pleasure to present Princess Clarita of Dovette, and my nephew, John Desmond."

Bowing their amazed acknowledgment at the introduction, King Modoc's emissaries departed, much perplexed at the sudden change of the situation.

Once in the spacious, luxuriously furnished room that Uncle Richard called his den, John told his uncle all that had befallen him. The eyes of the Commander-in-chief would light up with pride, and then grow as dark as night, as John described the various events. "And in conclusion," said John to his uncle, "let me present to you the bravest and dearest little girl on earth-my promised bride, Princess Clarita." "Bless you, my children," said Richard Wallace, clasping his nephew's hand and kissing the princess as she entwined her arms about his neck. "I know you will be happy-you were made for each other." "Thanks for your lovely thought and statement, Uncle dear," answered Clarita, with eyes whose moisture would not dim the lovelight shining therein. "You are real kind and-and perfectly adorable. John has been telling me all about you." 

"I am dying for news," said John, addressing his uncle. "What's doing?" "Haven't you any knowledge of current events, at all, John?" "No I do not know a thing. They kept all papers away from me.' 

Uncle Richard thereupon told his nephew about the ultimatum sent to King Modoc, the bombardment and destruction of the city of Helga by the air fleets of Dovette, and the annihilation of the army sent by King Modoc to destroy the air base. "Please, Uncle Richard, can you give me any news of my father and mother? Are they still alive?" pleadingly asked Clarita. "Yes, my dear. They are very much alive, and I expect to see them with you within the next few days."

"Oh, I am so happy! Thanks very, very much," said the princess, rising upon her toes to kiss his furrowed cheek. "You have opened the gates of Heaven for me. Ismar told me I was an orphan." "Ismar was a scoundrel, my dear, one who will never bother you again." Then upon noting the tired look on the little winsome face, he turned to his nephew, with the remark: "John, your little sweetheart has been through a lot of trying situations, and is in need of much rest. Better take her into the next room where she can lie down and take a nap." "I think I shall do what the Commander-in-chief orders, John dear," said Clarita, looking archly at them, “but I can manage alone. You and Uncle Richard must still have much to discuss." Coyly putting her arms about John's neck, Princess Clarita, unmindful of his uncle's presence, kissed him fondly and then smilingly left them.

After she had disappeared into the inner room, Uncle 

Richard turned to John, and said: "John, there goes the dearest and bravest little girl I have ever known. God bless you both. May you always love and cherish each other!" "Thanks, you are the best ever," said his nephew as their hands met. "To that-I concentrate my life. Thanks, also for what you have already done for me-you are wonderful!" "Tush, tush, boy," responded Uncle Richard, "or you will yet turn this old gray head. At my age it would be a real calamity." 

Laughing at the very thought of such a ridiculous situation, he suddenly added quite seriously, "Of course, you know that her parents are still in the power of that weaseled-up old king and his appropriate offspring." "They must be surrendered to us at once, and that is final!" retorted John. "I agree with you, there. Yet, I think it advisable to call in conference the King's Councilors, the men who

were instrumental in making me Commander-in-chief. Right along, they have given me much valuable counsel. Frankly,

I should have been up against it, without them. They should know what we should demand for the privilege of letting King Modoc go back home in peace!" Richard

Wallace was blessed with a broad sense of humor. "Good! the sooner, the better," replied his smiling nephew.

CHAPTER XLIV. 

THE following morning, after an all night conference with twenty of the ablest men of the realm, the Kingdom of Dovette's Peace Terms were drawn up and forwarded to 

King Modoc. They demanded: 

1. The return of their Majesties, the King and Queen of Dovette, unharmed, to the royal palace in Arcadia. 2. 3. 4. 

The withdrawal of King Modoc's army of invasion from the Kingdom of Dovette.

The cancellation of all marriage intentions and agreements relative to Princess Clarita and the 

Crown Prince. 

A seventy-five per cent demobilization of Atlanta's army and air force.
