5. The payment of one hundred million dollars, as a war indemnity. 

6. The surrender of a strip of territory five miles wide, lying between the borders of the now warring kingdoms. 

7. A peace treaty for fifty years.

As the strip mentioned comprised the richest and most fertile part of Atlanta the terms were at once rejected by King Modoc, who, in an uncontrollable rage, threatened the entire destruction of Dovette and all Americans within its borders. Try as they would, his councilors could not move him from his untenable attitude.

Upon the formal notification of Atlanta's rejection of the peace terms, the following notices immediately appeared in the newspapers in an open letter to King Modoc, and to the Kingdom of Atlanta: 246

Be it known, if the conditions already submitted by the 

Kingdom of Dovette, to the Kingdom of Atlanta, for the termination of hostilities, are not accepted within ten days from date, the Kingdom of Dovette hereby notifies the 

Kingdom of Atlanta, that- "On the eleventh day after this publication, without further notice, the air forces of the Kingdom of Dovette will begin to bombard and destroy all fortified cities within the Kingdom of Atlanta."

RICHARD WALLACE, Commander-in-chief,

Dovettian Air Fleets." 

This ultimatum was the last straw that broke the camel's back. Upon reading it, King Modoc's ungovernable fury mounted to such a high degree, that he burst a blood vessel and dropped upon the floor. He was dead when his retainers reached him. 

His son, the Crown Prince, was incompetent, pleasureloving and dissolute. Had he, as the new King of Atlanta, been at all competent, he might at least have insisted, and won some modification of the drastic peace terms. It was, therefore, much to his liking that he gave his royal consent to the formation of a council that would assume the responsibility and deal with the situation. 

Such a council was thereupon quickly formed. Instead of comprising the ablest men of the realm, its personnel was made up mostly from the ranks of the court nobility, and their cohorts. The results were, therefore, inevitable. 

Within a week, the terms offered by Dovette were accepted by Atlanta, without deviation or protest.

CHAPTER XLV. 

FLAGS are flying from housetops, from windows across winding picturesque streets, and from the battlements of the magnificent castle on the crest of the green-canopied hill overlooking Arcadia, the beautiful capital city of Dovette.

The castle's great, massive, shell-battered walls of hewn granite are now almost hidden by the miles of varicolored bunting used in the decorations. 

Through the wide open, massive bronze gates, in the center of the high stone wall, surrounding the kingly abode, green velvet lawns and sunken gardens of gorgeous flowers in full bloom can be seen. Here and there the cascade's lullaby mingling with that of the spouting fountains within the grounds is enhanced and mellowed by the multi-colored winged songsters, that hover over, rest upon or fly past them continually.

The streets are crowded with the joyous streams of the populace, whose smiling faces reflect the cheery warmth of the gay decorations. The alluring and resplendent raiment of the foot and mounted soldiery are now everywhere in evidence.

It requires no mental effort to realize it is a gala day, a holiday out of the ordinary. It is to celebrate the return of King Augustus to the throne of Dovette, and the wedding of his only child and daughter, Princess Clarita.

Beautiful, generous and lovable to the verge of idolatry, she and John Desmond, the American Croesus, to whom she is betrothed, are everywhere acclaimed by the joyous populace. 

The holiday and festival proclaimed by the king in commemoration and thankfulness for their deliverance from the yoke of Atlanta, will, together with the name of John 248

Desmond, live forever in the annals of the Kingdom of Dovette.

Just now, the streets for miles are lined on both sides by a happy, expectant multitude. 

They all are waiting for the appearance of the royal procession, just coming in sight a mile away. First in line is the royal coach, drawn by six jet-black horses in gilded trappings. It contains the smiling King and Queen of 

Dovette. In the next carriage, also royally equipped, behind six prancing, snow-white steeds, sits Princess Clarita, wreathed in smiles and bowing to the cheering subjects of her father, the King. 

The applause is also for John Desmond and Uncle

Richard, who sit on either side of her. The faces of all three are radiant as they bow to the cheering populace.

Approaching the palace, the gorgeous procession of carriages, brass bands and mounted lancers in their gold lace and multi-colored equipment, pass between rows of infantry, at attention, lined up on both sides of the road. 

The marriage of Princess Clarita to John Desmond was consummated one hour afterwards, in the Chapel of the Royal castle. The king gave his daughter away at the altar. After kissing her, he also embraced his son-in-law, John Desmond, and kissed him on both cheeks, to the silent merriment of Uncle Richard.

The Queen mother also smilingly acknowledged John, by opening her arms to him, at the pleased approval of the King and madly applauding spectators. 

Uncle Richard, too, came in for his share of popular adoration and kisses. Had he been inclined, he could then and there have had his pick of the flower of Dovette's womanhood, from old maids and widows of unbelievable ages, down to young misses just past their teens. 

At the close of the marriage ceremony, the bridal procession-the acme of royal splendor-came down the rosestrewn aisle of the Royal Chapel. Upon the snow-white velvet skin at the bride's throat reposed an exquisite pearshaped pearl, suspended from a necklace of scintillating blue-white diamonds.

It was truly worth a king's ransom and was the gift of 

John's invalid mother, Mrs. John Desmond, Sr., who was unable to be present. But the letter that accompanied the gift expressed the anticipated pleasure of soon embracing her new daughter.

Lillian Douglas realized her wish-she was the maid-ofhonor at the wedding of her Fairy Princess. Kissing Princess Clarita right after the ceremony, she naively said: "Princess Clarita, something seemed to whisper to me all the time that you were a real princess, but I did not tell anyone." 

At this, everybody laughed-Lillian, too, upon getting the gist of it, first flushed, and then good-naturedly joined in the merriment. "Never mind, dear," said the princess, putting her arm about the lovable girl, as she again kissed Lillian square on the lips, "just for that, and for other things, you must always call me just-Clarita." Then with a little squeeze, she left the delighted Lillian, amid the good-natured envy of the assembled bridesmaids.

Both John and his uncle were made peers of the realm, by an appreciative king and nation, without their having to give up their American citizenship. 

The death of Ismar, the arch plotter and brains of the

Ismarites, caused the cessation of the Brotherhood's activities and dreams. 

The resignation of Richard Wallace as Commander-in- 

Chief of the Dovettian Air Fleets was rejected. However, by royal decree, he was allowed to retire.

Most of the one hundred million dollars-the war indemnity which Dovette received from Atlanta as cost of the war, was in turn, paid over to John Desmond for the expense he had incurred in the defense and restoration of the Kingdom of Dovette.

Several thousand of the original thirty-five thousand men, whom John and his uncle had brought to Dovette, were, by their own desire, retained in the service by King Augustus. Furthermore, the whole thirty-five thousand received their pro rata share of the ten million dollars John

Desmond awarded them for their co-operation in the acquisition of his great happiness.

In recognition of their valuable services, James Holland and Henry Higginson were promoted to responsible and lucrative positions in the home office of the John Desmond 

Company, Inc.

Rocco Mattli was not forgotten. He received a handsome reward in the form of an annuity, together with the assurance that he had a home and life-long friends in the 

Desmonds and Uncle Richard.

A pleasant surprise awaited John Desmond and his wife, Princess Clarita of Dovette, upon their arrival in New 

York, several weeks after the royal wedding. That lovable little, tormenting piece of humanity, Lillian Douglas, who had preceded them, was now Mrs. Thomas Norton, Jr.

The fairy's wand must have waved over Norbert Miller, also, for while not neglecting his many responsibilities as manager of the ranch he loved so dearly, he, nevertheless, found time to brush his hair, look after his finger nails, wear tailor-made clothes, and to regularly answer the scented letters arriving weekly from New York, and Agnes Grant. 

John Desmond located Esther's daughter. He placed her in a select boarding school and put the 100,000 pounds reward in trust for her.

A cable from abroad, from the Baron and June Ostend, now Baroness Van Guard, tendered their best wishes to 

Mr. and Mrs. John Desmond. "I hope they will be happy," said John to his royal wife, upon receipt of the cablegram. "So do I," was her response. "The Baroness is entitled to her share of happiness. I would feel badly if it should be denied her. There is something I would like to do with your permission, John." "What is it, my dear?" "I would like to have Esther's little girl here, to live with us. I think we owe it to her mother.” "God bless you, my dear!" exclaimed her husband, "you

have a heart of gold." Then taking her face caressingly between his hands, he kissed her rosy-red upturned, responsive lips.

THE END
